
# MSDS 696 Practicum II: LLM Collaboration Log
Craig Rudman<br>
crudman@regis.edu<br>

Each log entry will be drafted by the LLM itself and reviewed before submission.  Each entry follows this convention:

- **Date:**
- **What was going on:**  (one line of context)
- **The exchange:** (paste or link the actual conversation)
- **What I kept, and why:**
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**

## Week 1

To kick off the effort, I wrote instructions to CLAUE.md files at the project level and at the weekly level to inform the LLM agents behavior.  Those files are included in my Week 1 submission.

### Entry 1.1
- **Date:** 2026-07-05
- **What was going on:** Brainstorming and pressure-testing the Week 1 project problem statement (US wildfire prevention using the FPA FOD / "1.88 Million US Wildfires" dataset).
- **The exchange:** Full reconstruction at [W1/llm_brainstorm_transcript.md](W1/llm_brainstorm_transcript.md). In brief: the LLM held my initial framing to the three-part test (question / stakeholder / action), flagged that "local, state, and federal agencies" was too broad, and verified against the USFS source that the dataset has cause, location, time, and size fields but **no** resource/budget/crew data.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The narrowing to a single, concrete stakeholder (a state/regional prevention planner) and a single decision — because the three-part rubric rewards a defensible question over a broad topic.
  - The "inform/target" framing instead of "optimize allocation" — because the data has no resource variable, so claiming to optimize would be an overclaim I couldn't defend.
  - The data verification step before committing scope — I directed the LLM to "check what the data supports," which confirmed the risk-map + cause-profile deliverable was actually feasible.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The LLM's proposed "one state deep + national contrast" scope. I overrode it with my own idea of **three region–cause example pairs**, because pairing region and cause with a few concrete examples proves the "different regions/causes need different strategies" assertion more directly and fits a one-slide talk better.
  - I also pushed to extend the data beyond the 2015 Kaggle snapshot (retrieving later public USFS editions) rather than accept the dataset as fixed — added to the plan as a milestone to strengthen present-day relevance.

### Entry 1.2
- **Date:** 2026-07-05
- **What was going on:** Extending the proposal's method — I wanted to add (1) exploratory work to find additional datasets we might integrate to support the outcomes, and (2) features we might engineer as a result.
- **The exchange:** Full reconstruction at [W1/llm_method_extension_transcript.md](W1/llm_method_extension_transcript.md). In brief: I asked the LLM to look at the proposal and add these two things to the method. It proposed candidate integration datasets keyed to my existing join fields (lat/long, discovery date, county FIPS) — drought/weather indices (PRISM, gridMET, PDSI/SPEI), fuels/vegetation (LANDFIRE, MTBS), wildland-urban-interface / human-exposure (Census WUI), and lightning-strike density — and a set of engineered features split into FPA-FOD-only ones (season, day-of-week/weekend flag, fire duration, log fire size, per-county dominant-cause share) versus integration-dependent ones (drought index at ignition, days-since-rain, WUI/urban-proximity, fuel type). It then asked me how to place the additions and how firmly to commit them.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The two additions as **new milestones plus one method line**, framed as **exploratory/optional** — because I want them visible in the plan without over-promising, and FPA FOD alone still carries the core assertion.
  - The weather/drought and lightning-density candidates in particular, because they speak directly to my lightning-vs-human distinction and could separate the two ignition regimes.
  - The split of engineered features into FPA-FOD-only vs. integration-dependent — honest about what I can do unconditionally versus what depends on integration proving fruitful.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The "committed enhancement" framing — I chose exploratory/optional instead, so a discovery step that doesn't pan out can't jeopardize the Week 1 deliverable.

- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**

## Week 2

### Entry 2.1
- **Date:** 2026-07-07
- **What was going on:** First-look feasibility check on the FPA-FOD SQLite database (`FPA_FOD_20221014.sqlite`, 2.3M rows, 1992-2020) — asking whether the data can actually answer the research question before committing to analysis. Also set up the conda environment for the effort.
- **The exchange:** Working in [notebook/feasibility.ipynb](../notebook/01_feasibility.ipynb). I directed the agent to read the data from `../data`; it inspected the schema and found the `Fires` table with `NWCG_GENERAL_CAUSE`, `STATE`, `FIRE_YEAR`, and `DISCOVERY_DOY` as the columns tied to the cause/region/season question. It initially wrote a full load of all 2.3M rows; I framed the task as a feasibility check instead, and it proposed a random 100k-row sample plus coverage checks. I then had it focus on seasonality: does the cause mix actually shift across (meteorological) seasons? Result: strong signal — Natural swings ~43 pts (1% winter -> 44% summer), Debris/open burning swings ~34 pts the opposite way, so the cause mix inverts between winter and summer. Separately, we authored [environment.yml](../environment.yml) (Python 3.11, pandas/geopandas/sklearn stack).
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The agent's suggested 100k-row sample for the feasibility check — enough to confirm the columns exist, are populated, and vary, without paying to load all 2.3M rows.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's initial full data load. I overrode it because the question at this stage is only "can the data answer the research question?", which a sample settles — a full load was premature.

### Entry 2.2
- **Date:** 2026-07-07
- **What was going on:** Assessing feasibility of the *spatial* half of the research question — is there enough cause variation by region? — in [notebook/feasibility.ipynb](../notebook/01_feasibility.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I raised a hexagonal grid but asked for a better approach if one existed. The agent argued for an administrative unit (STATE) on the grounds that my stakeholder acts within jurisdiction, not within arbitrary hexes. I redirected it to survey what administrative-grouping columns the SQLite data actually offers; it inventoried STATE / COUNTY / FIPS / AGENCY / OWNER and found STATE is the only clean, complete one (county/FIPS ~29% missing, agency/owner lopsided or sparse). I then asked it to web-search for other options given we have lat/lon. The search surfaced **EPA Level III ecoregions** as the unit the peer-reviewed FPA-FOD cause literature uses, because lightning-vs-human ignition tracks ecology/climate rather than state lines. I chose ecoregion. I flagged Alaska; the agent confirmed the CONUS ecoregion shapefile excludes AK (15k fires, 33% Natural) and HI, so we scoped the ecoregion test to CONUS and will handle AK/HI at state grain. I downloaded the `us_eco_l3_state_boundaries` shapefile (ecoregion polygons split by state, so the join returns both grains). The agent wrote a spatial-join cell (reproject points to Albers, point-in-polygon) plus a cause-variation cell. Result: across 78 ecoregions with adequate sample, **5 distinct dominant causes**, with Natural share spanning 0.5% (Interior Plateau, Central California Valley) to 82.6% (Idaho Batholith) — the spatial signal is strong.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - EPA Level III ecoregion as the spatial unit — it matches the published cause-analysis literature and groups by the physical drivers (fuel, terrain, storms) behind the lightning-vs-human distinction my question turns on, which a hex grid or raw state boundary would not explain.
  - The `us_eco_l3_state_boundaries` variant specifically — because it carries a state attribute, so the join yields ecoregion *and* state, letting me report results back to a state-level planner without a second join.
  - The CONUS-scoped ecoregion test with AK/HI handled separately at state grain — honest about coverage rather than silently dropping Alaska's lightning-heavy record.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's initial lean toward STATE as the primary unit. I overrode it by pushing first for a data inventory and then a literature search, which surfaced the better ecoregion option; STATE stays as the reporting grain, not the analytic one.
  - The hexagonal grid I first proposed myself — I dropped it once the ecoregion approach proved both more defensible (literature-backed) and more explanatory for the cause question.

### Entry 2.3
- **Date:** 2026-07-07
- **What was going on:** Shoring up the feasibility work with the evidence and honesty a reviewer would ask for — literature backing for the ecoregion choice, data provenance, and a documented account of how the data was collected and where that collection could bias the cause × region × season findings. All in [notebook/feasibility.ipynb](../notebook/01_feasibility.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** Three pieces. (1) *Literature:* I asked the agent to find and cite the peer-reviewed work supporting EPA Level III ecoregions as the cause-analysis unit. IOPscience blocked automated fetches, so the agent pulled authoritative citation metadata from the Crossref API and produced a References cell — Syphard et al. (2025), *Regional patterns in U.S. wildfire activity: the critical role of ignition sources* (Env. Res. Lett.), and Chen & Jin (2022), *Spatial patterns and drivers for wildfire ignitions in California* — plus the EPA ecoregion data citation. (2) *Provenance:* I told the agent my copy came from the Kaggle "2.3 Million Wildfires" mirror; it authored a two-tier Data Provenance cell recording both the Kaggle mirror actually used and the authoritative USDA Forest Service source (Short 2022, FPA_FOD_20221014, 6th ed., DOI 10.2737/RDS-2013-0009.6). (3) *Bias:* I asked what's known about how the data was collected and whether collection introduced bias. The agent explained FPA-FOD is a harvest-and-reconcile of existing agency reports (not a designed survey) and wrote a Known Biases & Limitations cell documenting five biases — non-random missing cause (~26%), varying non-federal completeness, federal/non-federal geographic skew, detection favoring large/human-proximate fires, and a mid-record cause-standard change — closing with the shares-not-counts reporting stance.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The two peer-reviewed citations and the Crossref-verified metadata — they establish that ecoregion is the unit the published FPA-FOD cause literature uses, not a choice I invented.
  - The two-tier provenance (mirror + authoritative original) — honest about what I actually downloaded while pointing to the citable source of record.
  - The Known Biases cell framed around *shares, not counts* — because reporting cause composition is robust to the reporting biases in a way raw fire counts would not be, which is the honest way to carry the feasibility finding forward.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing this session — it was additive (citations, provenance, bias documentation) with no course correction to record.

### Entry 2.4
- **Date:** 2026-07-07
- **What was going on:** Testing the one bias I can actually check with the data in hand — bias (1), non-random missing cause — to confirm or qualify the feasibility conclusion. The Known Biases cell had promised this diagnostic; this session wrote and ran it. In [notebook/feasibility.ipynb](../notebook/01_feasibility.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I asked the agent to describe the diagnostic before writing it: measure the "Missing/undetermined" rate and check whether it is uniform or concentrated across season, ecoregion, and their interaction — because reporting cause as *shares* is only robust if the dropped records are dropped at roughly the same rate everywhere. It wrote the cell, but its first version reused `joined`, which had been built from `usable` (Missing already removed), so the rate came back 0.0% everywhere. I flagged the output and had it check the implementation; it traced the bug to that pipeline dependency and rewrote the cell to re-join the *full* sample (`fires`, Missing included) to the ecoregions. Corrected result: missing rate is **flat across seasons** (23.9%–26.8%, 2.9-pt spread) but **strongly differential across ecoregions** (2.9%–65.1%, 62-pt spread; interaction cells 2.2%–77.4%). The low-missing regions are the high-Natural federal West (Idaho Batholith 2.9%), the high-missing are the human-ignition Southwest/Plains (Sonoran Basin and Range 65.1%) — bias (3) made quantitative. I then had the result written back into the Known Biases cell as a blockquote next to the claim.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The diagnostic framed around *spread* (season / ecoregion / interaction) rather than a single overall rate — the overall 25% says nothing about whether missingness is concentrated, and concentration is exactly what threatens the regional comparison.
  - The corrected finding as a genuine qualification, not a pass/fail: seasonal signal needs no caveat, spatial Natural-share contrast is real in *direction* but amplified in *magnitude* by differential missingness. This makes the feasibility conclusion stronger (a measured caveat) rather than weaker.
  - Writing the result back into the biases cell so the claim and its verdict live together in the notebook.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's first diagnostic implementation, which measured 0.0% missing everywhere. I didn't accept the clean-looking result — I flagged it and had the code checked, which surfaced the `joined`-from-`usable` dependency bug; the fix (re-join the full sample) produced the real, more useful finding.

### Entry 2.5
- **Date:** 2026-07-11
- **What was going on:** Standing up the literature review in [literature/literature.md](../literature/literature.md) and, in the course of adding a mitigation-treatment source, realizing the project scope as written was narrower than my actual intent. Also established the review's working conventions.
- **The exchange:** I set the review up to *justify method choices*, not prove novelty, and locked in conventions: cite in APA 7th, keep per-source abstracts purely descriptive (no analysis/commentary — moved method-relevance to a later synthesis), rename dropped PDFs to `author-year-shorttitle.pdf` under `literature/pdf/`, and treat PDF ingestion as a student-triggered flow with a human check on citations (I declined an autonomous file-watcher for that reason). I supplied and had the agent summarize three sources — Edgeley et al. (2025) on prevention best practices, Prestemon et al. (2013) on ignition modeling, and Davis et al. (2024), a meta-analysis of thinning/prescribed-fire effects on wildfire *severity*. The agent flagged that Davis sits outside a strict *prevention* framing (it's about mitigation, and about severity not ignition cause). I clarified my real intent: the model supports planners deploying prevention **and** mitigation, over ~30 years of **natural and human** fires, to address wildfire **risk**. The agent surfaced four scope ambiguities this exposed (prevention-only vs. +mitigation; human-only vs. all-cause; "risk" vs. "cause composition" as the unit; "pre-season prevention" phrasing) and put the two cascading ones to me as a decision. I chose prevention+mitigation and all-causes; we updated the root and literature CLAUDE.md and literature.md accordingly, keeping cause-composition-per-region-season as the unit of analysis and "addressing wildfire risk" as its purpose.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The scope expansion to prevention **+** mitigation and **all** causes — because it matches what I actually intend the model to support (a planner with one budget spanning both levers, over a record that includes lightning fires), and it's internally consistent: including natural fires only makes sense if mitigation is in scope, since natural ignitions can't be prevented.
  - Cause-composition-per-region-season as the unit of analysis, with "risk" as the purpose rather than a new modeling target — because my existing method choices and the feasibility work are built on cause composition, and I didn't want "address risk" to silently imply a target I haven't chosen.
  - The descriptive-only abstract rule and the human-checked ingestion flow — honest, low-overclaim documentation, and a citation check I don't want automated away.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's initial read that Davis (mitigation/severity) sat outside scope — I overrode it, because the out-of-scope verdict was an artifact of the *written* scope being too narrow, not of my intent. This is what prompted the scope fix rather than dropping the source.
  - An autonomous, file-drop-triggered ingestion agent — I kept ingestion student-triggered so citation details always get a human check, consistent with the no-fabrication rule.

### Entry 2.6
- **Date:** 2026-07-11
- **What was going on:** Deciding whether fire *size* (acres burned) should factor into the cause composition, since in practice many small brush fires are not as consequential as one large forest fire. Continuing from the scope clarification in Entry 2.5.
- **The exchange:** I raised that severity should factor in. The agent confirmed FPA-FOD's `FIRE_SIZE` supports this without new data, then laid out three ways to do it — a parallel acres-weighted composition alongside the count-based one, replacing count with acres entirely, or adding severity as separate features — and flagged the prevention-vs-mitigation tension (prevention tracks ignition *counts*, mitigation tracks *acres*). It also cautioned that acres burned is fire *size/extent*, an imperfect proxy for ecological *severity* (as Davis et al. measure it), and should be labeled "size-weighted." I chose to report composition **both** ways: by ignition count and weighted by acres. We recorded this in the literature CLAUDE.md scope note.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - Dual reporting (count **and** acres-weighted) — because it directly serves the two-lever scope: the count view informs prevention targeting, the acres view informs mitigation targeting, and reporting both surfaces the count-vs-consequence gap that motivated the question (100 small fires vs. 1 large one).
  - The "size-weighted," not "severity-weighted," labeling — because `FIRE_SIZE` is final extent, not ecological severity, and I want the claim to stay defensible.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Replacing count with acres-weighted composition outright — I kept the count view because prevention acts on ignition frequency, and dropping it would lose the lever that half my stakeholder's budget addresses.

### Entry 2.7
- **Date:** 2026-07-12
- **What was going on:** Drafting the Week 2 status report in [W2/MSDS696_W2_Status_Report.md](W2/MSDS696_W2_Status_Report.md). The agent asked me questions and filled the report from my answers and the feasibility notebook; in the course of reviewing the project summary I sharpened the product framing several times.
- **The exchange:** The agent drafted the progress/issues/next-steps sections from the feasibility notebook's figures (2.3M records; Natural share 1%→44% across seasons; 78 ecoregions, 5 dominant causes; the 26% missing-cause diagnostic). The substantive work was on the summary. I made three framing moves the agent then propagated through the summary, milestones, and issues: (1) the product is not descriptive but an **inferential next-season cause-risk profile** — the expected cause composition for a region and upcoming season, acted on pre-season; (2) that profile is **prioritized for impact by predicted fire size** — and when the agent first wrote this as ranking whole region-seasons, I corrected it to ranking **causes *within* a region-season** by expected burn, since causes are what prevention/mitigation acts on; (3) whether to add **systemic triage across region-seasons** (a higher-level allocation layer) is an **open question**, not a committed feature, so it went to Issues rather than the summary. I also had the agent add the three literature sources from [literature/literature.md](../literature/literature.md) as informing/validating references, and made several purely cosmetic edits (em-dashes→colons in milestones, splitting run-on sentences, "prevention and/or mitigation" wording).
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The inferential next-season-profile framing over the descriptive one — because it names the actual deliverable (a forward-looking, actionable profile) and finally makes the summary consistent with the Modeling milestone, which already said "predict region-season cause composition."
  - The correction to rank **causes within a region-season** by predicted burn impact — because the planner deploys interventions against *causes*, so weighting causes by expected burn (not just ignition count) is the actionable output; ranking whole region-seasons answers a different question.
  - Holding **cross-region-season triage as an open question** in Issues rather than promising it — because whether that allocation layer is needed depends on how the stakeholder actually apportions effort, which I haven't established, and I didn't want the summary to overclaim scope.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's first pass at impact-prioritization, which ranked entire region-seasons against each other. I overrode it to rank causes *within* each region-season — the region-season-level ranking is exactly the thing I then set aside as an open question, not the committed product.

### Entry 2.8
- **Date:** 2026-07-12
- **What was going on:** Preparing the Week 2 practice talk. In building the feasibility beat I noticed the product's impact-weighting step (rank causes by burned area, not ignition count) had been *asserted* in the status report but never actually interrogated against the data — a fit/feasibility gap for the exact thing this week is supposed to test.
- **The exchange:** I asked whether impact-weighting passes the feasibility test. The agent read [notebook/feasibility.ipynb](../notebook/01_feasibility.ipynb), confirmed every prior feasibility check was about cause/region/season and none touched `FIRE_SIZE`, and proposed a diagnostic testing three things: (a) is `FIRE_SIZE` complete, (b) is burn concentrated enough that weighting by size differs from weighting by count, and (c) does the top *ignition* cause differ from the top *burn* cause within region-seasons — because if it never does, impact-weighting is redundant. It wrote and ran the cell (reusing `eco_fires` + `to_season`). Result on the 100k sample: `FIRE_SIZE` is 0.00% null/non-positive; the **top 1% of fires drive 91% of all acres burned**; and in **39% of region-seasons (98 of 249) the ignition-leader is not the burn-leader** (e.g. Western Gulf Coastal Plain summer: Debris ignites most, Natural burns 91% of acres). The agent flagged the honest limit: this proves the impact-weighting *premise* is feasible, not that next-season burn is *predictable* — that stays a modeling-week question.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The three-part diagnostic, especially test (c) — because it's the one that decides whether impact-weighting *earns its place*; a feature that always agrees with the count ranking adds nothing, and 39% disagreement shows it changes the answer materially.
  - The scoped claim: premise validated, prediction deferred — because it's the difference between "the data supports ranking by burn" (true now) and "I can forecast burn-by-cause" (untested), and collapsing the two would overclaim.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — the session filled a gap I had flagged. The correction was to my own prior work: the status report had implied the whole product cleared feasibility when the impact-weighting half hadn't been checked; this entry closes that.

### Entry 2.9
- **Date:** 2026-07-12
- **What was going on:** The feasibility finding in Entry 2.8, plus the earlier scope moves (Entries 2.5–2.7), had pushed the project past its research question as written. I decided the canonical RQ needed to change to match, and the project documents refactored accordingly.
- **The exchange:** I told the agent the RQ should reflect this week's adjustments and asked it to refactor the docs. It grepped every statement of the RQ, then named two drifts: (1) the canonical RQ ([CLAUDE.md](../CLAUDE.md)) said causes "dominate," which reads as *by count*, but the product now ranks by **burned area**; and (2) the framing was descriptive while the deliverable is a forward-looking next-season profile. It offered three scope options; I chose a **two-part RQ** — (1) descriptive: which causes drive the most burned area across region-seasons, and do patterns differ; (2) predictive: can a next-season cause-risk profile be predicted well enough to pre-target prevention. The agent mapped which files were authoritative vs. downstream vs. historical and, on my confirmation, updated CLAUDE.md (the RQ + the "descriptive question, inferential product" approach note) and the W2 status-report summary, while **leaving the W1 proposal and the collaboration log unchanged as historical record**. Separately, it caught that the status report's and talk's close claimed the missing-cause bias was "measured and **bounded**" — an overclaim, since the numerical sensitivity bound is next week's work, not done. I had it corrected everywhere to "measured, so cause is reported as shares and the regional signal flagged as directional."
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The two-part RQ — because it's the honest description of what the project became: a descriptive comparison *and* a predictive product. Folding both into one sentence hid the predictive deliverable; splitting them makes the forward-looking model an explicit research question rather than something buried in the approach notes.
  - "Drive the most burned area" replacing "dominate" — because after Entry 2.8, dominance means burn, not ignition count, and the old wording would misdescribe the finding.
  - Leaving the W1 proposal and the log as historical record — because the RQ legitimately *evolved*; rewriting a submitted deliverable to match the current state would falsify the timeline the log exists to preserve.
  - The "measured, not bounded" correction — because I've measured the missingness and changed how I report because of it, but I have not yet bounded how far it could shift the regional contrast; claiming otherwise would assert an analysis I haven't run.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's default of updating the W1 proposal to match — it asked rather than assuming, and I chose to leave it as the historical record instead.
  - The single-scope RQ options (burn-impact-only, or predictive-only) — I overrode toward the two-part version because either single framing would drop half of what the project actually does.

## Week 3

Week 3 begins the exploratory data analysis in earnest, in [notebook/eda.ipynb](../notebook/02_eda.ipynb). I loaded the full dataset and worked through cause-by-year burned-area trends and a deep-dive on the dominant Natural cause, using the agent to build and iterate on the analysis while I directed which questions the plots should answer.

### Entry 3.1
- **Date:** 2026-07-14
- **What was going on:** Starting the EDA. I had the agent load the entire FPA-FOD `Fires` table (~2.3M rows) into a dataframe, and then asked a specific question: what was the undocumented `Shape` column for?
- **The exchange:** The agent loaded the full table but excluded `Shape` from the pandas read, noting it was a SpatiaLite geometry blob pandas can't parse and that `LATITUDE`/`LONGITUDE` already carry the location. When I asked what `Shape` was for, it checked the data dictionary (no entry — it isn't part of Short's published variable set) and then the SpatiaLite `geometry_columns` registry, which showed `Shape` is a **POINT geometry in SRID 4269 (NAD83)** with an R-tree spatial index (the `idx_Fires_Shape*` tables). It concluded that `Shape` encodes the same point location as LAT/LON but in a GIS-queryable form, and flagged that this — not the nominal `STATE`/`COUNTY`/`FIPS` fields, which the dictionary repeatedly marks "not from a spatial overlay" — is the column the eventual EPA Level III ecoregion join should use.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - Excluding `Shape` from the dataframe — because it's redundant with LAT/LON for the current tabular EDA and pandas can't read it anyway, so nothing is lost now.
  - The CRS finding (NAD83 / EPSG:4269) and the caveat that region assignment should come from the point geometry, not the nominal state/county fields — because it de-risks the ecoregion spatial join that the whole region-season grain depends on, and it confirms the nominal fields could disagree with where a fire actually falls.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — this was a targeted question to understand the data before relying on it, and the answer settled how the future spatial join should be done.

### Entry 3.2
- **Date:** 2026-07-14
- **What was going on:** First substantive EDA question: I wanted to know whether the distribution of fire size by cause holds steady year over year or trends, across 1992–2020.
- **The exchange:** The agent aggregated burned acres per cause per year and, because 13 causes would overplot, kept the top six burned-area drivers and folded the tail into "Other." It produced two views — absolute acres and each cause's share of the year's burned area. Two findings came out: (1) **Natural dominates and is volatile**, swinging from ~1M to ~8M acres with the big years (2004, 2006, 2012, 2015, 2017, 2020) being Natural-driven spikes and running 40–88% of each year's burned area; and (2) a **post-2010 rise in the "Missing/undetermined" cause share** of burned area, from ~10–20% early to 30–40%+ by 2016–2020. The agent labeled the second a **data-quality trend, not a fire trend**, tying it to the project's known differential-missingness caveat and noting it means recent-year cause attribution is getting *worse* — which matters for the predictive product.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The burned-area-weighted view (acres, and share-of-acres) rather than ignition counts — because the project weights cause by burned area, and the share view makes the trend readable independent of how big a given year was overall.
  - The Missing-cause finding as a data-quality signal — because it's a caveat the forward-looking model will have to reckon with (attribution degrading in exactly the recent years a next-season forecast leans on), not something to read as a change in fire behavior.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — the agent's plotting choices (top-N plus "Other," share alongside absolute) matched what I wanted, so the work here was directing the question and reading the result.

### Entry 3.3

- **What was going on:** Following the Entry 3.2 finding that Natural drives the most burned area, I directed a deep-dive into the Natural-cause fires: how their fire size relates to year. This entry is mostly about **iterating on the plot type** until it answered the right question.
- **The exchange:** I first asked for a scatter of burned area vs. year. The agent built one (log y-axis, jittered, low-alpha for 327k points) but I pushed back and asked whether there was a **better plot type**. It laid out three framings; I chose **distribution-shift-per-year**, which led to a per-year boxplot on a log axis, and then — at my request — a **violin plot**. When the agent's note about relabeling the log y-ticks was unclear, I said so, and it explained that the density is estimated on `log10(FIRE_SIZE)` (so the KDE isn't crushed by the skew) with ticks relabeled back to acres. Reading the violins, **I noticed the mass above the median thickening over time**; the agent verified this against the database rather than trusting the eye, finding the share of Natural fires ≥1,000 acres roughly **doubled** (≈1.3% → ≈3.5%) while the share ≥10 and ≥100 acres also grew. It surfaced the honest caveat that yearly fire **counts fall** in recent years, so a shrinking denominator could inflate the large-fire share, and added a companion plot pairing the share-above-threshold lines with a per-year count panel. Two process items also came up: I directed that **I run the notebooks manually** (the agent recorded this and stopped executing them), and I had the notebook **refactored into topic sections** with imports kept near first use and no section numbers.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The move from scatter to distribution-shift views (boxplot, then violin) — because a scatter of 327k points answers "do Natural fires get bigger?" poorly; it fixates the eye on a few extreme points, whereas the distribution views show the median drift and tail stretch, which is the actual question.
  - Verifying the thickening tail against the DB, with the falling-count caveat shown alongside — because "the violin looks fatter up top" is an eyeball claim, and the ≥1,000-acre share doubling makes it concrete while the count panel keeps the reporting-artifact alternative visible.
  - The manual-execution rule and the section refactor — because I want to run and inspect my own analysis, and topic sections make the notebook read as a deliverable.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The scatter plot — I overrode the agent's first rendering by asking for a better plot type, which drove the progression to the violin; the scatter buried the distribution the question is about.
  - The agent's opaque explanation of the log-tick relabeling — I rejected it as unclear and required a plain-language account before accepting the technique, since I won't rely on a chart whose axis I can't explain.

### Entry 3.4

Rather than pick one, I redirected it: **keep in mind the upward trend of the Missing-cause labels.** It took that steer and made the Missing-cause link the center of the reasoning — proposing a specific mechanism (Natural fires increasingly recorded as *Missing* cause after ~2010, the same window the Natural counts fall) and following it through to why it matters: if the unattributed fires are the small ones, the denominator loss is **size-correlated**, which is exactly the direction that would manufacture a false upward tail trend. It wrote the cell as observation + open question (not a conclusion), labeled the link a hypothesis rather than a finding, tied it to the project's existing report-as-shares / treat-magnitudes-as-directional posture, and listed two deferred follow-ups (a reporting-completeness check and a burned-area-weighted version of the trend). It also flagged that the Missing-cause percentages it quoted came from our earlier discussion and I should confirm they match the numbers in the cause-composition section once both are run.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The Missing-cause mechanism as the spine of the cell — because it connects two separate observations (falling Natural counts here, rising Missing share earlier) into one testable explanation, which is more useful than treating the count decline as generic under-reporting.
  - Framing it as an open question with the link labeled a **hypothesis** — because these two plots genuinely can't distinguish real growth from a reporting artifact, and I want the notebook to reason honestly rather than overclaim a fire-behavior trend.
  - The two deferred follow-ups and the tie to the existing missingness caveat — because they give the next analyst a concrete path (completeness check, burned-area weighting) and keep this consistent with how the project already handles the caveat.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
