### Entry 3.5
- **Date:** 2026-07-14
- **What was going on:** Turning from the Natural-cause deep-dive to **human-caused** fires — asking how the share of burned area breaks down across the human ignition causes, and iterating on the chart form until a per-cause trend was readable. In [notebook/eda.ipynb](../notebook/02_eda.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I asked to see the share of acres burned by cause for human fires. The agent surfaced a normalization choice — human causes as a share of *all* acres (~23%, each cause small) vs. renormalized *within* human-caused acres — and I chose within-human, since that's the composition a planner targeting human ignitions acts on; the ~23%-of-all-acres context went into the section's markdown. It built the section reusing the existing palette/top-N/"Other" conventions, dropping `Natural` and the `Missing/undetermined` bucket. The chart form then went through three iterations at my push: **stacked area → stacked bar → small multiples.** I rejected the first two as hard to parse — in any stack only the bottom band sits on a flat baseline, so no single cause's trend reads cleanly — and the agent's small-multiples version (one colored share-line per cause on a shared y-axis) finally made trend-by-cause instantly legible. Reading the panels, **I saw the human mix shifting**: Arson and Debris/open burning trend down, Equipment/vehicle and Power generation trend up. I proposed this might signal **an incursion of more people** (a wildland-urban-interface story). The agent agreed the read was coherent and testable but flagged what the chart can't show — shares are compositional (a down-trend can be an artifact of another rising), and "more people" is an exposure claim the shares don't measure, needing an external population/WUI layer we've deferred. At my direction it wrote the hypothesis and the follow-up questions into a closing markdown cell (counts vs. shares, ecoregion concentration, absolute-acres check, reporting drift), framed as a motivated lead, not a finding.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - Within-human normalization — because it shows the *mix* of human ignitions the prevention lever acts on, and the ~23% total-context in prose keeps it from being misread as human dominance.
  - Small multiples over any stacked form — because the question was "instantly see the trend by cause," and a stack denies every band but one a flat baseline; separate panels on a shared scale answer it directly.
  - The human-incursion idea recorded as a **hypothesis with explicit caveats and a test path**, not a conclusion — because these shares can't separate a real trend from compositional coupling or an exposure change, and the honest move is to write the questions the next analysis has to answer.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The stacked-area and stacked-bar renderings — I overrode both because they buried the per-cause trend I was after; the shared-baseline problem is intrinsic to stacks, so the fix was a different form, not a tweak.

### Entry 3.6
- **Date:** 2026-07-14 to 2026-07-15
- **What was going on:** Understanding the "Missing/undetermined" cause labels — the ~26% of records the earlier work had flagged but never characterized. This ran long and covered several threads: characterizing the missingness, a geospatial detour, extracting reusable code, and a two-grain concentration analysis whose conclusion I made the agent walk back as the evidence came in. In [notebook/eda.ipynb](../notebook/02_eda.ipynb) and [src/cause_density_maps.py](../src/cause_density_maps.py).
- **The exchange:** Five threads. **(1) Characterization:** the agent broke missingness down by reporting agency and state and found it isn't one phenomenon — `ST/C&L` (state/county/local, 1.7M rows) runs ~29% missing (genuine case-by-case undetermined), while `IA` is 99.8% missing and turned out to be almost entirely **Puerto Rico** with `OWNER_DESCR = MISSING/NOT SPECIFIED` — a reporting stream that never carried cause. PR (98.7%) and HI (98.1%) are functionally unattributed, not "differentially missing" — candidates for exclusion, not caveats. The post-2010 rise survives excluding PR/HI, so it's real in the CONUS data. **(2) Geospatial detour:** I asked to geoplot Natural vs Missing point density; the agent hit a slow point-in-polygon join over 2.3M rows, and I had it add a bbox pre-filter plus a parquet cache. I then declined a heavy standalone verification run (agent recorded this), fixed a plot layout bug (floating titles from `set_aspect("equal")`), and chose a shared color scale. **(3) The real question:** I clarified my actual hypothesis — if Missing and Natural share a spatial distribution it might mean Missing absorbs would-be Natural fires — then, noting the *rise over time* suggests a reporting/governance shift, chose to **characterize where/when the rise concentrated** (agency×year, region×year) rather than test spatial similarity. **(4) Code extraction:** rather than split the hexbin map by era, I had the agent **extract the mapping method into `src/cause_density_maps.py`** and remove the experiment from the notebook. **(5) Concentration analysis, state then ecoregion:** the agent built agency×year (rise is uneven: BLM +17, ST/C&L +12, FS +7, BIA flat — not a uniform standard) and a region×year scatter of missing-rate change vs Natural share. At **state** grain I looked at the actual plot and corrected the agent's "flat cloud" read to a **fan** (big swings only in low-Natural states, where absorption *can't* operate) — stronger evidence against absorption. I then asked to redo it at **ecoregion** grain (extending the cache with year+region via spatial join). Reading *that* plot, the state fan **did not survive**: low-Natural volatility compressed, and high-Natural ecoregions all drifted modestly *positive* (Colorado Plateaus +17, Southern Rockies +16, none negative) — the direction absorption predicts. I directed the conclusion to "weakly consistent, not evidence," and finally caught that the reading cells still **referenced the state view we had replaced in place** — which doesn't exist in the notebook — and had every such reference removed so the ecoregion cells stand alone.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The agency-vs-state decomposition of missingness, and the PR/HI-as-unusable finding — because it splits one 26% blob into distinct mechanisms (case-by-case undetermined vs. never-reported streams), which the model has to handle differently, and PR/HI at 98%+ have no cause signal to weight at all.
  - Extracting the hexbin method to a module and removing it from the notebook — because the map became a locator that couldn't settle the similarity question anyway, and I'd rather preserve the method (with its caveats) as callable code than leave a dead experiment in the analysis.
  - The **evidence-driven walk-back**: state-fan "strong evidence against absorption" → ecoregion "weakly consistent, not evidence, neither confirms nor rules out." I kept the finer-grain result even though it *weakened* my own earlier claim, because the ecoregion is the grain the project models on and the honest read is the ambiguous one. The direct follow-up (Natural count vs Missing count within high-Natural western ecoregions) is recorded rather than run.
  - Reading the *shape* of each scatter over its correlation — r ≈ 0.07 barely registers a fan or an asymmetric drift, so the plot itself is the honest artifact, not the coefficient.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's "flat cloud" reading of the state-level scatter — I overrode it from the actual plot; the fan (volatility concentrated where absorption is impossible) carried the opposite of "no signal," and the correlation number had hidden it.
  - Testing spatial similarity as the way to probe absorption — I set it aside once the temporal rise pointed at a reporting shift, redirecting to the where/when concentration analysis, which the data can actually answer without the confounds spatial similarity carries.
  - A heavy standalone verification script that replicated the notebook's expensive pipeline — I declined it (consistent with running notebooks manually); quick metadata checks were fine, but building artifacts the notebook itself produces is my step to run.
  - Stale references to the replaced state-level view — I required them removed, because the ecoregion cells overwrote the state cells in place, so citing "the state fan" points the reader at something the notebook never shows.

### Entry 3.7
- **Date:** 2026-07-15
- **What was going on:** Following the missing-cause work, I directed a look at **missingness versus fire size** — the earlier characterization had noted in passing that missing-cause fires skew smaller (26% of fires but 18.7% of acres) but never examined the shape behind that ratio. In [notebook/eda.ipynb](../notebook/02_eda.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** The agent plotted the missing rate across log-spaced fire-size bins (PR/HI excluded) and found the "missing skews small" headline is misleading about the mechanism: the rate is **not** a smooth small-to-large gradient. It's a bump among the smallest fires (~29% around 0.03–0.1 ac), a **flat ~20–25% band with no trend** across ~four orders of magnitude in the middle, and a clear **drop in the large-fire tail** (16.4% at ≥10,000 ac, 14.8% at ≥100,000 ac) — the biggest, most consequential fires are the *best*-attributed. It spot-checked that the small-fire excess holds within the dominant `ST/C&L` reporting stream (so it's a real size effect, not geography), and reconciled the two headline numbers as two edge effects (small-fire excess + large-fire deficit) on differently-weighted denominators, not a gradient. In verifying the numbers against the full database before writing the prose, the agent caught two data-quality issues and handled them without my prompting: (1) ~3,600 sub-0.01-acre records (mostly the value 0.001, below the FPA-FOD Class A floor of 0.25 ac) that are ~80% missing — degenerate placeholders it pulled out of the curve and reported separately rather than letting them render as an 80% spike; and (2) sparse tail bins (n<100) it suppressed from the rate line but kept in the count panel so the thinness stays visible. I chose the missing-rate-across-size framing and the national-with-confound-noted view when it put those options to me.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The missing-rate-across-size framing over a size-distribution-per-group view — because it answers "does missingness depend on size" directly and reconciles cleanly with the earlier count-vs-acres headline, which is the number this section had to explain.
  - The three-regime reading (small-fire bump / flat middle / better-attributed tail) rather than the flat "missing skews small" summary — because the aggregate ratio hid the mechanism, and the tail result is the one that matters: the largest fires are the *best*-attributed.
  - The project takeaway that this is **reassuring for the burned-area-weighted product** — the missing-cause problem is mildest exactly where the RQ puts the most weight (the tail), and worst in the small-fire class that contributes little burned area — with the honest caveat that a ~15% tail rate is still real and geographically uneven.
  - The agent's handling of the sub-0.01-acre placeholders and sparse tail bins — reporting-and-setting-aside rather than silently dropping or silently plotting them keeps the curve honest about what it does and doesn't cover.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — I directed the question and the framing choices, and the agent's verification surfaced the placeholder and sparse-bin issues before they reached the plot, so there was no bad output to correct this time.

### Entry 3.8
- **Date:** 2026-07-19
- **What was going on:** Deciding what *actions* the missingness analysis (now split into its own [notebook/missingness.ipynb](../notebook/03_missingness.ipynb)) actually licenses — turning the characterization into a concrete data-cleaning step rather than more caveats.
- **The exchange:** I asked the agent to separate findings that force a decision from findings that are just caveats already covered by "report shares, not counts." It flagged two live decisions (PR/HI exclusion; the Natural→Missing absorption question) and two it read as caveat-only (the post-2010 agency-uneven rise; the size finding). On PR/HI I first asked **what `IA` even is**; the agent explained it's the NWCG **Interagency** reporting code and that in this data it's functionally "Puerto Rico's fire stream" (99.8% missing, 21,802 of 21,853 rows are PR), and verified with a query that dropping PR/HI leaves only **51 stray `IA` rows** (0.0022% of the data, ~31% missing) — so the "two distinct mechanisms need different handling" finding **collapses**: exclude PR/HI and everything remaining is ordinary case-by-case undetermined cause. I concluded we could defend dropping **PR, HI, and any remaining `IA`** records. We settled the exclusion rule (`STATE in (PR,HI) OR AGENCY == IA`, 32,223 rows) and, rather than bake it into a shared loader, I **pivoted to a dedicated cleaning notebook** that materializes the drop as a parquet artifact. The agent proposed consuming that artifact from `eda.ipynb`; I **rejected** that — EDA is the *diagnosis* that leads to cleaning, so it must keep reading raw data; the cleaned parquet is consumed *downstream* by the modeling work, not by EDA. The agent built [notebook/cleaning.ipynb](../notebook/04_cleaning.ipynb) (raw → documented exclusion → `data/fires_clean.parquet`), left unexecuted for me to run, and scoped it to the exclusion only with room to add derived keys later. The absorption question I parked until I understand it better.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - Excluding **PR, HI, and remaining `IA`** — because PR/HI carry essentially no cause signal (1.4% attributed across 32,172 rows), and the `IA`/PR verification showed the "differential handling" concern evaporates once they're out, leaving one clean, defensible drop instead of a multi-mechanism special case.
  - The **separate cleaning notebook writing a parquet artifact**, over a shared loader with an exclude flag — because it materializes the decision as an inspectable file, applies the drop once, and keeps the raw-vs-clean distinction explicit rather than hidden behind a default argument.
  - **Exclusion-only scope for now** (derived season/index/ecoregion keys deferred) — because that's exactly what the missingness analysis licensed; folding in modeling-spine decisions here would exceed what the evidence justifies.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The agent's suggestion that **`eda.ipynb` consume the cleaned artifact** — I overrode it: EDA and missingness are the diagnosis that *justifies* cleaning, so they read raw by design; a cleaned input would make EDA assume its own conclusion. The artifact is for the downstream predictive work.
  - Handling the exclusion **as caveats only** (the "keep with caveats" option) — I chose an actual exclusion, because PR/HI at 98%+ missing are unusable, not merely caveat-worthy.
  - Deferred the **absorption question** rather than deciding it — I want to understand the mechanism before committing to run or drop the targeted Natural-vs-Missing-within-western-ecoregions test.

### Entry 3.9
- **Date:** 2026-07-19
- **What was going on:** Pressure-testing my own hypothesis about the missing-cause bucket — that it's just a data-quality artifact (e.g. declining investigation), distributed roughly evenly/randomly across the true causes rather than hiding any one cause preferentially. I asked whether we'd already tested that, and then had three new tests built in [notebook/missingness.ipynb](../notebook/03_missingness.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I asked whether the notebook had already tested whether missingness could be evenly/randomly distributed **across causes**. The agent read all four notebooks and drew the distinction I needed: everything in `missingness.ipynb` tests missingness against *predictors* (agency, geography, year, size) and finds it uneven on all of them, but the question I was asking is about the *target* — is the Missing bucket a random sample of the true cause mix — which is **not directly testable**, because the true cause of a Missing record is unobserved. The closest prior work (the absorption scatter) was the one piece gently cautioning against assuming cause-neutrality. It proposed three indirect probes, and I directed it to build all three **after the PR/HI/IA drop**: (1) compositional stability — do attributed-cause shares stay flat as the yearly missing rate rises; (2) a within-region×season test — within a place-season held fixed, does a cause's attributed share fall as that cell's missing rate rises (the fingerprint of selective hiding), controlling for the confound that some places both miss more and have a different mix; (3) small-vs-large attributed cause mix — since missingness concentrates in small fires, do small and large fires differ in cause mix (which would make "missing skews small" also "missing under-represents whatever small fires tend to be"). Mid-build I told it to **drop the notebook's summary section** — we'll write a fresh one once these are run. The agent surfaced two design choices for my review: Test 2 uses **STATE** (not ecoregion) as the region grain because the cached ecoregion frame doesn't carry `DISCOVERY_DOY` and deriving season from it would mean rebuilding the shared cache; and it left the cells **unexecuted** per my manual-run rule, flagging the sample-size thresholds as untuned guesses. It did not execute the notebook.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The target-vs-predictor reframing — because it names why my hypothesis hadn't actually been tested: every prior section is about what *predicts* missingness, none about whether the bucket draws evenly across the causes it hides, and that distinction is the whole point of the question.
  - All three tests as **indirect probes explicitly labeled unable to prove cause-neutrality** — because the target is unobserved, so the honest framing is "look for the footprints a non-random draw would leave in the attributed record," not "prove randomness"; read together they're stronger than any one.
  - The within-region×season design (Test 2) using **year as the within-cell variation** — because holding region and season fixed is what removes the confound; a raw cross-cell correlation would mix selective hiding with real geographic differences in cause mix.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — the session was additive and matched my direction. Two things I steered rather than corrected: I scoped the tests to run **after** the PR/HI/IA drop, and I had the stale **summary section removed** so it doesn't describe an analysis that's about to change. The STATE-vs-ecoregion grain for Test 2 is left as an open choice I'll revisit if the state-grain result warrants the cache rebuild.

### Entry 3.10
- **Date:** 2026-07-19
- **What was going on:** Running the three missingness probes designed in Entry 3.9 and reading the results — the session that finally settled (or didn't) the Natural→Missing absorption question I'd parked twice. In [notebook/missingness.ipynb](../notebook/03_missingness.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I ran the cells and had the agent write the reading prose for each test. **Test 1 (compositional stability over time):** the agent had built the raw-correlation "trap" into the output deliberately — reporting each cause's raw `corr(share, missing rate)` beside the detrended version — and the trap fired exactly as predicted: Smoking −0.55→+0.18, Railroad −0.70→−0.01, Fireworks +0.67→−0.01, all collapsing once shared calendar drift was removed. **Natural came back −0.02, flat.** The one negative survivor was **Arson at −0.52**, which the agent flagged as mechanistically plausible (arson is the hardest cause to positively establish, so the first to become "undetermined" when attribution effort falls) but explicitly told me not to lean on, since detrending erases *gradual* absorption by construction. **Test 2 (within region×season)** is the arbiter, and it did two things: Natural stayed flat (+0.02) under a design that holds region and season fixed *and* preserves drift, and **Arson reversed to +0.21** — the opposite sign — so the Test-1 crack was compositional residue, not hiding. The agent also explained why most sizeable values came back *positive* (shares are computed within attributed fires, so a shrinking attributed denominator mechanically lifts the survivors) and argued that this makes the absence of any real negative more telling, not less. I then asked for the **acre-weighted** version, since that's what the product actually ranks by; Natural moved +0.02 → **−0.20**, the one number in the hiding direction. Rather than report it at face value the agent wrote a probe cell: the −0.20 is dominated by a single cell, **`AK|Summer` at r=−0.61** carrying ~19% of the acre weight, and **removing Alaska halves it to −0.10**, with the residual a faint tilt (median −0.08, 61% of cells negative). **Test 3 (cause mix by size)** came back positive for a real effect — small fires are 32% Debris / 12% Natural, the ≥10k-acre tail is **78% Natural / 3% Debris** — so the bucket *is* size-selective, but against small human/Debris fires, landing on the count view the project doesn't use. Finally I had the agent write the fresh section summary to replace the one I'd deleted in Entry 3.9.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - The **raw-vs-detrended trap shown side by side** in Test 1's output rather than just reporting the corrected number — because the raw correlations look like strong findings and would have been reported as such by anyone reading only the obvious statistic; showing both makes the methodological point visible instead of silently getting it right.
  - **Test 2 as the arbiter over Test 1**, including accepting that it reversed Arson — because Test 2 controls the confound Test 1 can't (regions/seasons that both miss more *and* have a different mix) and preserves the gradual drift Test 1's detrending erases, so when the two disagree the within-cell design wins.
  - The agent's **hedged verdict — "no supporting evidence found under either probe," not "ruled out."** It proposed the weaker framing itself and I accepted it, because the true cause of a Missing record is unobserved, so these are footprint-hunting probes, not proofs of neutrality; "ruled out" would claim a test I can't run.
  - The **Alaska sensitivity check on the one non-flat number** — the acre-weighted Natural −0.20 was the result most likely to be reported as a finding, and decomposing it into "one edge-case cell plus a faint residual" is what keeps it a precision caveat instead of a bias claim.
  - **Test 3's positive result reported as a positive result**, not smoothed over — the draw genuinely is *not* random by size. What makes it harmless is the direction (it under-samples small human fires, not Natural) and the weighting (it hits counts, not acres), and saying that plainly is stronger than claiming clean randomness.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — the tests were built to my direction in Entry 3.9 and I ran them as designed. The one thing I added mid-session was the **acre-weighted Test 2**, because the count-weighted result doesn't automatically transfer to a product that ranks by burned area, and a clean count answer would have been a false all-clear.

### Entry 3.11
- **Date:** 2026-07-19
- **What was going on:** Executing the Cleaning milestone — extending [notebook/cleaning.ipynb](../notebook/04_cleaning.ipynb) from the exclusion-only scope of Entry 3.8 to the full pipeline (region key, temporal spine, analysis-grain aggregate), and discovering along the way that Alaska had been silently disappearing from the analysis this whole time.
- **The exchange:** I directed the agent to add the derived keys the modeling work needs. Building the ecoregion join, **the agent flagged that Alaska was being dropped, not handled** — the `us_eco_l3_state_boundaries` shapefile is explicitly the *conterminous* US layer, so Alaskan points simply fall outside it and vanish from the join. Entry 2.2 and every plan document since said "AK/HI at state grain," but **nothing had ever implemented that**; the state-grain plan was a description of an intention, not of code. The stake: **20.4% of all burned acres (36.7M of 180M)**, a fifth of the dependent variable, disappearing silently. The agent proposed joining a **second layer** (EPA Level III Ecoregions of Alaska) instead of the planned state-grain fallback, noting its attribute schema matches the CONUS layer field-for-field on the hierarchy columns, so Alaska could enter the same `region` column at the same Level III grain with no special case anywhere downstream. I chose that over state grain. It ran each join in its own layer's CRS with the fire points reprojected to match — refusing to reproject either polygon layer, since forcing Alaska into CONUS Albers distorts badly at those latitudes — and routed fires by `STATE` rather than joining both layers to all points, so no fire could match twice. Result: **99.97% of acres matched** overall (Alaska 99.93%), 105 ecoregions. For the temporal spine it surfaced the **December rule** as a decision rather than an implementation detail: meteorological winter spans Dec–Feb, so December has to be assigned to the winter that *ends* the following year (70,119 fires, 1.45M acres) or every physical winter is split across two calendar years and "predict the next season" becomes incoherent. It asserted `season_idx` contiguity (no gaps, 0–116) and reported the partial boundary season-years (1992, 2021) rather than silently trimming them. Before building the aggregate it ran a **support check** on whether Level III is even viable — 402 region×season series, median 29 of 30 possible years, 86% with ≥20 years covering 99.6% of attributed acres — and carried `na_l2name` alongside so a Level II roll-up would be a `groupby` change rather than a re-join. It also densified the aggregate grid explicitly, on the grounds that a plain `groupby` would leave absent causes as missing rows rather than the real zeros they are, silently distorting every share.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The two-layer Alaska join over the planned state-grain fallback** — because state grain was always a concession to a shapefile limitation, not an analytical choice, and the Alaska layer removes the limitation entirely. Keeping Alaska at Level III means no exception anywhere in the design: one region column, one grain, one modeling path.
  - **Per-layer CRS with points reprojected, never the polygons** — the agent's reasoning (each Albers projection is tuned to its own latitudes) is the kind I can check and defend, and reprojecting Alaska into a CONUS projection would have introduced distortion into the exact geometry the region key depends on.
  - **The December rule made explicit and quantified**, rather than buried in a season mapping — because it's a genuine analytical decision about what "a season" is, it affects 70k fires, and getting it wrong makes the forecast target incoherent rather than merely slightly off.
  - **The support check run before the aggregate, not after modeling failed** — it answers whether Level III can carry a forward-chaining split at all, and the answer (86% of series with ≥20 years, 99.6% of acres) is what lets me keep the finer grain instead of pre-emptively coarsening to Level II.
  - **Densifying the aggregate grid** — an absent cause in a region-season is a real zero, and leaving it as a missing row would make shares sum to something other than 1 without ever raising an error.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The **state-grain plan for AK/HI carried since Entry 2.2** — I overrode my own earlier decision once the agent showed it had never actually been implemented and that a better option existed. HI still drops (per the Entry 3.8 exclusion, on cause-quality grounds), but Alaska has no cause-quality problem, so excluding it on a "no cause signal" rationale that isn't true of it would have been indefensible.
  - Nothing else overridden this session — the substantive work was choosing the Alaska fix and confirming the design decisions the agent surfaced as decisions rather than making them silently.

## Week 4

Week 4 opens the modeling design against the cleaned analysis grain (`data/region_season_cause.parquet`), in [notebook/analysis.ipynb](../notebook/06_analysis.ipynb). The week's move was structural rather than empirical: I refined the model from a flat 12-cause composition into a hierarchical three-branch architecture, then propagated that pivot through the project's agent-facing documentation so it can't be silently contradicted next session. Documented in [coursework/W4/design_refinement.md](W4/design_refinement.md).

### Entry 4.1
- **Date:** 2026-07-21
- **What was going on:** Starting the analysis phase. Before touching a model I asked two framing questions — whether 30 years of history even helps a next-*season* forecast, and how to pin down the "contrasting region-seasons" set — and out of that discussion proposed a three-classifier design. In [notebook/analysis.ipynb](../notebook/06_analysis.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I opened with the naive-but-real question: does 30 years of data help when the target is next season? The agent separated three distinct roles the history plays — estimating the *level* (persistence needs only recent analogous seasons, and long history can *hurt* under non-stationarity), characterizing the *variance* of a heavy-tailed region-season (which genuinely needs many seasons to know if the target is predictable at all), and training a *pooled* model across regions (where the volume pays off). Useful reframe: 30 years may be wrong for the level but right for the error bars and the pooled dynamics. On scoping, I asked it to load the grain rather than pick region-seasons in the abstract; it showed burned area is extremely concentrated (top 20 region-seasons = 63% of acres, almost all JJA). Then I made the design call: **a coarse Human/Natural/Unknown classifier as a first pass, then a separate deep-dive per branch.** The agent checked the cleaning notebook at my request and confirmed the missing-cause mass is *not* dropped — it's held in `missing_acres`, excluded from the `cause_share` denominator but fully recoverable — so the three-way target is buildable without re-running cleaning. I had it add three cells to `analysis.ipynb` (load, coarse mapping, snapshot) and I ran them. Snapshot over the full record: **Natural 58.9% (105.5M ac), Human 22.7% (40.6M), Unknown 18.5% (33.1M), total 179.3M.** I then set the interpretation for each branch: Natural → target mitigation by predicting *location*; Human → deep-dive into *cause* for prevention; Unknown → deep-dive into a *data-quality improvement plan*. The agent's contribution there was pointing out these are three *different* prediction problems at possibly different grains, not one problem at finer resolution, and flagging that the Human 22.7% is plausibly a floor because missingness leans toward the high-Natural West.
  > **Later correction (Entry 4.13):** that last clause is wrong and I've since retired it. `09_unknown_dataquality.ipynb` measured the Natural-share × missing-share correlation directly and found it **negative** (Pearson ≈ −0.64) — Unknown concentrates in low-Natural, human-dominated regions, not the high-Natural West. The floor *conclusion* survives with the mechanism reversed: if unattributed acres are disproportionately human, true Human share is if anything higher than 22.7%. Kept here as written because the log records what was actually said.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The three roles of history, as the frame for the whole predictive question** — because it converts "is 30 years useful?" from a yes/no into three separately-testable claims, and it named the one that actually gates the project (is a region-season predictable at all, or is it tail noise?).
  - **The decision to predict Unknown as a first-class class, not renormalize it away** — this is mine: a regional planner has to deal with the unattributed mass, so hiding it inside resolved shares would misrepresent what the planner faces. The agent laid out both framings; I chose to keep Unknown visible.
  - **The heterogeneous-branch reading** (Natural→location, Human→cause, Unknown→data quality) — I set the three interventions; I kept the agent's point that they're methodologically distinct sub-projects, because it stops me from forcing a cause-composition model onto the Natural branch where it's ~one cause and useless.
  - **`Other causes` → Human**, not its own class or Unknown — it's a resolved determination, just miscellaneous, so it belongs with the resolvable branch.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - The **flat 12-cause composition model** implied by all prior planning — overridden in favor of the hierarchy, because the flat version lets the 18.5% Unknown mass contaminate every other share and applies a cause frame to a branch (Natural) that doesn't have a cause question. The hierarchy quarantines each problem where it lives.
  - I **declined to pre-select a small contrasting region-season set** as a scoping decision — kept it as a presentation choice, and let RQ2's scope be a burned-area floor instead, so the model isn't scored on near-empty cells.

### Entry 4.2
- **Date:** 2026-07-21
- **What was going on:** Immediately after settling the pivot, making sure it actually reaches the agent. A design change that lives only in a new W4 doc gets silently contradicted the next time the agent reads the older planning docs, so I directed a regression pass over all project documentation to propagate it.
- **The exchange:** I asked the agent to run a "regression on all project documentation so agentic behavior is informed by this design pivot." It enumerated every markdown doc, the five `CLAUDE.md` files, and the memory store, then triaged by how strongly each surface steers the agent. It updated the ones that load automatically: **root `CLAUDE.md`** got a new "Architecture (refined W4)" subsection (the Tier-1 allocator, the three branches, the cause→class mapping, the 58.9/22.7/18.5 shares, the Human-floor caveat) and a fix to a stale line still saying "AK/HI at state grain"; the **`predictive-framing-region-season`** memory and its `MEMORY.md` index line were rewritten from flat to hierarchical; **`literature/CLAUDE.md`** gained three new method choices for the review to ground (nested cause modeling, unknown-cause-as-deliverable, Natural-as-spatial-target); and **`todo.md`**'s modeling and cleaning items were corrected. A grep for residual "flat/12-cause" language came back empty — the old framing was never spelled out, which is exactly why it was a silent-contradiction risk rather than a visible one. It deliberately left the frozen W1–W3 specs, past status reports, and transcripts untouched, and left checking off completed todos to me. It also flagged one thing it did *not* fold in: the still-open "higher-level allocation layer that ranks region-seasons against each other" question is genuinely different from Tier-1 (which allocates causes *within* a region-season), so it stays open.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The triage-by-steering-strength approach** — updating the auto-loaded surfaces (root CLAUDE.md, memory, literature CLAUDE.md) first and hardest, because those are what actually shape the next session; a pivot recorded only in a dated W4 file wouldn't change behavior.
  - **Leaving W1–W3 specs, status reports, and transcripts frozen** — they're point-in-time submissions and the historical record; editing them to match a later design would falsify what happened when.
  - **The agent keeping the "allocation layer" question open rather than treating the pivot as answering it** — it's a real distinction (across region-seasons vs. within one), and collapsing them would have quietly dropped an open design question.
  - **The additive edits over rewrites** — the region-season unit, persistence baseline, and ablation ladder are all still true, so layering the hierarchy on top of them (rather than replacing the framing wholesale) keeps the still-valid W2 approach intact.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing overridden — the pass matched my direction. The one thing I withheld was letting the agent draft the collaboration-log entry itself or check off completed todos; those are mine to own.

### Entry 4.3
- **Date:** 2026-07-21
- **What was going on:** Building and scoring **Tier 1** — the coarse Human/Natural/Unknown allocator — in [notebook/analysis.ipynb](../notebook/06_analysis.ipynb). The goal was the persistence floor before any learned model, and the design work of settling what that floor even measures.
- **The exchange:** Before writing code I worked through the design with the agent. I set the target as the **full three-way composition vector** (not the dominant class). The agent introduced the constraint framing — the target is a point on the **2-simplex** (three shares summing to 1), so a model must respect the sum-to-1 rule rather than fit three free regressions; I had it explain the simplex, and separately explain **TVD** (total variation distance — half the summed absolute difference, i.e. the share of the composition sitting in the wrong class), which I hadn't seen before. For the baseline I asked it to argue question 2 — *persistence of what?* It laid out three candidates as competing hypotheses about how compositions behave: **`t-4`** (same season last year — responsive but a single heavy-tailed year is noisy), **trailing mean k=3** (stationary-with-noise), and **expanding mean** (fixed long-run climatology). Its argument I accepted: don't pick one, build all three, because their *ranking* empirically settles the static-vs-dynamic-target question I'd carried open since W2. It built the target (total-acres denominator so shares sum to 1, asserted), three forward-chaining predictors (same-season history only, `t-4` = `season_idx − 4`), and TVD scoring on a held-out tail (season-year ≥ 2010), reported unweighted and acre-weighted. **I ran the cells.** Result on the tail: **`t-4` 0.331, trailing-mean-3 0.278, expanding 0.284** (acre-weighted TVD). I then had the agent write an interpretation grounded in the actual outputs, and revise it for concision.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The full composition vector as the target, not dominant-class** — the deliverable is the composition, and argmax throws away the magnitude a planner needs.
  - **Building all three baselines rather than choosing one up front** — because the comparison *is* a finding, not overhead. It answered the static-vs-dynamic question: the averaging baselines beat `t-4` by ~5 TVD points, so **the composition is static — best estimated by its own history's average, with a single prior year mostly tail noise.** The mechanism (one megafire swings a single year; the mean washes it out) matches the heavy-tailed size distribution found in W3.
  - **The acre-weighted metric as the one that governs** — the product ranks by burned acres, so the acre-weighted column decides, and on it **trailing-mean k=3 (0.278) is the baseline to beat.**
  - **The strategic read the agent drew from the static result:** since there's little season-to-season structure, a learned *temporal* model has little to exploit, so lift must come **cross-sectionally** (region/season features, external covariates). This redirects the ablation ladder.
  - **Reporting `t-4` on fewer cells (3,874 vs 4,024) honestly** — it skips gap-prone series by design, which makes its clear loss more telling, not less.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **A log-ratio metric for scoring** — rejected in favor of TVD, because the data is full of exact zeros (many cells are 0% Human or 0% Unknown) that log-ratios can't handle, and TVD is directly interpretable as "share misplaced."
  - I **declined to lock the trailing mean yet** — the trailing-vs-expanding gap is thin and the two flip between weighted and unweighted, so a `k` sensitivity check comes before treating trailing-3 as final.
  - I asked the agent **not to over-claim the floor as good or bad** — ~28% of the composition misplaced is the honest bar for the ladder; whether it's "good enough" is a planner's question, not one to answer in the abstract.

### Entry 4.4
- **Date:** 2026-07-21
- **What was going on:** Closing the open item from Entry 4.3 — running the `k` sensitivity check before locking the trailing-mean baseline — and, once its result overturned the tentative pick, restructuring the notebook's interpretation prose around what the sweep actually settled. In [notebook/analysis.ipynb](../notebook/06_analysis.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I had the agent add a `k` sweep over `k=1..8` plus the expanding limit, scored the same way as Entry 4.3 (forward-chaining, held-out tail ≥ 2010, both TVD metrics). **I ran it.** The result overturned the tentative trailing-3 pick: acre-weighted TVD falls monotonically as the window widens — k=3 0.278, k=6 0.267, **k=7 0.266**, k=8 0.268 — with k=6–8 essentially tied and all beating k=3 by ~1 point, and the **expanding** mean (0.284) *worse* than k=6–8 because pulling in the oldest early-1990s years adds noise. So I locked the baseline at **k=7**, and the reading of the finding *strengthened*: not only do averages beat `t-4`, averaging over more years keeps helping, which sharpens the static-target conclusion. Then a sequence of prose edits I directed: split the single interpretation cell into two (baseline floor before the sweep, sweep conclusion after it); when the agent titled them "part one / part two" I had it drop the ordinals in favor of self-describing titles, since the two-cell split wasn't foreordained; I had the duplicated markdown tables removed once I noted the numbers already print from the code cells (keeping only the few figures that anchor the argument); and I rewrote the closing "Next" list to be purely forward-looking rather than restating the now-completed sweep. The agent left all cells unexecuted per my manual-run rule and flagged that k=6/7/8 are effectively tied, offering k=6 as the more parsimonious choice at negligible cost.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Locking k=7 over the placeholder trailing-3** — because the sweep is what the Entry 4.3 caveat demanded and the acre-weighted metric (the one the product ranks by) puts the minimum there; treating trailing-3 as final without the sweep would have been the untested assumption I'd explicitly deferred.
  - **The strengthened static read** — the monotone descent past k=3, plus the expanding mean *losing* to k=6–8, is stronger evidence for a stable target than Entry 4.3's three-baseline result alone: more history helps right up to ~7 years, then the oldest years start to hurt. That the unbounded window overshoots is itself a finding.
  - **Splitting the interpretation at the sweep and titling each cell for its own conclusion** — so each half stands on what it establishes rather than on its order, and a later reader isn't told there's a "part two" before the notebook has earned one.
  - **Referencing the printed cell output instead of re-tabulating it in markdown** — the numbers live in the code output; duplicating them in prose invites the two to drift apart, so I kept only the handful of figures that carry the argument.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The tentative trailing-3 baseline from Entry 4.3** — overridden by the sweep's evidence; I'd flagged it as a placeholder precisely so this check could move it, and it did.
  - **The "part one / part two" titling** — I overrode it because the split into two cells was a choice I made this session, not a structure the analysis dictated, so ordinal titles would impose a sequence the reader shouldn't have to assume. Self-describing titles say what each cell concludes.
  - **The redundant markdown tables and the backward-looking "Next" list** — removed both: the tables duplicated printed output (a drift risk), and a next-steps list that recites completed work isn't next steps. I left the k=6-vs-k=7 tie noted but chose the literal argmin (k=7); parsimony (k=6) stays available if a later rung wants it.

### Entry 4.5
- **Date:** 2026-07-21
- **What was going on:** Completing the Tier-1 deliverable's shape by adding the **total-acres level predictor** (so the profile reads as predicted acres-per-class, not just shares), then building two demonstration plots and tidying the notebook's structure. In [notebook/analysis.ipynb](../notebook/06_analysis.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** Given the two forward-looking items from Entry 4.4, I chose to do the **level predictor** first. Before any code I decided the scoring metric: the agent laid out log-space MAE/RMSE, a relative-error (MAPE-like) metric, and raw-acre MAE with a skill ratio; I picked **log-space** because total acres spans 8+ orders of magnitude (0.01 to ~2.9M) and raw error would be swamped by a few megafire cells. I had it predict `log10(total_ac)` with the same forward-chaining trailing-mean family as the shares and **re-sweep `k`** rather than assume the shares' k=7 transfers. **I ran it.** The level's best window came out **k=6** (acre-weighted log-MAE **0.94 ≈ ~9× off**), different from the shares' k=7 — the level's tail behaves differently. Combining shares (k=7) × level (k=6) gave the acres-per-class profile; top-1 class agreement was **72.7%** acre-weighted, and the largest held-out cell (Interior Forested Lowlands, JJA 2015) actually burned 2.8M acres against a ~69k prediction — off ~40×. I then asked directly whether the weak level number is a **fatal design flaw**; the agent argued no — it is the persistence *floor* (headroom for later rungs, not failure), and megafire-blindness is a property of the target, not the architecture. I kept that framing and had the interpretation prose rewritten twice: once grounded in the real run numbers, then again for a **lay reader**. Next I directed two plots. First, Natural-cause acres for contrasting region-seasons on a log axis to show size variability: I cut it from **5 examples to 3**, switched from end-labels to a **legend**, asked for cosmetic **smoothing** (I explicitly chose curved-line interpolation that preserves every real point over a rolling average that would flatten the spikes), and required all three to be the **same season (summer), different regions** — the agent swapped in Yukon Flats (AK) for an Alaskan contrast. Second, a composition-stability plot: I asked for the same treatment on the three-way mix; the agent proposed a stacked area with a paired total-acres panel, I had it switch **area → stacked bars**, and it chose a *different* region (Central Basin and Range) than the acres plot because Interior Forested is ~98% Natural and would read as a cherry-pick. Finally I had the standalone **"Next:" cell relocated to the end** of the notebook and the now-stale duplicate "Next" list (which still listed the just-completed level predictor) **trimmed** from the k-sweep cell. All cells left unexecuted per my manual-run rule; the plot numbers are from the agent's dry-runs, to be confirmed when I run them.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Log-space scoring for the level** — the honest metric for a multiplicatively heavy-tailed target; it reads as "off by a factor of," and it exposed that the acre-weighted error (~9×) is far worse than the unweighted (~4×), localizing the failure to exactly the big-burn cells the product cares about.
  - **Re-sweeping k for the level instead of inheriting k=7** — and the result justified it: k=6, not k=7. That the two targets want different windows is itself a small finding about the level's tail.
  - **The "it's a floor, not a flaw" reading** — the ~9× level error is what persistence *can't* do, which is the case for the cross-sectional rung, and eventually for external weather/fuels data. The sharpened thesis — **cause mix is predictable, season size is not from history alone** — is stronger and more defensible than "we predict next season's fire."
  - **The lay-reader rewrite of the interpretation** — the finding has to land for a fire-agency planner, not just a modeler; translating log-MAE into "typically off by ~9×" and TVD into "share of the mix in the wrong bucket" is the version that communicates.
  - **Cosmetic (curved-line) smoothing that keeps every real value** — the whole point of the variability plot is the spikes; a rolling average would have erased the very thing being shown, so I took the smoothing that changes only the rendering.
  - **Same-season/different-region framing for the acres plot** — mixing seasons muddied a "regions differ" message; holding season fixed makes the contrast purely regional.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Relative-error and raw-acre metrics for the level** — rejected in favor of log-space, for the heavy-tail reason above.
  - **Five examples, end-labels, and the stacked *area* plot** — trimmed to three for legibility, moved to a legend, and switched to stacked **bars** because I found the area fill harder to read.
  - **Data smoothing (a rolling mean)** — overridden: it would flatten the spikes that are the plot's entire message.
  - **Reusing Interior Forested Lowlands for the stability plot** — I accepted the agent's swap to Central Basin and Range, since a ~98%-Natural region would prove stability trivially and look like a cherry-pick; Central Basin has a genuine three-way mix that is *still* relatively stable, which is the non-trivial result.
  - **The duplicate "Next:" list** — trimmed the stale one (it still named the completed level predictor) so the relocated end-of-notebook cell is the single authoritative next-steps list. I left k=6 (level) and k=7 (shares) as independent windows rather than force-aligning them, since they are different targets.

- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** Planning the full Week-4 build before writing any model code — deciding how far to take Tier 1, how to stand up the three Tier-2 branches, and how to reorganize the notebook pipeline. Plan captured before implementation.
- **The exchange:** I asked the agent to plan the week: make progress on Tier 1 and start separate workstreams for the three Tier-2 models. It read the W4 assignment, the design refinement, the existing Tier-1 notebook, and the analysis grain, then ran an Explore pass that surfaced two things I had half-forgotten — Tier 1 is further along than my notes said (TVD is already the locked composition metric; `features.ipynb` already builds the K=7 fingerprint feature table with a leakage audit, it's just not generated or wired into analysis yet), and a notebook renumbering would break references scattered across CLAUDE.md, every notebook's prose, `src/*.py` comments, all three status reports, the design doc, and ~30 links in this log. It then asked me a series of scoping questions and I made the calls: **(1)** separate notebook per Tier-2 branch, and renumber *all* notebooks with `NN_` prefixes for legibility; **(2)** attempt a first working pass on **all four** models (Tier 1 + three branches) this week for an early efficacy read, not just scaffolds; **(3)** fix *every* stale reference from the rename, including the historical W2/W3 docs and the full log — not just the one link that actually breaks; **(4)** on Tier 1, do **both** open items — resolve the partial-winter boundary rule (1992/2021) *and* wire in the learned cross-sectional rung above the persistence floor; **(5)** numbering in **exploration-first** order (feasibility → eda → missingness → cleaning → features → analysis), reflecting that eda/missingness actually run on raw pre-cleaning data; **(6)** the Natural branch gets a **forward-chaining predictive** first pass at Level III *and* I stated the intent to augment it with **drought and fuel-density** data if we can source it — the first time the deferred climate/fuels layer becomes active. The agent wrote the plan to a plan file and I approved it. The renames themselves were already applied (`01_feasibility` … `06_analysis`); reference cleanup and all modeling are still pending.
- **What I kept, and why:**

  - **Doing both Tier-1 items** — the boundary rule is a known correctness gap and the learned rung is the ablation ladder's next real rung; leaving either open would make the "first pass" less honest.
  - **Exploration-first numbering** — eda and missingness genuinely run before cleaning (on raw data, per my cleaning-exclusion rule), so numbering them after cleaning would misrepresent the pipeline to a reader.
  - **Forward-chaining predictive Natural branch + the drought/fuels intent** — the design deferred whether Natural is a real sub-model; I'm choosing to treat it as one now, and this is the natural place to finally test whether external weather/fuels data buys predictive lift.
  - **The iterative cadence with logging as we go** — the collaboration record is more faithful written close to the exchange, and I want to review between chunks rather than approve one large sweep. Saved as a standing preference.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Deferring the renumbering** (an option offered) — rejected; pipeline legibility is worth the edit sweep now, while the branch notebooks are being created anyway, rather than renaming a larger set later.
  - **Fixing only the one live broken link** (the agent's recommended lighter-touch option) — overridden; I want the whole record internally consistent, including the historical status reports, even though those are dated artifacts.
  - **The agent's move to keep building past the rename** — I interrupted it to install the log-as-we-go rhythm and to write this planning entry first, so the record leads the build rather than trailing it.
