### Entry 4.8
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** The Tier-1 iteration from the Week-4 plan: closing the two open Tier-1 items — the partial-winter boundary rule and the learned cross-sectional rung — in [notebook/05_features.ipynb](../notebook/05_features.ipynb) and [notebook/06_analysis.ipynb](../notebook/06_analysis.ipynb). The goal was the first real rung of the ablation ladder above the persistence floor, and an honest efficacy read on it.
- **The exchange:** First the boundary rule. Rather than let the agent guess a rule, I had it inspect the actual spine: meteorological winter (DJF) is labeled by the year it *ends*, so `season_idx = 0` (1992 DJF) is missing its December 1991 and `season_idx = 116` (2021 DJF) is missing its Jan–Feb 2021 — both are half-seasons by construction, and they're exactly the first and last points on the spine. The agent showed `season_idx = 116` sits *inside* the held-out tail (≥ 2010), so leaving it in would have scored the model against a structurally-incomplete winter. I settled the rule as **drop `season_idx ∈ {0, 116}`** — 141 cells, 1.37%, leaving all 28 full DJF winters — applied right after the load in both notebooks so every downstream target, baseline, and score inherits it identically. Then the learned rung. The design already said the target is static, so lift (if any) had to come cross-sectionally; the agent wired `06_analysis` to consume the K=7 fingerprint table from `05_features`, fit gradient-boosted regressors (three per-class for the shares, renormalized onto the simplex; one for the log-level), and score them with the **exact same** acre-weighted TVD and log-MAE on the **same** forward-chaining split, so the head-to-head is apples-to-apples. I caught one correctness risk myself — the agent's first draft aligned the persistence floor to the feature rows *by position*; I had it re-do the alignment by **key** (region, season, season_idx) via a merge, so a floor value can never be paired with the wrong cell. Because my manual-run rule keeps the notebook cells unexecuted, the agent also wrote a standalone validation script against the real data to confirm the logic runs and to get the actual numbers. **Result (from that dry-run, to be confirmed when I run the notebook):** on the acre-weighted metric that governs, the learned rung **does *not* beat the shares floor** (TVD 0.289 vs 0.266 — slightly worse, mild overfit) but **does beat the level floor** — **~6.0× off vs the floor's ~8.6×** on the big-burn cells the product ranks by. I kept that as the headline finding.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Drop-both-partial-winters as the boundary rule** — it's the minimal, defensible fix: it removes the only two structurally-truncated seasons (1.37% of cells) without touching a single full season, and it matters because one of them was silently contaminating the test tail.
  - **Applying the rule at load in *both* notebooks** — so the feature table and the baselines are built on an identical cell set; a mismatch there would quietly bias the head-to-head.
  - **Scoring the learned rung with the floor's own metrics on the same split** — the whole point of an ablation ladder is that each rung is judged on the identical bar; a new metric would make "did it beat the floor?" unanswerable.
  - **The split result as the real finding** — the learned rung helping on *size* and not on *mix* is exactly what the static-mix / unpredictable-size thesis predicts, now confirmed from a second direction (a learned model, not just competing baselines). That the size gain shows up on the **acre-weighted** metric (the big cells) while the unweighted metric is worse is the honest nuance: it improves precisely the cells the product cares about.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Position-based alignment of the floor to the feature rows** — overridden in favor of a key merge; the two frames apply the same filters but I wasn't willing to trust that they emit rows in identical order, and a silent off-by-one in the floor would invalidate the entire comparison.
  - **Reading the shares result as a failure** — I didn't; a learned model *failing* to beat a simple mean on the cause mix is positive evidence that the mix is genuinely static, which is a finding, not a defeat. The size improvement is where the rung earns its place.
  - **Treating the dry-run numbers as final** — they're the agent's validation-script output, not a notebook run; I'm carrying them as provisional until I execute the notebook myself, per my manual-run rule.

### Entry 4.9
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** First of the three Tier-2 branch notebooks (one branch per iteration, my chosen cadence): building [notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb](../notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb), the Human branch — the RQ2 forecast partner that conditions on Tier-1's Human slice and asks *which human sub-cause drives the burn*, the question prevention targeting turns on.
- **The exchange:** I had the agent build the branch in the Tier-1 mould so the three tiers stay methodologically consistent: same grain, same partial-winter boundary rule, same forward-chaining split (score ≥ 2010), persistence-floor-first. The one branch-specific design point I insisted on: the target must be conditioned on Human, i.e. re-normalized over the 11 human sub-causes on a **Human-only denominator** — explicitly *not* the existing `cause_share` column, whose denominator still carries the Natural (lightning) mass and would dilute every human share. It's scored with the same acre-weighted TVD as the Tier-1 shares, over the 11-sub-cause simplex, plus a **top-1 hit-rate** (did the profile name the right #1 human cause?) for planner legibility. I also had the agent add a **global-prior baseline** — predict every cell as the training-years' overall human mix — as the reference the persistence floor has to beat, because that comparison is what actually tests whether human cause is *regionally structured* at all (the branch's whole premise). As with Tier 1, the agent validated the logic against the real data in a standalone script since the notebook stays unexecuted. **Result (dry-run, to confirm on run):** the persistence floor scores acre-weighted TVD **0.489** with a **54%** top-1 hit-rate on the dominant human cause, versus the global prior's TVD **0.643** and only **16%** hit-rate — so region-season identity cuts the error by **0.154 TVD** and more than triples the hit-rate. The agent flagged the honest caveat that the absolute TVD (0.49) is higher than Tier-1's coarse-mix floor (0.27), which is expected: an 11-way simplex is a harder target than a 3-way one.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Human-only denominator, not `cause_share`** — the branch's question is *within* human burn; leaving lightning in the denominator would make every human sub-cause look artificially small in the high-Natural West, exactly the regions the product cares about.
  - **The global-prior baseline as the real test** — the headline finding isn't the floor's absolute TVD, it's that the floor *crushes* a one-size-fits-all mix (0.49 vs 0.64, 54% vs 16%). That's the evidence that human cause is regionally structured enough for prevention targeting to mean something — the opposite of Tier-1's coarse mix, which was already near-static.
  - **Reusing the Tier-1 metrics and split verbatim** — so the three branches are directly comparable and the whole hierarchy reads as one method, not three ad-hoc analyses.
  - **The top-1 hit-rate alongside TVD** — a planner doesn't read a simplex distance; \"we name the right dominant cause 54% of the time\" is the legible version, and it's the number the prevention recommendation rests on.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Borrowing Tier-1's k=7 window without comment** — I let the first pass reuse it for consistency, but had the agent explicitly flag it as *borrowed, not fitted*, with a per-branch k-sweep named as a next rung; assuming the coarse-mix window is optimal for an 11-way human mix would be an untested inheritance.
  - **Any learned model in this first pass** — deferred to a next rung by design; the point this iteration was the floor and the region-structure test, not to chase lift before the floor is understood.
  - **Treating the dry-run numbers as final** — same as the Tier-1 entry, they're validation-script output pending my own notebook run.

### Entry 4.10
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** Second Tier-2 branch: [notebook/07_natural_location.ipynb](../notebook/07_natural_location.ipynb), the Natural branch. Per the design, Natural (59% of all burned area, unpreventable lightning) is a *location/mitigation* question, not a cause-composition one, so this notebook predicts *where* Natural burn lands rather than *what* causes it. I'd also flagged in planning that this is where I want to eventually bring in drought/fuels data.
- **The exchange:** I directed the branch to have two products — a descriptive concentration read (how concentrated Natural burn is across regions, via HHI and top-region share) and a predictive first pass on per-region-season Natural acres in log space, mirroring the Tier-1 level. For the external drought/fuels data I chose **placeholder-only this iteration** (source it next week), so the agent wired a documented stub — candidate sources (US Drought Monitor / PDSI / SPEI; LANDFIRE fuels; nClimGrid anomalies), the ecoregion-season join grain, and an explicit pre-season no-leakage rule per covariate — without fetching anything. The validation is where this one earned its keep. The agent's first framing assumed persistence would beat a dumb global-constant prior, as in the other branches. The dry-run **overturned that**: persistence wins the *unweighted* metric handily (0.91 vs 3.76 log-MAE — it nails the many small cells) but **loses the acre-weighted metric** (14× off vs the global prior's 3.16×). I had the agent dig into why rather than paper over it; a diagnostic on the six largest held-out Natural cells showed persistence under-predicting every megafire by 1–1.7 orders of magnitude (e.g. Interior Forested Lowlands JJA 2015: 2.8M actual acres, persistence guessed ~54k). So a region's own calm-year history is *actively misleading* on exactly the big-burn years that carry the acres, and a high global constant lands closer by accident. I had the notebook rewritten to report both metrics and read the disagreement as the finding, not to assert a winner. The descriptive read held up cleanly: summer Natural burn spreads across many western regions (HHI ~0.14) while winter/spring concentrate in a few (HHI ~0.35–0.38, top region ~50%). Cells left unexecuted; numbers from the validation script.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The metric-disagreement as the headline** — persistence being *worse than a constant* on the acre-weighted metric is a real, defensible result, and it's the Tier-1 'can't see a megafire coming' story in its sharpest form. It makes the external-data stub the point of the branch, not a nice-to-have.
  - **Digging into the megafire cells instead of reporting the summary number** — the six-cell diagnostic is what turns a confusing metric flip into an explanation a planner can follow; without it the acre-weighted loss just looks like a bug.
  - **Placeholder-only external data with a real leakage rule per covariate** — I don't want to burn an iteration on data plumbing before the floor is understood, but I do want the seam specified honestly (pre-season only) so that when I do source it, the no-leakage discipline is already decided.
  - **Keeping Natural as a location question, not forcing a cause deep-dive** — Natural is ~one cause, so a composition model would be the wrong tool; the concentration + per-cell-acres framing matches the mitigation intervention.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent's initial 'persistence beats the prior' framing** — overturned by the dry-run; I made it report the split honestly rather than inherit the pattern from the other branches. Assuming the result would rhyme with Tier 1 was exactly the trap.
  - **Attempting to source drought/fuels this iteration** — deferred deliberately; time-boxing a data hunt risked eating the branch's first pass, and the floor result (history is misleading on big cells) is what justifies the hunt in the first place.
  - **Excluding zero-Natural cells from the log target — kept, not overridden, but noted** — many eastern regions never see lightning fire, so the prediction problem is 'how much given it burns at all'; folding in the structural zeros would be a different (occurrence) question, parked for later.

### Entry 4.11
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** Third and last Tier-2 branch: [notebook/09_unknown_dataquality.ipynb](../notebook/09_unknown_dataquality.ipynb), the Unknown branch — the one whose deliverable is an operational data-quality recommendation, not a fire forecast. It has two jobs: forecast where attribution will be weak (`missing_acre_frac`), and test the project's live caveat that missingness leans to the high-Natural West.
- **The exchange:** I had the agent build the branch to (1) forecast `missing_acre_frac` with the same forward-chaining persistence machinery (it's a bounded fraction, so raw MAE, no log transform) against a global-mean baseline, and (2) test the West caveat directly. Then the validation turned up something that stopped the iteration: the correlation between missing-cause share and Natural share is **negative**, not positive — and at the ecoregion level strongly so (Pearson ≈ −0.64). The highest-missing regions are low-Natural human/agricultural Plains (Central Great Plains, Southern Texas Plains, Flint Hills); the near-zero-missing regions are the high-Natural Alaskan/Arctic ecoregions. That directly **contradicts a claim written into `design_refinement.md`, the W4 status report, and the project `CLAUDE.md`** — that missingness concentrates in the high-Natural West, making Human 22.7% a floor. Rather than let the notebook quietly assert the design's story or quietly overturn it, the agent surfaced the contradiction and asked me how to handle it. I chose: **build the notebook honestly around the real result, flag the doc conflict for a separate follow-up, and don't rewrite the design docs in this iteration.** I then asked directly whether we were re-discovering something `03_missingness.ipynb` had already found — and that provenance check changed the framing materially. `03_missingness` **never actually claimed the 'high-Natural West' concentration**: it characterized missingness by *agency, state, size, and time* (its high-missing states are a mix — NY 54%, CO 47%, KS 41%, AZ 41%, CA 38% — and it explicitly hedged 'differential across ecoregions'; the post-2010 rise sits in the BLM and ST/C&L reporting streams, an agency/practice axis). The 'high-Natural West' phrasing was an **interpretive leap I introduced downstream** in `design_refinement.md`. So `09_unknown` is not re-discovering the missingness pattern — it is the **first direct measurement** of the Natural-share × missing-share relationship, and it corrects that downstream leap, not the original notebook. The Human-floor *conclusion* survives — if unattributed acres are disproportionately human, the true Human share is if anything *higher* than 22.7% — so the floor holds, for a cleaner reason than the one I'd written. Q1 came out modestly positive: persistence MAE 0.166 acre-weighted beats the global mean (0.223 unweighted), so data-quality is region-season-specific and forecastable pre-season. The deliverable is a targeting list of region-seasons where attribution is both weak and material (led by Southwestern Tablelands MAM, Central California Foothills JJA). Cells unexecuted; numbers from the validation script.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Reporting the overturned caveat honestly** — a documented project assumption failing against the data is a finding, and burying it to protect the design narrative would be the worst thing I could do in a record that's itself graded. The negative correlation is strong and consistent (cell and region level, Pearson and Spearman).
  - **Separating the notebook result from the doc edits** — I want the empirical result banked now, but correcting `design_refinement.md` / `CLAUDE.md` / the status report is a deliberate cross-document change I'd rather make as its own step, after checking the original claim's provenance. Rushing the doc surgery into a modeling iteration risks a hasty correction.
  - **Noting that the Human-floor conclusion survives the mechanism being wrong** — it would be easy to over-claim ('the whole floor argument collapses'); the more careful read is that the *direction* still supports a floor, just via human-region under-attribution rather than hidden-Natural-West, and that nuance matters for how the docs get fixed.
  - **Framing the deliverable as a targeting list, not a forecast** — the branch's value to a planner is 'where are your own cause inputs unreliable and material,' which is an operations recommendation; dressing it up as a fire prediction would misrepresent what it does.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent's initial inheritance of the design's 'high-Natural West' framing** — overturned by the data; this is the second branch in a row where I made it *not* assume the result would match the design's prior, and again that was right.
  - **Fixing the design docs in this same iteration** — deferred deliberately. The provenance check (done this session, once I recalled `03_missingness` had already looked at where missingness sits) showed the source notebook never made the 'high-Natural West' claim — I did, downstream — so the doc fix is correcting my own interpretive leap in `design_refinement.md` / `CLAUDE.md` / the status report, and I'd rather make that deliberate cross-document edit as its own step than rush it here.
  - **Adding a learned rung here** — declined; this branch's worth is the targeting list and the caveat correction, not squeezing forecast accuracy past persistence, so a learned model is explicitly low-priority.

### Entry 4.12
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** The graded Defend-Your-Method activity: name my method, get an LLM's case for one named alternative, then write a one-paragraph defense — or switch. Deliverable at [W4/defend_your_method.md](W4/defend_your_method.md).
- **The exchange:** I picked the alternative to defend against myself — the **flat 12-cause classifier**, since my own W4 design refinement had already displaced it, so I'd be defending on ground I'd actually worked. I had the agent state the LLM's *honest* case for the flat model (one model / one metric / one training loop; answers RQ1 directly; no cascade risk from Tier-1 allocation errors; the right MVP under a practicum time budget) rather than a strawman, then write my rebuttal. The defense I kept rests on three data-grounded points: a flat vector has **no honest slot for the 18.5% missing-cause mass** (drop it and silently rescale, or keep a 'missing' pseudo-cause that contaminates every share), whereas Tier-1 predicts that Unknown share directly as a confidence signal; the flat model **wastes the cause axis on the 58.9% Natural mass** where there's ~one cause and the real deliverable is spatial mitigation siting; and the **'cascade risk' objection actually favors me** — Tier-1 is the *stable* 3-class split (72.7% acre-weighted top-1 agreement under persistence), so the hard sub-cause work is isolated in the one branch (Human) that needs it. I conceded the flat model is the right MVP *if* this were pure RQ1 description with clean labels, and noted the ablation ladder is what guards against over-engineering. Every number is pulled from the design docs so it stays consistent with the status report.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Defending rather than switching** — the stronger move here, because the design refinement already did the intellectual work of retiring the flat model; the activity rewards a defensible choice, and this is one I can ground in my own data (58.9 / 22.7 / 18.5, −0.64, 72.7%).
  - **Steelmanning the alternative** — I had the LLM argue *for* the flat model in good faith (simplicity, direct RQ1 answer, MVP discipline). A defense against a strawman is worthless; the concession ('flat is the right MVP for pure RQ1 with clean labels') is what makes the rebuttal credible.
  - **The missing-mass argument as the lead** — the 18.5% Unknown having no honest home in a flat vector is the single most concrete, data-specific reason the hierarchy fits *this* dataset, so it carries the paragraph.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **A generic 'hierarchies are more expressive' defense** — rejected; it would be true of any project and defend nothing specific. I forced every claim to cite a fact about *this* data (the three mass fractions, the −0.64 correlation, the Tier-1 agreement rate).
  - **Overclaiming the alternative's weakness** — I kept the honest concession that the flat model is the correct MVP for a pure-description question, rather than dismissing it outright, because the assignment is graded on the quality of the trade-off reasoning, not on winning.

### Entry 4.13
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** Moving the Human branch ([notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb](../notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb)) along. Its persistence floor + global-prior baseline were already built and run; the notebook named three next rungs. I chose the **learned cross-sectional rung** — the direct parallel to the Tier-1 ablation ladder — to test whether a region's coarse "fingerprint" predicts the within-Human sub-cause mix beyond the cell's own trailing mean.
- **The exchange:** I had the agent mirror the Tier-1 learned rung exactly — one gradient-boosted regressor per sub-cause on the `05_features.ipynb` fingerprint table + season one-hot, same forward-chaining split (train <2010, score ≥2010), predictions renormalized onto the 11-sub-cause simplex, graded with the same acre-weighted TVD and top-1 hit-rate as the floor, key-aligned for a same-cell head-to-head. The agent flagged up front that the fingerprints are *coarse* (Tier-1) trailing summaries, not human-sub-cause history, so the rung tests whether a region's overall character recovers its within-Human mix. A dry-run against the real data (cells left unexecuted per project practice) **overturned the agent's initial hedged framing** ("barely clears the floor"): the learned rung doesn't just fail to add signal, it **loses** — acre-weighted TVD 0.588 vs the floor's 0.489 (delta +0.099, worse), and top-1 hit-rate drops 0.54 → 0.36. The floor TVD reproduced the notebook's already-executed 0.4887 exactly, confirming same-cell alignment. I had the agent rewrite the prose to state the loss as the finding rather than anticipate an ambiguous one, and reorder the remaining rungs so **sub-cause-history fingerprints** (a feature set closer to the target) becomes the priority response to the negative result.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The negative result as the finding** — a coarse fingerprint being *worse* than the trailing mean is a real, defensible outcome and rhymes with the other branches' "the record doesn't carry this" story. It sharpens where lift must come from (target-closer features or upcoming-season conditions), which is the same external-data seam.
  - **Exact parallel to the Tier-1 rung** — same model, split, metrics, and key-aligned head-to-head, so the ablation ladder stays apples-to-apples across tiers and the comparison is honest.
  - **Dry-run validation before leaving cells unexecuted** — reproducing the floor's known TVD (0.4887) is what proves the join is same-cell and the loss isn't an alignment bug.
  - **Reprioritizing sub-cause-history fingerprints** — the coarse rung's loss makes "try features closer to the target" the obvious next move, ahead of the k-sweep and the prevention translation.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent's initial "barely clears the floor" hedge** — overturned by the dry-run; I made it state the loss plainly. Anticipating an ambiguous result would have misread a clean negative.
  - **Fitting this branch's own k first** — deferred; the k=7 was borrowed from Tier-1, but the learned rung's loss is large enough that a window tweak won't flip it, so the target-closer feature set is the higher-value next step.

### Entry 4.14
- **Date:** 2026-07-25
- **What was going on:** Closing the deferred thread from Entry 4.13 — the Human branch's persistence floor uses **k=7**, a window borrowed from the Tier-1 shares rather than fit for this branch. I asked whether sweeping k could sharpen the branch, motivated by the AZ/NM-2011 example where the trailing mean under-called a dominant cause (predicted 45% Recreation vs 93% actual) by diluting it with calmer prior years. In [notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb](../notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb).
- **The exchange:** I first asked whether the earlier interpretation "accounted for the moving average." The agent clarified the persistence floor *is* the moving average — simultaneously the best performer and the source of the smearing — and flagged that k=7 had never been tuned for this branch. I directed a k-sweep. The agent factored the exact floor recipe (`shift(1)` then trailing mean, forward-chaining) into a function of k and swept k ∈ {1,2,3,4,5,7,10,15,20} on the identical held-out cells, same acre-weighted TVD + top-1. I had the agent examine the run itself. Result **overturned my hypothesis**: short windows are *worse*, not better — k=1/k=2 score TVD 0.546/0.512, top-1 37%/41% (one atypical prior year is pure noise). From k=3–10 the curve is flat; best TVD is k=3 (0.483, a 0.006 gain over k=7's 0.489) but its top-1 is a hair *below* k=7's, and best top-1 is k=10 (0.544, +0.3 pt) with slightly worse TVD. Nothing beats k=7 on both metrics, and the largest available gain wouldn't change which cause a planner targets. I had the agent write the reading into the notebook and keep k=7.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Keeping k=7** — it sits in the flat basin, within noise of the best on either metric, so the Tier-1-inherited window is *vindicated* here rather than merely convenient. Reporting the sweep turns a borrowed default into a defended one.
  - **The negative-of-my-own-hypothesis reading** — I predicted a short window would help; the sweep showed it hurts. Keeping that honestly (short windows overcorrect; the AZ/NM smearing isn't fixable by window length because any window short enough to sharpen that one big cell wrecks the many thin cells) is the real finding.
  - **The sweep as a third confirmation of the branch ceiling** — alongside the coarse and history-aware learned rungs, it shows the ceiling is set by the *information in FPA-FOD*, not the smoothing knob, pointing lift back at target-closer features or external upcoming-season conditions.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **My own going-in hypothesis that a shorter window would sharpen results** — I directed the test rather than assume the answer, and overrode the hypothesis when k=1–3 came back worse or flat; asserting the shorter window without checking would have been wrong.
  - **Re-locking the floor at k=3 for its 0.006 TVD edge** — rejected, because it costs a hair of top-1 and the gain is within noise and immaterial to the prevention call; a metric-shopped window change would be false precision.

## Week 5

### Entry 5.1
- **Date:** 2026-07-26
- **What was going on:** Executing the named next-week task from W4: source the drought/fuels data and populate the external-covariate stub in [notebook/07_natural_location.ipynb](../notebook/07_natural_location.ipynb). This is the first real activation of the climate/fuels layer deferred since W2. The justification was already established — on the six largest held-out Natural cells, persistence under-predicts every megafire year by 1–1.7 orders of magnitude, so a big lightning year is a weather event the fire record cannot see coming.
- **The exchange:** I asked where drought/fuel data stood; the agent inventoried the stub and confirmed nothing had been fetched. I said "let's go looking for that data now." The agent researched candidates and came back with a **hard constraint I had not considered**: my grain includes **20 Alaska Level III ecoregions**, and both obvious drought sources — **gridMET PDSI** and **nClimGrid SPEI/PDSI**, the two named in my own stub — are **CONUS-only**. Either would have silently dropped every AK cell. It proposed **TerraClimate** instead (global terrestrial, 1/24°, monthly, 1958–present) and verified the endpoints live rather than trusting docs, confirming OPeNDAP server-side subsetting works so the pull is a bounding box rather than ~15 GB. On fuels it raised a second problem: **LANDFIRE does not fit a 1992–2020 panel** — base map circa 2001, discrete vintages, usable Alaska coverage only from the 2016 Remap — so a `fuel_load` feature would carry almost no *interannual* variance, which is exactly the variance the megafire problem needs. It laid out three options and recommended dropping time-varying fuel load in favor of TerraClimate water-balance variables as fuel *condition*. I chose that (option 1). The agent then wrote [src/terraclimate.py](../src/terraclimate.py) (OPeNDAP fetch, cos-latitude area-weighted zonal means per ecoregion, pre-season lag rule), hit and fixed a GEOS side-location error from an invalid self-intersecting AK polygon by dropping an unnecessary dissolve, verified 105 unique regions matched the analysis grain exactly, and wired a 4-rung ablation ladder into the notebook. Fetch was still running at the time of writing; cells left unexecuted per project practice.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **TerraClimate over gridMET/nClimGrid** — the Alaska constraint is decisive and I would not have caught it from my own stub, which named two CONUS-only sources. This is the agent finding a real defect in my documented plan.
  - **Fuel condition instead of fuel load** — `def`/`soil`/`vpd` describe how dry the fuel *is* going into the season, which is what drives a big lightning year. Fuel load is slow-moving and mostly cross-regional, which ecoregion identity already partly encodes. Rejecting LANDFIRE on panel-fitness grounds is a stronger position than including a near-constant feature and pretending it contributes.
  - **The DJF year-boundary trap being made explicit and unit-tested** — DJF of year Y starts in December of Y−1, so its pre-season window is Sep–Nov of *Y−1*. Getting that wrong leaks a full year of future climate into every winter cell. The agent caught this unprompted and asserted it in `_self_check()`, re-run in the notebook rather than trusted from the module.
  - **cos(latitude) area weighting** — at Alaska's latitudes a naive grid mean over-weights northern cells. Small detail, but it biases exactly the regions the AK coverage was added to serve.
  - **The ablation ladder framing** — climate-only and persistence+climate as separate rungs, judged on the *acre-weighted* metric specifically, because that is where the floor failed. An improvement only in the unweighted metric would mean the covariates sharpened small cells and left the real problem untouched.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **LANDFIRE as a time-varying fuel feature** — rejected on the vintage argument above. Left the door open for it as a *static* per-region descriptor if a cross-sectional rung later wants one.
  - **Hunting an annual NDVI/greenness product** — declined for this iteration: MODIS starts ~2000 and misses a third of my panel, and AVHRR has documented spurious trends in exactly the Alaska tundra I would need it for. Not worth the time-box when fuel condition already covers the mechanism.
  - **Pre-committing to a 3-month lag as correct** — kept 3 months as the default but had it left as a tunable parameter with lag sensitivity written into the next-steps, since antecedent-wetness effects on fuel plausibly operate over longer horizons than drought itself. I don't want an unexamined default hardening into an assumption.

### Entry 5.2
- **Date:** 2026-07-26
- **What was going on:** I suspected the project had gone weedy and asked for an inspection plus an object-oriented refactor proposal, then for a plan to execute it. Scope decision was mine: **everything including `04_cleaning.ipynb`**, verified by **a pytest suite** (the agent had offered narrower/cheaper options). This entry covers Phases 0, A and B of that plan.
- **The exchange:** The agent inspected all 9 notebooks and 4 `src/` modules and quantified the duplication: the forward-chaining trailing-mean idiom written from scratch **~8 times** across 05–09, `PARTIAL_WINTERS` in 5 notebooks, `TEST_START` in 4, TVD in 5 copies with drifting signatures, the `MISSING` literal in 4 notebooks + 2 modules. Three findings changed the plan. **(1)** The agent claimed a positional-merge bug in `06_analysis.ipynb`; on my asking it to verify, it **retracted that** — `floor_df` and `pred_share` both derive from the same sorted frame, so it is fragile-by-construction, not wrong. **(2)** In its place it found a real hazard by *experiment*: the `shift(1)`→`rolling(k)` idiom on an unsorted frame silently attaches one region's history to another's rows — returning `[nan,1.0,1.5,nan,10.0,15.0]` instead of `[nan,10,15,nan,1,1.5]` — with no NaN, no exception, shares still summing to 1 and TVD still computing. Our notebooks are correct only because each sorts immediately beforehand. **(3)** This was **not a git repository**, so the executed notebooks were the only, unversioned copy of the baseline. I also had a Plan subagent critique the design; it argued down two classes from the agent's own first sketch. Phases 0/A/B then landed: baseline capture, `src/config.py`, and a 55-test suite.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Baseline-before-anything (`git init` + commit notebooks *with outputs*)** — given finding (3) this was non-negotiable, and it pays off twice: the stored outputs became the golden record, and `git diff` on an `.ipynb` now shows exactly which printed numbers move after each phase.
  - **Scraping golden metrics from already-executed notebook JSON** — this is the move that breaks the circularity of needing golden values *from* the notebooks being refactored. Zero notebook executions; my later manual re-run only has to *confirm* the pin.
  - **Content fingerprints instead of file hashes** — parquet writes aren't byte-deterministic, so a file hash would false-alarm on an identical rebuild. Row count + schema + `hash_pandas_object` sum + grand totals is the right invariant.
  - **`shares_k` named for its target, not a generic `default_k`** — the subagent's catch, and correct: k=7 is the *result* of 06's shares sweep, the level independently sweeps to k=6, and the Human branch sweeps a different grid. A generic name would have erased the finding that k was tuned per target.
  - **Killing the `Branch` base class and the `Scorer` hierarchy from the agent's original proposal** — the branches don't share a control flow (07's finding *is* the metric disagreement plus six diagnostic cells; 09 does a correlation study that isn't a prediction), so an inherited `ablation_ladder` would need so many hooks it would hide the very sequence the course grades. Protocol + free functions gets ~90% of the dedup with ~40% of the abstraction.
  - **Keeping 04's validation cell in the notebook while *also* porting it to pytest** — deliberate duplication: the PASS/FAIL roster is graded evidence for a human reader, the test file is the agent-runnable gate. They must agree.
  - **Pinning `n_cells` exactly while metrics use `approx` at 4 decimals** — a silently-changed scorable-cell population is the likeliest way a refactor corrupts a result, and it shows up in the count before the metric. It's the canary.
  - **Verification by side-by-side simulation rather than trust** — for each of 05–09 the agent ran the original and refactored logic against the real parquet in one process and compared. All frames bit-identical; every golden value reproduced, including 06's gradient-boosted rungs (floor TVD 0.2676/0.2659, learned 0.3141/0.2894, `best_level` k=6), the full k-sweep, `XCOLS` order, and all `n_cells` (5399/3941, 3850, 2727/2708, 3954/3949).
  - **Mutation-testing the new suite** — a green suite that can't fail is worthless. It detects a single 0.01-acre change, one dropped row, and a tampered per-cell value; and it quantified the dedup-don't-sum trap at exactly 12× (397.5M vs the correct 33.1M), which is now guarded.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent's asserted merge bug** — I made it verify before I accepted a claim that my published numbers were suspect; it retracted. Worth noting as the agent over-claiming on first pass.
  - **The narrower scopes it recommended (05–09, or 06–09 only)** — I chose the widest scope including 04 and the most durable verification, accepting a bigger up-front cost for a project that stays sustainable.
  - **Refactoring 01–03** — left alone. They're dated diagnostic records that earned specific decisions; rewriting them would churn my evidence trail for no analytic gain.
  - **An `if REBUILD:` flag around 04's `to_parquet` calls** — rejected per the agent's own advice; a conditional write reads as hedging in a graded notebook. Backed up the parquets instead.
  - **Committing the generated parquets to git** — rejected; the fingerprints in `tests/` serve the pinning role without vendoring 110 MB of derived data.
- **Flagged, not yet resolved:** `data/region_season_climate.parquet` is absent (only `terraclimate_cache/` exists), so 07's climate section can't currently re-run — the TerraClimate fetch from Entry 5.1 needs finishing before Phase C re-runs that notebook.

### Entry 5.3
- **Date:** 2026-07-26
- **What was going on:** Continuing the OO refactor from Entry 5.2 through its remaining phases — the forward-chaining engine (C), the panel and its four branch targets (D), the scoring/model helpers (E), and the cleaning extraction (F). I also re-ran 05–09 myself to confirm the refactor against the real execution path rather than the agent's simulations.
- **The exchange:** Phase C extracted the trailing idiom that had been retyped ~8 times. The agent added two things the inline version lacked: an assertion that the frame is sorted before any window is computed, and index-aligned rather than positional output. It justified both by demonstrating the failure — on a B-then-A ordered frame the raw idiom returns `[nan,1.0,1.5,nan,10.0,15.0]` where the correct answer is `[nan,10,15,nan,1,1.5]`, with no NaN, no exception, shares still summing to 1. On the real feature table the corrupted and correct versions even share the same mean, so no summary statistic could catch it. Phase D added `RegionSeasonPanel` with four target builders that deliberately return **different** row counts (10,135 / 9,924 / 10,135+6,662 positive / 10,135), since 07 computes its split masks on the Natural-positive subset; unifying them would have changed its numbers. Phase E kept scoring as free functions rather than the class hierarchy from the original sketch. Each phase was verified by side-by-side simulation before I ran anything. **Then I ran 05–09 manually.** The agent re-scraped my fresh outputs and diffed them against the Phase-0 golden record: **209 pinned values, zero mismatches.** Phase F extracted 04's logic and hit the one real surprise — see below.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The sort-invariant guard** — the single most valuable line in the whole refactor. My notebooks were correct only because each happened to sort immediately beforehand; that safety now lives in the code that depends on it rather than in call-site discipline.
  - **`T4Predictor` kept separate from `TrailingMean(k=1)`** — they differ (t-4 additionally requires no year gap, so it scores 3,803 cells vs 3,949), and my 06 narrative turns on exactly that difference. Collapsing them would have erased a finding.
  - **Deliberately unequal target populations in the panel** — with the row counts pinned in tests, so a future "tidy-up" that unifies them fails loudly instead of quietly moving 07's result.
  - **Verification by simulation *then* by my own re-run** — the simulations predicted every number, and my manual run confirmed all 209. Belt and braces, and the second one is the one that counts.
  - **`FINDINGS.md`** — a standing record of things the refactor exposed that are properties of the data or the original code, so a future reader doesn't mistake them for damage.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The `Branch` base class and `Scorer` hierarchy from the agent's own first proposal** — dropped on the subagent's argument and my agreement: the four branches don't share a control flow, and a shared `ablation_ladder` would need so many hooks it would hide the sequence the course grades.
  - **"Fixing" the `f_log_total_std` `min_periods` asymmetry** — I had the agent *preserve* it. It means 06 reports the floor's TVD as 0.2663 in the k-sweep and 0.2659 in the head-to-head (populations differ by 8 cells). Both are correct for what they measure and the comparison is key-aligned, so silently converging them would have moved a published number under cover of a refactor. Recorded in `FINDINGS.md` as a live analysis choice instead.
  - **Pinning the region assignment of boundary fires** — rejected as false precision (see below); the test pins the acreage bound instead.
- **The Phase F finding — and how it was diagnosed:** Re-running 04's ecoregion join produced a **different region for 14 of 2,271,343 fires** than the stored artifact. My instinct was that the refactor had broken something. The agent's first hypothesis (a dedup tie-break on duplicated index labels) was **wrong, and it disproved its own hypothesis** by checking: at full scale the join returns zero multi-polygon matches, so that line never fires. It then ran the *original* `join_ecoregion` function verbatim on the disputed points and found it produces **exactly what the refactored code produces** — both disagreeing with the stored parquet. Root cause: all 14 fires sit within **centimetres** of an ecoregion seam (0.06 m, 0.13 m, 0.31 m), and the CONUS layer ships 1,631 polygons for only 85 distinct Level III names (split by state), so which side a boundary point lands on is decided by the WGS84→Albers reprojection at sub-metre precision — which differs across PROJ/GEOS versions. Scale: 0.0006% of rows, 830 of 179.4M acres (0.000463%), 11 of 10,276 cells. Net effect is a small *improvement*: an 800-acre Alabama fire that previously matched no ecoregion now correctly joins. Excluding those 14, the rebuilt fire-level artifact is **hash-identical across all 43 columns** and the aggregate matches on all 123,036 rows outside the touched cells.
- **What I decided about it:** Accept the change and re-capture the fingerprints deliberately rather than chase byte-equality against a stale PROJ version. I rejected a nearest-polygon fallback — it would add machinery for 0.0006% of rows and would assign a definite region to fires that are genuinely ambiguous. The honest position is that region assignment is **undefined** within centimetres of a seam, and that this cannot affect any acre-weighted conclusion. The test asserts that bound (<1e-5 of burned area) instead of pinning a PROJ release.

### Entry 5.4
- **Date:** 2026-07-28
- **What was going on:** Planning the W5 assignment. My stated goal was to build the case, before and after, for augmenting the fire record with satellite imagery — specifically that the data cannot support (a) highest-risk ignition geocoordinates within a region or (b) acres likely to burn. The plan went through five substantive revisions driven by my pushback; the final version lives at [coursework/W5/w5_plan.md](W5/w5_plan.md) under version control.
- **The exchange:** The agent inventoried the existing evidence and reported an asymmetry I found useful: claim (b) was already proven several times over (persistence 8.6× off, best learned model 5.98×, the 40× miss on AK 2015, climate covariates making it *worse* at 14.0→16.9×), but claim (a) had **no computed number anywhere** — only the dataset's documented ~1 sq mi PLSS inclusion rule. Nothing in the repo had ever computed below Level III grain. Five iterations followed, each from a correction I made:
  1. I raised **the confound the plan was missing**: FPA-FOD gives a pinpoint lat/lon but `FIRE_SIZE` is an *area*. At hex grain, assigning a 2.8M-acre fire's entire acreage to the single hex containing its ignition point measures point-attribution error, not fire behavior. The agent agreed this partly invalidated its Phase 2 and proposed satellite burned-area rasters as the fix.
  2. I told it to **check the original FPA-FOD for dropped shapefile data first**. It queried the SQLite directly and found `Shape` is POINT — nothing had been lost in cleaning — but that the `Fires` table carries **`MTBS_ID`**, a live foreign key to MTBS perimeters covering **13,870 fires = 81.6% of all burned acres** (93.4% among fires ≥1000 ac). The confound was solvable with a join, not a raster pipeline.
  3. I asked for **clarity on how features would account for burn regions**. The agent designed everything on a single primitive (`hex_burn_fraction`: intersect perimeter with hexgrid, distribute acres proportional to intersected area, weights summing to 1 so acres are conserved by construction) and split the targets — ignitions as a point process, burned area as an area process.
  4. I sharpened **the hypothesis**: we inject imagery describing *combustible fuel condition going into the region-season*. The agent had written fuel *consumption* (burn history as a proxy for absent fuel); I wanted fuel *condition* (direct measurement of load and dryness). It restructured the ablation to `persistence → climate → prior-burn → fuel condition`, keeping prior-burn as its own rung so imagery must beat the cheap proxy rather than beat nothing.
  5. I noted a **subset of ecoregions would lower the MODIS bar**. The agent measured the shapefiles and found the single region it had planned on — AK Interior Forested Lowlands, 269,627 km² — was the most expensive pull available, and that three contrasting CONUS regions (Klamath + Idaho Batholith + Blue Mountains) total 179,553 km², ~33% *smaller*, while adding cross-regime generalization evidence.
  Finally I specified the executive framing (we lack the data → we ran a PoC → here is the finding) and required resilient, resumable, memory-safe fetching with a storage check. Phase 0a then executed: MTBS downloaded (372 MB, integrity-verified), join confirmed at **95.44% of MTBS-linked acres**, and **96.0% acre coverage across the three PoC regions in the MODIS era**.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Checking the source before reaching for external data** — this is the entry's main lesson. The agent's first instinct was an external raster pipeline (MCD64A1); my instruction to look in the original database first surfaced a join key that was already there. The 0.6% of fires with perimeters are precisely the megafires carrying 82% of acres, and the 2.29M point-only fires average 14 acres — smaller than a hex, where point attribution is fine. Hybrid target: perimeters where they exist, points elsewhere.
  - **Prior-burn as its own ablation rung** — the agent's suggestion, and correct. Burn history and fuel-condition imagery are partly redundant (a recently burned hex has low NDVI *because* it burned). Separating the rungs is what lets a positive result be attributed to measurement rather than to the cheap proxy.
  - **Three contrasting CONUS regions over one AK region** — cheaper *and* stronger. One region gives one climate regime, so a positive result couldn't be distinguished from a local quirk. I accepted the stated cost: this drops the AK 2015 megafire, but that failure is already documented at ecoregion grain in 06, so the probe needn't re-derive it.
  - **The agent's flag that imagery leakage would be invisible in the metrics** — a vegetation index composited over a window overlapping the fire season reads the *burn scar itself*, producing a spectacular and entirely circular result. Given how much of this project's integrity rests on the pre-season rule, I kept the explicit assertion that every feature's max source date precedes its season start.
  - **Missing imagery must surface as NaN, never imputed to zero** — a zero NDVI anomaly reads as "average fuel," which is a fabricated observation in exactly the rung the hypothesis rests on.
  - **Pre-committing V3's null-result headline** — the finding doesn't exist yet. Writing "fuel condition didn't close the gap at this grain" into the plan *now* is what stops a null being retrofitted into a success later.
  - **Reusing `fetch_monthly()`'s resilience pattern rather than inventing a second one** — per-chunk parquet checkpointing, reload-instead-of-refetch, and atomic `.tmp`→rename writes were already proven in `src/terraclimate.py`.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent's initial single-ecoregion scope (AK Interior)** — I pushed for a subset to lower the MODIS cost and it turned out my instinct was right for a reason neither of us had stated: AK is not just the largest pull but also has genuine high-latitude MODIS quality problems (solar zenith angle, cloud/snow, short compositing windows) that would degrade the exact signal the hypothesis depends on.
  - **The agent's first visual set** — it proposed a concentration curve, a sparsity plot, and a four-rung ablation bar chart. These are analyst encodings; an ablation ladder in ×-off log-MAE units is unreadable to an executive. I specified the three-beat structure and it rebuilt beats 1 and 2 as **maps** (a real perimeter vs. the single dot the record stores), which need no axis at all. The ladder moved to an appendix.
  - **A bar chart for the finding** — rejected on the agent's own reasoning: the ×-off metric is a log-scale error *ratio*, so bar length invites misreading in either direction. That is precisely the axis-honesty failure the W5 Chart Redesign activity is about, so the deck must not commit it. One stat tile instead.
  - **A separate `w5_plan_snapshot.md`** — W4 used one, but the W5 plan is version-controlled, so git history *is* the snapshot and a frozen copy would only drift.
  - **The agent's claim it would "treat the repo copy as canonical"** — I questioned whether it could actually hold to that. It conceded it could not: in a new session it would start from whatever plan file the harness hands it and would have no signal the repo copy exists. I chose to keep both files and accept the drift, knowing I need to point it at the right file explicitly.
- **Corrections worth recording:** I said I had about a TB of storage; the agent measured **213 GB free** (460 GB volume, 52% used) and flagged the gap rather than accepting the premise. It also projected the actual footprint at **under 1 GB** — because the cache stores reduced hex means, not rasters — so the external storage I offered isn't needed, and the binding constraint is network time and API rate limits, not disk.
- **Flagged, not yet resolved:** **Phase 0b is blocked.** No Earthdata or GEE credentials exist on this machine and neither `earthaccess` nor `earthengine-api` is installed; `h3` is also missing and is required for Phase 2 regardless of the imagery route. I'm checking what access I have. Phase 1 (the pinpoint-vs-area diagnostic) needs no imagery and proceeds in the meantime.
- **Unmatched MTBS IDs, characterized rather than ignored:** 1,144 of 13,870 don't join. They split cleanly — **478 are agency-prefixed** (`FS-`, `NP-`, `BL-`), a different identifier scheme that matches 0% by construction, and **666 are geo-format IDs genuinely absent** from the published perimeter set (median 1,037 ac). Among correctly-formatted IDs the join rate is 95.03% of rows and 95.80% of acres.

### Entry 5.5
- **Date:** 2026-08-02
- **What was going on:** Completing the W5 Practice Talk deliverable — present one visual to non-technical executives. I directed the agent to build it on V1-A (`img/w5_v1a_one_fire.png`, the Tepee Springs point-vs-perimeter map) rather than any of the other four W5 figures.
- **The exchange:** I pointed the agent at [W5/assignment.md](W5/assignment.md) and said to use V1a for the practice talk. It read the assignment, the W5 plan, the drafted status report, and the two prior practice-talk artifacts (W2 script, W3 track), then wrote [W5/practice_talk_track.md](W5/practice_talk_track.md) on the W3 Describe/Interpret/Implication structure, with delivery notes, a chart-honesty paragraph, two anticipated pod questions, and the self-assessment the discussion post requires.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The W3 Describe/Interpret/Implication skeleton rather than W2's slide-by-slide shot list** — this is a single-visual talk, not a three-slide deck, so beats about *reading* one figure fit better than beats about advancing through several.
  - **The jargon blocklist** — hex, res-5, H3, MTBS ID, foreign key, Tier 2 are all banned, with plain-language substitutes given. The assignment's audience default is non-technical executives, and my W2 and W3 talks both leaned technical.
  - **"0.6% of fires, 82% of the acres" as the one repeated number** — it is the single line that makes the fix sound cheap instead of heroic, which is what an ask needs.
  - **The chart-honesty paragraph** — Week 5 grades axis honesty, and my figure has no axes, so I wanted the defense stated affirmatively: both shapes are drawn to true geographic scale on one projection, and the 62,494-acre cell figure is spoken aloud so the size comparison is auditable rather than eyeballed.
  - **Naming Tepee Springs as the *median* multi-cell fire in the talk itself** — the honest version of the claim. A cherry-picked megafire would prove less, and saying so out loud pre-empts the obvious challenge.
  - **The self-assessment critiquing my own talk structure** — that the ask lands late and the talk over-explains the mechanism. Writing a real weakness beats writing a flattering one, and it is graded.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Using V1-B (the before/after panels) for the talk** — V1-B is the better *evidence*, but V1-A is the better *hook*: it makes the problem visible with no prior setup, which is what a two-minute cold open needs. V1-B stays in the status report where the reader has the surrounding prose.
  - **Opening with the funding ask**, which the agent's own self-assessment recommends — I left it at the close for the recording. The talk is graded on presenting a visual, not on pitching; leading with the ask would subordinate the figure to the request. Noted as a real trade-off rather than pretending it isn't one.
- **Still outstanding:** recording the talk, posting it with the self-assessment, and replying to podmates on the Pod Feedback Card. The agent wrote the track; none of the submission steps are done.

## Week 6

### Entry 6.1
- **Date:** 2026-08-04
- **What was going on:** Opening the session by asking the agent to examine the project and pick up where W5 left off. I stated my assumption that the next step was sourcing satellite imagery to support spatiotemporal inference.
- **The exchange:** The agent inventoried the repo before accepting my framing and pushed back on two points. First, **the climate layer was not pending — it was already built and run.** `src/terraclimate.py` is a complete module (TerraClimate PDSI/soil/def/vpd via THREDDS OPeNDAP, area-weighted to ecoregions, with the DJF off-by-one leakage trap explicitly unit-checked), and both `data/region_season_climate.parquet` and a 242-file `terraclimate_cache/` were on disk dated 2026-07-26. The W5 status report's "blocked, no Earthdata credentials" line refers to *fuels imagery specifically*, not to climate. Second, that module's docstring **already pre-rejects LANDFIRE** for this panel — circa-2001 base map, discrete vintages, Alaska only from the 2016 Remap — because against a 1992–2020 season-year panel it contributes almost no *interannual* variance, which is the variance the megafire problem needs explained. It also flagged that `region_season_climate.parquet` (Jul 26) predates the W5 hex-grid work (Jul 29), so it sits on the Level III polygon grain, not hexes. The agent laid out three coherent paths — W6 as assigned (narrative/storyboard), Earthdata/GEE credentials for fuels imagery, or the carried-over Human-branch learned rung — and asked me to choose rather than choosing for me.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The correction to my own framing.** I opened the session with a wrong premise. Having the agent check the repo state before acting on it saved me from re-sourcing data I already had.
  - **The observation that W6's assigned focus is narrative, not modeling** — worth having stated explicitly, because it sets the deadline pressure that shaped every later decision this session.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **All three of the paths as offered.** None matched what I actually wanted. I used the agent's inventory as input and then redirected the inquiry myself (Entry 6.2).

### Entry 6.2
- **Date:** 2026-08-04
- **What was going on:** Redefining the prediction product. I told the agent the deliverable I want is "where in my region are fires most likely to start?", which requires hex-level grain.
- **The exchange:** The agent's substantive contribution was pointing out that **W5's central problem does not apply to this target.** W5 was hard because FPA-FOD stores a *point* while `FIRE_SIZE` describes an *area*, so a point cannot carry acres — that mismatch is what forced the whole MTBS perimeter build. But "where do fires start" is a question *about points*: `LATITUDE`/`LONGITUDE` is exactly an ignition location, and `assign_point_acres` already does the `latlng_to_cell` mapping. So the perimeter machinery is not needed for this target and would actively corrupt an ignition count by smearing one ignition across ~26 hexes. The starts target uses the raw point, all 2.27M fires, no MTBS join. The agent also revised its own earlier objection: it had argued finer spatial grain doesn't buy the *interannual* megafire signal, but noted that objection was aimed at the acres target — an ignition-likelihood surface is about *where within a region* conditions favor a start, which is exactly the within-region variation a ~4 km climate grid can express and a 105-region average destroys. It flagged that this is a redefinition of the Tier-2 Natural branch as specified in CLAUDE.md, and asked two design questions (cause scope; the exposure denominator).
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The point-vs-area asymmetry.** This is the load-bearing insight of the session: the defect that made the acres target expensive makes the starts target cheap, because the record stores ignition location exactly right.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Answering the two design questions when asked.** I told the agent to do nothing because I hadn't absorbed the argument yet. It stopped, compressed the argument to its single load-bearing claim, and waited — which is what I wanted. Worth recording that pausing an agent mid-design is a legitimate move and it complied without re-litigating.

### Entry 6.3
- **Date:** 2026-08-04
- **What was going on:** Pushing back on the agent's prevention-vs-mitigation framing, which it had inherited from CLAUDE.md.
- **The exchange:** I argued that **ignition likelihood is a mitigation siting question**: if mitigation were properly sited, small fires wouldn't become big fires and big fires wouldn't become megafires. The agent conceded and named its own error precisely — it had treated prevention and mitigation as partitioning by *cause* (the CLAUDE.md Natural-branch framing: "lightning cannot be prevented, only planned around") when they actually partition by *lever*. Fuel treatment, defensible space and suppression pre-positioning are all *sited* works, and whether they pay off depends on whether fire arrives there. It withdrew its earlier "this is a new Tier-2 product, not the one CLAUDE.md specifies" framing as too strong. It kept one narrower distinction, which I found useful: the two targets can disagree, and the disagreement is informative — ignitions concentrate near roads and settlement, acres concentrate in remote fuel-continuous terrain, so a hex can be high-ignition/low-acre (starts caught small, mitigation arguably already working) or low-ignition/high-acre (rare starts that run, where one ignition is expensive).
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The high-ignition/low-acre vs. low-ignition/high-acre regime distinction** — the agent's addition, and it survives my correction rather than contradicting it. Both regimes are siting-relevant but call for different treatments, which is exactly the "match the intervention to the pattern" outcome the project promises.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The cause-based partition of prevention and mitigation**, which is currently written into CLAUDE.md's Tier-2 Natural branch description. The agent imported it without examining it. **CLAUDE.md needs updating to reflect the lever-based framing** — not yet done.

### Entry 6.4
- **Date:** 2026-08-04
- **What was going on:** Adding prior-burn history as a covariate. I argued that whether a hex burned last season, or within *n* seasons, should carry inferential power — and that this requires burn *perimeters*, not ignition points.
- **The exchange:** The agent agreed and sharpened why: this makes the W5 perimeter work load-bearing for the ignition product rather than a leftover. A point says where a fire started and nothing about which hexes it consumed; two thirds of MTBS-mapped fires cross more than one res-5 hex, so a burn-history feature built from points would mark one hex burned and leave ~25 that actually burned marked unburned — with the error concentrated in exactly the large fires that remove the most fuel. It also noted prior burn is a **state, not a forecast** — known with certainty before the season opens, unlike drought. It proposed measuring coverage before building, which I approved. The measurement returned: 84.9% of hexes ever burned, but only **2.01% of hex-years carry a perimeter burn** (vs 37.4% carrying *any* burn). Median burned fraction when a hex burns at all is only 3.6%. And it surfaced a data problem — a maximum burned fraction of **2.13**, i.e. a hex reporting 213% of its own land area burned.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Measuring coverage before building the feature** — cheap, on data already on disk, and it changed the design. The 2.01% prevalence means this is a strong-signal/low-prevalence feature, which is a normal shape but has to be known in advance.
  - **Excluding point-only fires from the history feature on semantic rather than quality grounds** — the agent's argument, and the sharpest thing in this beat. Point fires average 14 acres against a ~62,494-acre hex (0.02% of the cell), so including them would make the feature encode *where people report small fires*, which is close to the ignition target itself — leaking the target into its own predictor. That gap between 37.4% and 2.01% is almost entirely this.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing in this beat; I approved the measurement as proposed.

### Entry 6.5
- **Date:** 2026-08-04
- **What was going on:** Deciding how to handle the >100% burned-fraction cells before they became a feature.
- **The exchange:** I proposed a 100% ceiling or fence. The agent ran the diagnosis first and **ruled out the benign explanation**: 114 of the 166 overflow cells are *single-fire* cells, so multi-fire overlap and complex double-counting cannot account for them. Root cause is a numerator/denominator footprint mismatch — `distribute_perimeter_acres` intersects perimeters against the **full H3 cell**, while `land_area_acres` measures only the part inside the ecoregion polygon. Every offending hex is a partial one clipped by a region boundary or coastline (land areas ~21k against a grid median of ~61.8k), so a fire covering a full hex that is 35% inside the region reports ~2.9×. It separated two things I had conflated: the **acres** are sound (W5 verified conservation at 99.61% on-grid), only the **ratio** is wrong. Scale: 166 of 21,866 perimeter hex-years (0.759%) holding 7.04% of perimeter acres. It proposed dividing by full H3 cell area and carrying a `hex_completeness` column instead of clamping, but noted that trades one bias for another (full-cell area over-counts denominators for coastal hexes with genuinely less burnable land).
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The diagnosis before the fix.** I asked for a clamp; the agent measured what it would be clamping first. That's the difference between a fence and a cover-up, and it produced the caveat that now has to travel with the feature.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The full-cell-area denominator plus `hex_completeness` column.** I chose the simpler clamp — "keep it simple is going to be good enough" — at 0.759% of cells. **The consequence I accepted, and which is documented in the module:** genuine full-burn cells and clipped partial-hex artifacts both land on exactly 1.0 and are not distinguishable afterward.

### Entry 6.6
- **Date:** 2026-08-04
- **What was going on:** Building the burn-history module and then re-fetching TerraClimate at hex grain. I directed the re-fetch and separately required that retrieval be batched small enough to interrupt, resume, or recover from error.
- **The exchange:** The agent wrote `src/burn_history.py` (4,239,378 hex-season cells; `any_burn` at 1/3/5-year windows, `seasons_since_burn`) and its `_self_check` **caught two real bugs before they reached the panel**: `seasons_since_burn` was reporting every gap one season short (it recorded the index where the *shifted* value appeared rather than where the burn happened), and season 0 emitted NaN where 0.0 is correct — the latter would have silently dropped the first season of all 36,234 hexes from any model that masks incomplete rows. On the climate re-fetch it reported a hard constraint up front: **the existing `terraclimate_cache/` cannot be reused**, because those checkpoints hold values already reduced to region means and a region mean cannot be disaggregated back to the hexes inside it. It then wrote `src/hex_climate.py`, importing `preseason_months`/`season_start` from `terraclimate.py` rather than reimplementing them, so the DJF leakage trap keeps exactly one definition in the project. Per my resumability requirement it made the checkpoint unit one (variable, landmass, year) — 240 units, ~11.5s each — with atomic `.tmp`→rename writes, per-unit failure capture that leaves a hole rather than aborting the run, and the expensive grid→hex index cached separately per landmass. It **verified resume empirically** (0.01s cache hit vs 11.5s cold, no orphaned `.tmp` files) before starting the long run. Mid-run I asked for regular progress updates and it put the job in the background with a tailable log.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The self-check as a precondition for building the panel, not a formality.** It caught two silent bugs. Both would have produced plausible-looking numbers.
  - **Importing the leakage rule rather than copying it** — consistent with why `src/config.py` exists. A second copy of the DJF rule is the single easiest way to leak 12 months of future climate into every winter cell.
  - **Empirical verification of resume before committing to the long run.** I asked for resumability; the agent proved it worked rather than asserting it.
  - **The grid→hex index cached separately from the variable-year checkpoints** — it's the expensive one-time computation, more than any single year's download, so an interrupted run must never rebuild it.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Testing burn-history against ignitions first.** The agent recommended this sequencing — it costs nothing, and a positive result would justify the fetch while a null would save the download time. I directed the re-fetch instead. My reasoning is that I want both layers in hand for the W6 storyboard, and the fetch runs unattended while other work proceeds.
- **Corrections worth recording:** The agent initially reported the background job as "completed, exit code 0" on a harness notification; it checked and **corrected itself in the same turn** — the notification was the launcher shell exiting, not the fetch, which was alive and writing checkpoints. It also revised its own cost estimate downward with evidence: the smoke test measured 11.5s/unit against its quoted 1.5–2 hours, projecting ~45 minutes. And the CONUS index resolved 471,511 grid cells across 29,883 hexes — **~15.8 TerraClimate cells per hex, not the 4–6 it had estimated** — which strengthens the case that hex grain will behave differently from the W4 pooled null.
- **Still outstanding:** the fetch is running (26/240 units at time of writing); the burn-history-vs-ignition test is not yet run; CLAUDE.md still carries the cause-based prevention/mitigation framing corrected in Entry 6.3.
