### Entry 6.9
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** Returning to the modeling work with both covariate layers built, I asked what steps were needed to put the data to use. This settled the two ignition-target design questions left open since Entry 6.2.
- **The exchange:** The agent's first substantive point was that **the target did not exist** — I had two feature layers and nothing to predict. It laid out five steps (build the target, assemble the panel, persistence baseline, ablation ladder, score) and flagged that step 1 was blocked on the two open design questions. It recommended **Natural-only** scope with a log-area offset, arguing that Natural is where the climate covariates have measured signal and that it makes the cleanest storyboard claim. I chose **Human and Natural modeled separately** instead. It then wrote `src/hex_ignitions.py` — raw ignition points, no MTBS join, all 2.27M fires, densified across the full 36,234-hex grid so that "no fire started here" is a zero rather than an absent row. Result: 4,166,910 hex-season cells, 98.86% of fires on-grid.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The log-area exposure offset**, as recommended. A raw count target would substantially rediscover which hexes are large and populated rather than where conditions favor ignition, and the offset also handles the partial hexes clipped by region boundaries found in Entry 6.5.
  - **Densifying the panel.** The zeros are the data. Building the target from the fire table alone would have silently conditioned the whole model on already-burning cells — a mistake that would not have announced itself in any metric.
  - **The agent surfacing that the target didn't exist before proposing anything else.** I had been thinking in terms of covariates for two sessions and had lost track of the fact that nothing was being predicted yet.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Natural-only scope**, the agent's recommendation. Modeling Human and Natural separately doubles the build but tests the agent's own Entry 6.2 reasoning — that human ignition is static geography and natural ignition is weather-driven — rather than assuming it. That turned out to be the right call: the Human branch produced the strongest result in the session (Entry 6.10) and would have been invisible under the recommended scope.

### Entry 6.10
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** Running the ablation ladder, and then asking the question that had never been asked anywhere in this project: has anything we have built been measured against a **naive random baseline**?
- **The exchange:** The agent's ablation ladder produced results, and then produced **different results**, three times. The same burn-history rung scored −30%, −115% and +3.4%; the same climate rung scored +3.6%, −190% and −82%. It found one real bug mid-sequence — `log_area` passed as an ordinary tree feature rather than as a Poisson offset, which let the model treat hex size as a free parameter and inflate every prediction by ~68% — but the numbers still would not stabilise. To its credit it stopped and said plainly that its harness was not trustworthy and that I should not act on any of its ablation numbers. Separately I asked whether Poisson was chosen because fire sizes are heavy-tailed; it said no, it had chosen Poisson reflexively because the target is a count, and had **never checked the dispersion**. Measured: variance/mean is **4.3× (Natural) and 9.3× (Human)**, and Poisson predicts 65.7% zeros on Human where the data has 85.3%. Then I asked for the random baseline. That single comparison produced the most valuable result of the session.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The shuffled-persistence null.** This is the agent's construction and it is the right one: it holds the exact set of predicted values and destroys only the hex-to-hex mapping, so it isolates *spatial* skill from the ability to emit plausible-looking magnitudes. Spearman collapses from **+0.53 to −0.001 (Human)** and **+0.34 to −0.001 (Natural)**. The skill is real and it is spatial.
  - **Reporting both MAE and Spearman, and noting they disagree.** Uniform beats random noise on MAE while being useless for ranking. For a siting product the planner needs to know which hexes to rank first, so Spearman is the metric that matches the decision — and reporting only MAE would have hidden the entire result.
  - **The agent's refusal to publish its own ablation numbers.** It excluded them from the notebook and wrote a section explaining why. Given that a null result is publishable in this project, the distinction that matters is that a null has to be measured with an instrument that works.
  - **Human ranking better than Natural (+0.53 vs +0.34)** — the expected direction, and evidence for the Entry 6.2 reasoning: human ignition tracks near-static settlement geography, which a hex's own history describes almost completely.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent's characterisation of my own conclusion.** I said the takeaway was that nothing we had done improved our ability to infer. The agent pushed back that this treats the baseline as zero — persistence cuts deviance ~50% below the global rate, so the accurate statement is that we *can* predict ignition hexes well above chance and that what failed is the covariates improving on it. I accepted the correction; the distinction is the difference between a dead end and a finding.
- **Corrections worth recording:** the agent handed me an ablation table and then had to retract it. It also proposed a "fix" (log1p scaling) that made results dramatically worse — deviance 163 on Human, obvious nonsense — before isolating the real `log_area` bug by testing whether a tree given only persistence could reproduce the persistence floor. That diagnostic worked; the guessing before it did not.

### Entry 6.11
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** I said I was not ready to conclude the investigation, and that I understood the burn-perimeter history to be indicative of **combustible terrain** rather than depleted fuel.
- **The exchange:** The agent agreed and turned my reading into a discriminating test. Static terrain-quality and dynamic fuel-state make **opposite predictions about timing**: depletion implies strong suppression at year 0 recovering over 5–15 years, while terrain quality implies a flat curve. `seasons_since_burn` separates them, descriptively, with no likelihood assumption — so the test is immune to the harness problem from Entry 6.10. The first run showed a clean alternating high-low pattern that looked like a biennial signal; the agent recognized it as an **aliasing artifact** and diagnosed it: `seasons_since_burn` counts *seasons*, most perimeter burns are JJA, and JJA carries 0.44 natural starts per cell against DJF's 0.003, so consecutive offsets land on systematically different seasons. Restricted to JJA the answer is unambiguous. The curve is flat — **4.2× the never-burned rate at year 0, still ~3.5× at year 18**, with no suppression immediately after a burn. Holding persistence fixed: time effect **1.30×**, propensity effect **17.76×**.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The whole test.** My hypothesis from W6 was fuel consumption; the data says the opposite, and now says it with a mechanism-discriminating measurement rather than a correlation. Prior burn marks terrain that carries fire, and such terrain keeps carrying fire.
  - **The explanation it gives for Entry 6.10's instability.** Burn history and persistence are collinear because both encode the same static property — and persistence encodes it better, being built from all 2.27M ignitions rather than the 0.6% with MTBS perimeters. That converts one of the three ablation mysteries into a known property of the features.
  - **Keeping the aliasing artifact visible in the notebook** rather than quietly fixing it. It is a trap that any future season-offset feature will hit, and it produced a pattern convincing enough to have been reported as a finding.
  - **The scope caveat** — res-5 hexes, perimeter-linked burns, JJA 2010+. A depletion effect at finer grain or over sub-seasonal intervals would not be visible in this test, and the claim should not be read as broader than what was measured.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing in this beat. The agent declined to speculate on *why* the association runs this direction — listing reburn in lightning-prone terrain, grass and shrub regrowth being more ignitable than the mature fuel that burned, and detection effects in monitored scars — and left the interpretation open rather than picking one. Correct: separating those needs evidence we do not have.
- **What this does not touch:** the fuel-**condition** hypothesis. Prior burn measures fuel *history*; `hex_climate` measures how dry the fuel *is*. That question remains open and untested at hex grain.

### Entry 6.12
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** Capturing the session's results in a notebook, since the against-baseline numbers had been produced in throwaway shell scripts and existed nowhere durable.
- **The exchange:** The agent wrote `notebook/12_hex_ignition_baselines.ipynb` (25 cells) and then extended it with the decay analysis from Entry 6.11. It validated the code by extracting and executing every cell against the real data, but **left the notebook itself unexecuted** for me to run. It made one editorial decision I want on record: it **excluded the ablation ladder entirely** and wrote a "What is NOT established here" section listing the three contradictory results, the two resolved causes and the one outstanding one, rather than publishing numbers with caveats.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The exclusion of the unstable ablation.** The alternative was publishing numbers that swung −30%/−115%/+3.4% with a footnote. A caveat does not repair an instrument.
  - **Selecting for descriptive, likelihood-free results.** Every published finding in the notebook — the baseline comparison, the prior-burn association, the decay test — is a comparison of observed means or a rank correlation. None can be reached by the overdispersion defect or by any ablation specification. That selection is what makes the notebook safe to build a report on.
  - **Keeping the `log_area`-as-feature demonstration as a live cell.** The failure is silent: the model still trains, still predicts non-negative counts, still looks reasonable while over-predicting by 68%. Showing it is worth more than fixing it invisibly.
  - **Ordering the overdispersion measurement *before* any modeling section**, so it reads as a reason rather than an excuse.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing yet — but the agent offered to include the ablation numbers with caveats if I preferred, and I am leaving them out.
- **Still outstanding:** the W6 storyboard (BLUF and assertion headlines) remains TBD; the notebook and `src/hex_ignitions.py` are uncommitted; the negative-binomial/hurdle rebuild is unstarted; the fuel-condition hypothesis at hex grain is untested.

### Entry 6.13
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** I directed a new phase: nail down whether hex-grain climate boosts predictive power. This is the question the entire hex build was for — W4's climate null at Level III grain was attributed to *grain*, not to the data, so hex grain was supposed to rescue it. Mid-task I added three constraints: persist anything that sticks into code and notebooks, stop reinventing the baseline on every run, and cache the results once demonstrated.
- **The exchange:** The agent's first move was to fix the instrument rather than run the test. It found that **Spearman sidesteps the overdispersion problem entirely** — rank correlation is invariant to monotone transforms, and it verified this by showing a tree given only persistence scores an identical 0.3435 under Poisson, squared-error and a hurdle classifier. That made the metric stable regardless of the likelihood question left open in Entry 6.10. It then ruled out two candidate explanations for the earlier instability by experiment: **ties** (a tree on persistence alone reproduces the floor exactly, so tree discretisation is not destroying rank) and **overfitting** (a regularisation sweep from depth-6/300-iter down to depth-2/60-iter/l2=10 is *flat* at ~0.23, so variance is not the cause). Then my "stop reinventing the baseline" note landed and the agent identified that as the actual root cause: it had re-typed the `groupby → shift(1) → rolling(k).mean()` idiom **roughly eight times** across throwaway scripts — the precise duplication `src/trailing.py` was extracted to eliminate at region grain in W5. It wrote `src/hex_panel.py` **calling `trailing.TrailingMean`** rather than reimplementing it, and verified the module reproduces +0.3435 (Natural) and +0.5264 (Human) to four decimals on identical cell counts. The ladder then produced identical numbers three runs running. **The answer: no rung beats persistence — on either branch, in any season scope.** The best case is burn history on JJA Natural at −0.0038, a rounding error; climate costs −0.038 to −0.110.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **Fixing the instrument before running the test.** Three prior ablation attempts had produced contradictory numbers. Running a fourth on the same broken harness would have produced a fourth answer and no knowledge.
  - **The model-free marginal analysis, which is what makes this a finding rather than a shrug.** An ablation alone cannot distinguish "the model failed to use the covariate" from "the covariate carries nothing." Measuring raw rank correlations separates them — and the answer is precise: raw `pdsi` correlates −0.137 with JJA natural starts (physically correct sign, real signal), but as a **within-hex anomaly** it collapses to −0.073, and `water_deficit` falls from +0.160 to −0.004. The climate signal is almost entirely **cross-sectional** — it identifies dry *places*, not dry *years*. Dry places are static geography, which persistence already encodes more completely than four climate variables can.
  - **`src/hex_panel.py` calling `trailing.TrailingMean` instead of reimplementing the idiom.** This is the durable fix for my "stop reinventing the baseline" instruction. One definition, called everywhere.
  - **Caching the panel and the baseline scores** (`hex_panel_modelling.parquet`, `hex_baseline_scores.csv`). The floor is now *quoted*, not recomputed — which is also what prevents it being silently re-specified again.
  - **Computing climate anomalies on training years only.** The agent flagged that using the full record would let the held-out period's own climate define the normal it is measured against — a leak I would not have caught.
  - **Testing both raw and anomaly forms of the covariate.** They ask different questions (is this hex dry / is this hex drier than usual), and the gap between them is the entire finding.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing substantive this beat. My three mid-task constraints — persist it, stop reinventing the baseline, cache it — were all adopted, and the second one turned out to be the diagnosis rather than a style preference.
- **Why this null is stronger than W4's:** the W4 result could be dismissed as an artifact of averaging over 105 heterogeneous ecoregions with sign-inverting per-region correlations. This one tests that explanation directly, at a grain where ~15.8 TerraClimate cells resolve into each hex, and the covariates still do not add lift — with a model-free measurement showing *why* rather than merely *that*.
- **Scope, stated so it is not over-read:** what fails is *pre-season seasonal-mean dryness for predicting ignition counts*. This says nothing about whether the same covariates predict **burned area** given an ignition — a different target, and the one the W4 megafire finding was actually about. Sub-seasonal timing (a dry spell in the week before a lightning outbreak) is also invisible to a three-month seasonal mean.
- **Still outstanding:** the W6 storyboard (BLUF and assertion headlines) remains TBD; a dispersion-appropriate likelihood is still unbuilt but is **off the critical path**, since the siting product consumes a ranking rather than an expected count.

### Entry 6.14
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** After the hex-grain climate null, I raised that we would want to know whether satellite imagery helps with **either** question — where fires start, or how much burns. Only the first had a target built. I directed the agent to build the burned-area target while I was offline.
- **The exchange:** The agent measured the target before designing for it, and the distribution decided every subsequent choice: **the top 1% of burning cells hold 55% of natural acres and the top 10% hold 98%**, spanning five orders of magnitude from a median of 1 acre to a maximum of 606,945. It concluded the target contains two different questions — *does this hex burn at all* (4% of cells) and *how much given that it burns* — and that a single model asked both would answer only the first, because 96% of rows are zeros. It built `src/hex_acres.py` with a hurdle split and a log10 target. **Then it produced a wrong result and caught it.** Scoring magnitude against the all-season trailing mean gave Spearman **−0.052** for Natural, which it reported to me as "burn size is essentially unpredictable from history, and if anything mildly anti-informative." Checking the largest cells, it found an implied error of **10⁸×** — physically impossible — and traced it to its own `LOG_FLOOR = -4` placeholder. A hex that has never burned carries −4 meaning "no history", but it was being scored as a *prediction of 0.0001 acres* against real megafires. **18% of burning cells were in that state.** Conditioning the baseline on burning seasons only, and dropping no-history cells as genuinely unscoreable, reversed the finding to **+0.37**, with typical error falling from ~370× to 6.3×.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The hurdle split.** Not a modeling preference — a property of the data. 96% zeros means the two questions cannot share a model without one of them being ignored.
  - **The self-correction, and the decision to keep the bug visible in the notebook.** The agent had already told me burn size was unpredictable; it went back, found the cause, and reversed itself. The wrong answer was *internally coherent* — plausible magnitude, the expected sign, consistent with a prior belief — and nothing about the number flagged it. What flagged it was a physically impossible error ratio. That is worth a reader's attention more than a silent fix would be.
  - **The decile analysis**, which is the finding I would not have asked for. Error grows monotonically with burned area: 9× over-prediction on the smallest cells, 1.07× at the median, **855× under-prediction on the top decile**. That reproduces the W4 megafire result at hex grain and converts "median miss 6.3×" — which is misleading, since the median cell burns 1 acre — into a precise statement of where the model fails.
  - **The `require_history` flag rather than an imputation.** Cells with no prior burn are unscoreable against a persistence floor by construction. NaN is the honest answer; a filled value would have been a second version of the same bug.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Folding this into notebook 12.** The agent proposed a separate notebook 13 and I agreed — 12 is titled and framed for ignition, the acres work is a different target with a different grain treatment and model structure, and burying it as an appendix would hide the half my stakeholder actually cares about.
- **What I asked for afterwards:** a plain-language description of the finding, then whether that framing was actually *in* the notebook. It was not — the notebook had the evidence but stopped at the technical statement, and the 55%/98% concentration stat was computed but never connected to the decile result. Joining them produces the load-bearing sentence: **the baseline is reliable on the cells holding almost none of the acres, and unreliable on the cells holding nearly all of them.** The notebook now carries both an analyst summary and a plain-language one.

### Entry 6.15
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** Running the MODIS fuel-density probe against both targets. I said I wanted to know whether NDVI adds value and that it would be good to have in the W6 deliverable — the agent began resuming the fetch, and I stopped it, because I was describing my thinking rather than issuing an instruction. It held until I asked.
- **The exchange:** The fetch completed at 126/126 units, zero failures, 55,923 hex-seasons across six forest ecoregions, and the regional NDVI ordering was physically right (Klamath coastal forest 0.649, Idaho Batholith and Great Plains ~0.27). **Ignition: a fourth null.** Raw NDVI +0.004, anomaly −0.009, and the marginals repeated the pattern the climate work established — raw NDVI correlates +0.228 with JJA natural ignitions but only +0.098 as a within-hex anomaly. **Burned area: the first covariate gain in the project.** NDVI alone +0.001, climate alone −0.008, and **together +0.049**. The agent flagged this itself as the pattern most likely to be an artifact — a combination beating both its parts — and refused to report it until it had run two checks I approved. **Check 1 (is it real):** shuffling the covariates across cells while keeping persistence intact gave −0.0134 ± 0.0024, never better than −0.0089, against the real +0.0493 — roughly **25 standard deviations** apart, and the gain held across five forward-chaining split years (+0.012 to +0.066). **Check 2 (is it useful):** it is not. Broken out by burned-area decile, the improvement lands entirely in deciles 6–8 (fires of 1–20 acres), deciles 1–5 get *worse*, and the top decile goes from 855× under-predicting to **868×** — no improvement at all.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The two-check discipline, and the agent proposing it before I asked.** Check 1 alone would have produced a publishable-looking "+0.049, fuel density helps." True, and misleading to anyone using it to site mitigation. Check 2 is what converts a headline number into an actionable one.
  - **The mechanistic reading of why the combination works when neither part does.** Heavy fuel that is wet will not carry fire; dry ground with no fuel has nothing to burn. Fuel load and fuel dryness are jointly necessary, and a model given only one has no way to express that. This is the first result in the project where a covariate interaction has a physical story behind it.
  - **Recording the ignition null as a finding rather than a fourth disappointment.** Four independent covariates — drought, prior burn, vegetation density, and their combinations — have now failed on the same target for the same measured reason. That is a statement about the phenomenon: **where lightning fires start, at this grain, is a property of the place rather than of the year.**
  - **The contrast between the two notebooks as the substantive result.** The same covariate pair that says nothing about ignition location does say something about burned area. Fuel state does not tell you where a fire starts; it says something about how much burns once one does.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The agent acting on my stated thinking as though it were an instruction.** I said it would be good to have the NDVI result in the W6 deliverable and it started the fetch. I stopped it and said so explicitly. It held the fetch until I asked, which is the behavior I want — I was reasoning aloud about sequencing, not authorising a network job.

### Entry 6.16
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** Summarizing where the modeling had landed. My reading: persistence is the best we have for anticipating where fires start; the augmented model is slightly better for average-sized fires' acres; no model predicts megafires. Then I added the point I had raised before — **every megafire was once a small fire.**
- **The exchange:** The agent confirmed the summary and then argued my last point was doing more work than I had claimed for it, and was testable. If a megafire is a small fire that escaped, the leverage moves upstream: a planner cannot pre-position against an unforecastable megafire, but can pre-position against the *ignition* that might become one — and ignition location is exactly what the project predicts well. It also sharpened one thing: "no model predicts megafires" understates the result, because what has been shown is that **pre-season** covariates do not; a same-day model with wind and suppression status is a different problem and untested. It proposed two questions with different answers, and I approved the test. **Question 1 — is ignition a gate? Yes:** a hex-season that ignites at all is **22.8×** more likely to produce a ≥1,000-acre burn (6.7% vs 0.29%). **Question 2 — does ignition *count* rank the risk? No:** escape probability per ignition *falls* steadily with count, and **49% of big fires came from hexes with exactly one natural ignition that season, 70% from two or fewer.** Start count also ranks burned area worse than the hex's own burn history (+0.253 vs +0.357).
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The whole test.** My premise survived, but its useful form changed from graded to **binary**: the question is not *how often does this place ignite* but *does it ignite at all*. That is a cleaner statement for a planner, and it rests on the strongest result in either notebook.
  - **The finding that high-ignition hexes are where fires get caught small.** The falling per-ignition escape rate is the opposite of what a naive "more starts, more risk" reading would predict, and it is why a ranked ignition count would be the wrong siting rule.
  - **The two bounds the agent attached without my asking.** (1) The gate is necessary, not sufficient — 93% of igniting hex-seasons still produce nothing large, so this narrows the field rather than identifying the fire. (2) This does not make megafires predictable; it relocates the leverage to a stage that is. Both keep the recommendation from being over-sold.
  - **Reframing a null as a recommendation.** If megafire size cannot be anticipated pre-season, then siting works against where fires *start* is the correct strategy rather than a fallback from a failed one. That is the argument the W6 storyboard rests on.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing this beat. The agent's correction to my own phrasing — pre-season covariates fail, which is narrower than nothing predicts megafires — is one I accepted, because the distinction is what leaves the same-day question open rather than closed.
- **Still outstanding:** the W6 storyboard (BLUF and assertion headlines) remains TBD in the status report and is the graded item due Sunday; notebooks 12 and 13 are uncommitted; the doc-consolidation work from Entries 6.7/6.8 is still uncommitted and needs its own commit.

### Entry 6.17
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** A scope conversation. I described the product I had actually envisioned: a planner sits down, the **Tier 1** model starts them down three tracks — mitigation of natural-cause fires, prevention of human-caused fires, a quality-improvement plan for missing-cause fires — and they drop into Tier 2 for each. Natural gets the hex ignition surface and burned-area target; Human gets a ranked cause triage, possibly with the same hex products; Unknown gets a ranked triage by agency and region.
- **The exchange:** The agent checked the branch notebooks against that description and reported that the architecture was **already ~85% built**, correcting its own earlier understatement: T1 in `06_analysis.ipynb`, Human cause triage in `08_human_cause.ipynb`, and — further along than it had credited — the Unknown branch in `09_unknown_dataquality.ipynb`, which already produces the ranked "where should the planner invest in cause reporting" list, filtered so a region-season qualifies only if attribution is weak *and* the burn is material. It identified two real gaps: the Unknown triage is by region but not by **agency**, and nothing joins the tiers into a single planner walkthrough. It also flagged that its own W6 storyboard led with the hex Natural surface, which under my architecture is one of three tracks presented as though it were the whole product. I then said to leave the remaining tracks for the future and present with the Level III product; the agent began reading the Level III branch results, and I stopped it and redirected to hex-grain work — I had changed my mind mid-thread rather than the agent misreading me. It paused and asked which of two readings I meant rather than guessing, since "hex-grain work" could have meant presenting the existing Natural surfaces or building new Human ones. I confirmed: build the Human hex products.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The agent asking instead of guessing when my two messages pointed opposite ways.** It named the ambiguity precisely — present existing hex work, or build new hex work for Human — and offered that its "85% built" summary might itself have been what prompted my reversal. That was the right read to check.
  - **The what/where formulation that came out of it.** Level III answers *which causes to target*; hex grain answers *where to site the work*. They are different decisions made with different data, not competing versions of one product. This resolves the scope tension rather than papering over it, and it means **RQ2 survives intact** — the next-season cause-risk profile was always the Level III product, and the hex work extends rather than replaces it.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **My own instruction from two messages earlier.** I said present with the Level III product, then reversed to hex-grain. Recording it as mine, not the agent's misreading.
  - **The agent's storyboard ordering**, implicitly. Leading with hex ignition inverts the hierarchy; the deck should open at T1, establish *what* at Level III, then drop to hex for *where*. Not yet rewritten.

### Entry 6.18
- **Date:** 2026-08-05
- **What was going on:** Building the Human branch at hex grain — the ignition-likelihood and burned-area surfaces that Natural already had. I told the agent to write the notebook before starting anything else ("put away your toys").
- **The exchange:** No new data was needed: `starts_human` and `acres_human` were already in the cached panels. The agent checked seasonality first and found the structural difference that shaped everything after — **human fire is a four-season phenomenon**, starts peaking in MAM and acres in JJA, against ~62% of natural ignitions falling in JJA alone. Notebooks 12 and 13 restrict to JJA; doing that here would have discarded most of the target, so every Human result is reported per season. Three findings followed. **(1) Human ignition is the most predictable surface in the project** — all-season Spearman **+0.53**, spring **+0.59**, against natural's +0.34, with every shuffled control within ±0.003 of zero. **(2) Both burned-area stages carry skill** — occurrence +0.45 to +0.57, magnitude +0.43 to +0.45. **(3) The tail failure is two orders of magnitude milder than Natural's** — the human top decile is **12.3x** under-predicted on a median cell of 135 acres, against natural's **855x** on a median of 5,073. Covariates were a fifth consecutive null, and the agent noted this one needed no explanation hunting: fuel state has no mechanism to move where roads and houses are.
- **What I kept, and why:**
  - **The per-season treatment.** Not a preference — the seasonality measurement forced it, and pooling or JJA-restricting would have understated the branch.
  - **The tail comparison as the branch's defining result.** Notebook 13 concluded that megafire size is not forecastable pre-season, so mitigation should be sited against *ignition* rather than predicted burn size. The agent flagged that this argument was built on the natural branch and **does not transfer wholesale**: human burned area is predictable to within about an order of magnitude even in the top decile, so a human-branch product can reasonably rank hexes by expected acres. The two branches support different products because their tails behave differently — I would have carried the natural conclusion across without that check.
  - **Naming the Unknown asymmetry deliberately in the summary.** Unknown has a Level III triage and no hex counterpart. That is defensible — reporting quality is an agency-and-region property, not a spatial one — but the notebook says so explicitly rather than leaving it as an artifact of what happened to get built.
- **What I rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - Nothing this beat.

I told it to do a complete regression.

Neither summary said which. That mattered because notebook 14's headline comparison put human 12.3x (all seasons, all regions) against natural 855x (JJA, six regions) — a mismatched pair that inflated the gap into "two orders of magnitude."

Corrected to 12.3x vs 270x, still a 22-fold gap, and the storyboard no longer claims "two orders of magnitude."

  - **Three smaller corrections the regression caught:** ~25 SD to **26.6**, the shuffled bound stated as ±0.003 when notebook 13's magnitude shuffles reach ±0.007, and **~62% of natural ignition in JJA when the computed value is 78.1%** — that last one appeared in no output anywhere and had propagated into both `CLAUDE.md` and the report.
  - **The confound measurement the agent surfaced unprompted.** Notebook 12 holds hex propensity fixed and finds burn *timing* moves ignitions **1.3x** while the hex itself moves them **17.8x**. That is the sharpest evidence in the project for "place, not year," and it was computed but unused in the narrative. It is now the evidence on storyboard beat 12.

### Entry 6.21
- **Date:** 2026-08-08
- **What was going on:** After the regression, I wanted the covariate story drawn rather than asserted — beat 13 rests on a pair of correlations (NDVI +0.228 raw vs +0.098 within-hex) that no audience absorbs in thirty seconds. That turned into a longer design conversation that ended somewhere other than where it started.
- **The exchange:** The agent measured before designing and found the figure in one line: between-place sd 0.132 against within-hex sd 0.048, so **NDVI is ~2.8x more about which hex than which year**. It built `src/w6_visuals.py` and `notebook/15_w6_visuals.ipynb` following the W5 conventions (no text baked into images, every function returns the caption numbers). Then I pushed on several ideas and it pushed back with measurements rather than agreement:
  - **A scatter of past vs future ignitions colored by NDVI.** Built, then the agent flagged its own figure: pooled across the six ecoregions NDVI correlates **+0.385** with future ignitions, so the color *would* sort and a reader would conclude NDVI predicts ignition — the opposite of the finding. It was showing the marginal correlation, not the incremental one.
  - **A map of where NDVI adds signal.** The agent ran the check first: pooled, NDVI explains **+0.038** of the persistence residual, and the per-hex distribution is symmetric around zero (IQR −0.109 to +0.198) — the shape of noise at ~21 observations per hex. It said mapping it would draw sampling variability and invite me to read it as signal, and named the shuffled control that would settle it. I dropped the idea; my selection bias was leading me.
  - **Siting by burned acres.** When I asked for gray burned hexes under the siting recommendation, the agent stopped before rendering: scored against acres the same ranking captures **4.6%** of the burn against **49.7%** of ignitions. Not a rendering problem — the project's central finding appearing on real ground.
- **What the student kept, and why:**
  - **Every refusal to draw something.** Three times the agent declined to render a figure I asked for and gave the measurement that made it wrong. That is the behavior I want; a compliant agent would have produced three misleading slides.
  - **The final design, which is not what I first asked for.** I wanted hits and misses in four colors. The agent built it, then showed that with a fifth of the ground treatable and 63% of hexes igniting, **89 misses against 35 hits is guaranteed by arithmetic** — the map reads as failure no matter how good the ranking is. Its diagnosis: this is a *ranking under a budget*, not a classification, and scoring it as classification is the wrong frame.
  - **Tiers calibrated by capture rather than by ground.** My instinct was saturation; the agent's addition was to cut the tiers at 30/60/90% of starts caught rather than at a ground budget it had invented. That removed an arbitrary parameter that had been deciding whether the figure looked like success or failure, and it surfaced the real finding: **30% of starts sit under 6.1% of the ground (4.95x lift), while 90% takes 77.8% (1.16x)**. The pale third tier is now the argument for stopping early rather than a recommendation to treat.
  - **The causal caveat, unprompted.** When I said catching ignitions would stop those acres burning, the agent agreed it was the right mechanism but named it a hypothesis the record cannot test — FPA-FOD contains fires that happened, never fires an intervention stopped. It also noted two of my own results that complicate it: per-ignition escape rates *fall* with count, and 49% of large fires came from single-ignition hexes that a frequency ranking tends to leave untreated.
- **What the student rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Four-color outcome maps and stroke encoding.** Stroke made the reader inspect edges hex by hex; four categorical fills made errors dominate. Both failed the "lands at a glance" test I set.
  - **Side-by-side panels**, twice. A reader should not have to hold one panel in memory to read another.
- **Still outstanding:** the notebook title still describes a covariate notebook although it now opens with the product; `plot_siting_map`, `plot_siting_single` and `plot_persistence_vs_covariate` are superseded and should be pruned; `CLAUDE.md`'s pipeline line stops at notebook 14.

- **What was going on:** Building evidence for slide 1. The beat-1 evidence column was the only one in the storyboard describing a slide ("a map and a budget") rather than a measurement, and its headline — "Prevention is a pre-season decision" — asserted agency practice, which `CLAUDE.md` forbids.

  - **The agent's own correction of its framing.** It flagged that the three-way distinction is prevention / mitigation / response and that the deck's opening had collapsed the first two into a single "prevention" frame while the second half of the deck is almost entirely mitigation siting. That is a real structural defect in the storyboard, not just a wording problem.
  - **The finding that the seasonal outlook does not forecast cause.** The agent searched the full August 2026 outlook and found zero occurrences of "human-caused"; cause appears only as physical description ("when lightning or human ignitions occur"), never as a forecast quantity. That is the precise hole this project sits in, and it is stronger than the gap-statement framing the agent had proposed one round earlier.
  - **Its warning against overclaiming the gap.** It noted the seasonal-outlook infrastructure is more mature than a "planners have nothing" framing implies — monthly issuance, four-month horizon, mandatory GACC products, PSA-level spatial resolution — and that the deck should claim the narrower, defensible thing: what is missing is cause composition and sub-PSA siting.
  - **Prestemon as the warrant.** "Scientists have not advanced much in the development of similar hotspotting tools for wildfires... especially those ignited by people," written under the National Cohesive Strategy by a team including a Florida Forest Service mitigation coordinator. That names my Human branch as an identified gap, in a source I already had.
- **What the student rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The containment-duration evidence entirely.** Not weakened — dropped. It answers a question the deck does not ask.
- **What the student had to decide:** the agent stopped on two citation fields rather than guessing — the Mobilization Guide's corporate author (PDF metadata says National Interagency Coordination Center; search results variously said NWCG and NIFC) and where agency products belong in `literature.md`. I chose NICC per the document's own metadata, and a separate "Agency planning products" subsection, because these are evidence of practice rather than research findings and should not sit in the same list as peer-reviewed work.
- **What this changed:** two sources ingested (`nicc-2023-mobilization-guide-ch60-predictive-services.pdf`, `nicc-2026-national-significant-wildland-fire-potential-outlook.pdf`) under a new `## Agency planning products` heading. Slide 1 becomes a documented-practice contrast rather than an assumption: a monthly, four-month, PSA-grain outlook exists and is used for staffing and allocation — it just does not forecast cause.

The research is sound and the sources are worth having, but the storyboard is settled and I am not rebuilding the narrative in W6. The agent dropped it without argument when told.

The agent's read: printing that beside the word "typical" asserts exactly what the project's own tail analysis disproves. It surfaced this as a correctness problem rather than quietly switching grain. Related: it had already found that anchoring the spring callout to the curve's global maximum pointed the leader at the July 4 week while the caption read "spring."

I went further and dropped both, leaving "most fires start in spring" and "most acres burn in summer" — two claims, no numbers to challenge. The derivation and the mean/median caveat moved into the notebook, where they can be precise.

I tried the agent's longer suggestion ("The season that starts the most fires is not the season that burns") and cut it: that sentence is the *caption's* job, and as a headline it spends the opening slide explaining a nuance before the audience has accepted the premise. Three words assert the premise; the figure supplies the nuance.

### Entry 6.29
- **Date:** 2026-08-08
- **What was going on:** Slide 3, "A region's cause mix is stable enough to forecast." The evidence line promised "flat lines, not swings."
- **The exchange:** Long, and it changed the slide three times before landing.
  - **The agent checked the claim first and found it unsupported.** Mean within-cell SD of natural share is 0.246 against a between-cell spread of 0.291 — a ratio of 1.18x, nothing like the 2.8x separation NDVI shows. Region-seasons genuinely move. It said drawing "flat lines" would misrepresent the data and a podmate plotting any mixed cell would catch it.
  - **It found the real structure:** volatility splits along the same bimodality as beat 2. Committed cells hold still (sd 0.151 human-dominated, 0.186 natural-dominated), mixed cells swing (0.294), Spearman −0.88 between commitment and volatility. It flagged unprompted that part of that is *mechanical* — a share pinned near 0 or 1 has less room to move — so "commitment causes stability" would overclaim.
  - **I asked for a volatility map at yearly grain**, which it built. Then I asked the underlying question — is the mix stable enough to forecast? — and it scored persistence against two uninformed baselines, which is what actually answers it.
  - **I asked for three tiles.** It invoked the dataviz skill, which says the answer is sometimes not a chart, and built stat tiles.
- **What the student kept, and why:**
  - **Inverting TVD to "share placed on the right cause."** The agent verified — and the notebook now asserts — that on a simplex `1 − TVD` is exactly the overlap between predicted and actual composition. So the inversion is not a cosmetic flip to make the big number the good one; the label is literally what the arithmetic computes.
  - **The national-average tile.** Not in my original three, and it is the most quotable thing on the slide: applying the national mix to a specific region scores **42%**, *worse* than an uninformed even split at 52%. That is beat 2's bimodality resurfacing as forecast error.
  - **`talk_notes.md` guidance on saying the number.** I asked whether 73% means "right 73% of the time." It does not, and the agent's catch is that a *second* number — top-1 agreement, 72.7% — is 0.7 points away and does mean "of the time." Saying "right 73% of the time" while the tiles are on screen describes the wrong metric.
- **What the student rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **Captions inside the PNG.** The agent had added a footnote line and a per-tile TVD figure. The module's own design contract says no captions in the image; I had it strip them.
  - **Then a title inside the PNG.** I said the slide should explain itself and the agent read that as "put the explanation in the figure," adding a title and subtitle. Wrong reading: the *slide* explains itself, and the slide is the headline plus the figure. The PNG is a figure. Rolled back.
  - **The two-line stability figure**, superseded by the tiles and pruned from the module. The volatility map is kept but unwired — it answers "how stable, really?" in Q&A.
- **What the student contributed:** the one-line description the notes now use — **73% is an accuracy derived from the magnitude of the error**, not from counting hits and misses. That is clearer than the agent's framing and it is what makes the "of the time" phrasing obviously wrong.

### Entry 6.30
- **Date:** 2026-08-08
- **What was going on:** Slide 4, "More history is better" — the k-sweep.
- **The exchange:** The agent verified the storyboard's evidence line before drawing and found two errors in it. **The acre-weighted curve is not monotonic** — it ticks up at k=4 (+0.0019 TVD) and k=8 (+0.0014); only the unweighted sweep falls monotonically, and acre-weighted is the metric that governs. And **`t-4` loses by 6.5 points against k=7**, not the ~5 the line claimed; the ~5 in notebook 06 is `t-4` against mean3/expanding, a different comparison. It then quantified the shape: 1→3 buys 5.3 points, 3→7 buys 1.2, and every window from 3 up sits within 1.4 points of the best.
- **What the student kept, and why:**
  - **The headline change, which I initiated but the agent had already argued for.** It flagged that "more history is better" overstates a curve that is front-loaded and then flat. I asked for something about a rolling average, and picked "A few years of rolling average is enough" from its three options — the one that leads with the plateau rather than the rise.
  - **Shading the plateau instead of marking k=7.** Once the headline says the window stops mattering, a figure crowning a winning k contradicts its own slide. The agent removed the k=7 marker and shaded from k=3 up, with `plateau_from` computed as the first window every longer window stays within tolerance of — not hardcoded.
  - **Refusing to smooth the non-monotonic dips.** Its reasoning: a forced-monotone line would assert a cleaner result than the data gives. The dips at k=4 and k=8 are visible on the slide and written into the notebook.
  - **Calling `trailing.TrailingMean` rather than reimplementing the window**, so the figure and notebook 06 cannot drift. The sweep reproduced notebook 06's printed table exactly.
- **What the student rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **"More history is better"** as a headline, and with it the implicit defense of k=7. I am not married to k=7 and the data says I should not be.
- **A second pass simplified the figure, in three steps I drove:** the plateau annotation went first (it restated the headline inside the figure), then I asked whether we were really measuring the loss function. We were not — the agent checked `TrailingMean.predict` and confirmed it is a shift and a rolling mean with nothing optimized, so TVD is an evaluation metric, not a loss. That exchange clarified TVD is an MAE-family score, which led to the last simplification: **plot TVD directly instead of `1 - TVD`, and drop the shaded band.** The curve now falls from 0.331 to 0.266, the axis values are exactly notebook 06's, and nothing on the canvas needs explaining. The tiles keep the inversion because a stat tile whose largest number is its worst result reads backwards; a falling curve has no such problem.
- **What this changed:** `plot_k_sweep` and `tier1_k_sweep` in `src/w6_visuals.py`, `img/w6_k_sweep.png`, a new notebook 15 section (seven results now), and beat 4's headline and evidence. The agent also updated the compression note, which had listed beats 4 and 6 together as "the simple thing won" — beat 4 is now a parameter-tuning beat whose finding is that the parameter barely matters, while beat 6 is the only beat conceding a model was tried and lost, which is what keeps the null credible.

On the Bayesian question: the hierarchy is already a chain-rule factorization, the global-prior tiles are literally priors and the history tiles posteriors, and what is genuinely missing is any propagated uncertainty — with a specific argument for where it would matter (the level, not the ranking).

Python bound the later one, so the old legend kept rendering and the fix appeared to do nothing. It diagnosed this by grepping for duplicate `def` lines, confirmed which copy was intact, removed the stale block, and verified that the NDVI functions the duplicate had swallowed were still whole.
- **What this changed:** `plot_siting_glance` rewritten again (two bands, leader labels, `locator` and `label` flags, `cfg` parameter); beat 8's evidence column and two notebook 15 sections updated with the band table, including the incremental figure the cumulative labels hide — the light band alone buys +28.4% of starts for +22.7% of ground, a 1.25x lift.

### Entry 6.36
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** Beat 9, "That skill is spatial, not statistical luck" — the shuffled-control test.
- **The exchange:** I picked a predicted-vs-actual scatter over the agent's two bar-chart options. It flagged the obstacle before building: 84% of human hex-seasons and 96% of natural ones record zero starts, so 1.59M raw points would be a solid blob on the origin. Its fix was binning into 20 equal-count strata by predicted value and plotting each stratum's mean prediction against its mean outcome — which is the honest version of "does the ranking order correctly" and works at any density. It built the two-panel version first; I asked for the series superimposed instead, and that is clearly the better figure.
- **What the student kept, and why:**
  - **The binned calibration form.** It shows the relationship rather than summarizing it, which is what the beat needs: a Spearman value cannot distinguish "the ranking knows where fires start" from "the target has structure this metric rewards," but a control that keeps every value and destroys only the pairing can.
  - **Recomputing from `hex_panel` rather than transcribing.** Reproduced `12_hex_ignition_baselines.ipynb` exactly on the first attempt — real ρ +0.5264 / MAE 0.4316, shuffled ρ +0.0002 / MAE 0.7666.
  - **The MAE observation the bars would have hidden.** Shuffling does not merely remove the signal, it makes the prediction *worse than useless*: MAE rises to 0.767, above even the uniform train-mean baseline's 0.703. A confidently wrong prediction costs more than no prediction at all.
- **What the student rejected or overrode, and why:**
  - **The two-panel layout.** Side by side, a reader has to hold the left shape in memory to judge the right one, and the flat line only reads as flat *against* the climb. Superimposed, the two series share an origin and separate — the divergence becomes the mark itself rather than a comparison the reader performs.
- **Two label fixes I asked for afterwards, both worth recording as method:** the series label trailing to the right of its last point broke the plane of the final x tick, so it moved to sit directly above the endpoint; and the "perfect calibration" label was hardcoded at 38 degrees, which is only correct for one figsize. The agent replaced the constant by measuring the diagonal's angle off `ax.transData` after a draw — the screen angle of a 45-degree data line is not 45 degrees unless the axes are square, and now it stays aligned if the figure is ever resized.
- **The label went through three versions, and the student supplied the framing that settled it.** "The ranking" was project jargon a viewer could not parse; "forecast vs random" was legible but described the control loosely; the agent argued for "shuffled" on the grounds that random is the *weaker* control — it changes the values and the pairing at once, so its failure is ambiguous, while a permutation changes exactly one thing. I then put it in the words the talk now uses: **we are not changing how many fires the region is forecast to get, only where we say they will be.** That names precisely what the control isolates — the forecast has a level and an allocation, and shuffling holds the level fixed while destroying the allocation.
- **Plain-language delivery notes added.** I asked for language to describe the chart out loud; `talk_notes.md` now carries a one-liner ("same numbers, wrong places — and the prediction stops working"), a 30-second prose version, four follow-up answers, and one phrase to avoid: **do not say "the model is accurate."** The line runs below the diagonal — it under-predicts the busiest hexes — so the honest claim is "it ranks well, it does not promise counts," which is also the distinction the whole product rests on.
- **What this changed:** `plot_shuffled_control` and `shuffled_control` in `src/w6_visuals.py`; `img/w6_shuffled_control.png`; a new notebook 15 section; beat 9's evidence column; a delivery section in `coursework/W6/talk_notes.md`.

### Entry 6.37

I rejected the first premise — the seasonality half is already beat 1, so repeating "when fires happen" would be redundant — and then raised the larger gap: **we have never shown the audience any distribution or confidence for any prediction in this deck.** Every number in beats 1–9 is a bare point estimate.

It then checked the comparison that matters and found **human beats natural in all 44 season-years, 0 exceptions**, with the nearest approach in JJA (human's worst year 0.449 against natural's best 0.456, in different years). That is a materially stronger claim than the pooled +0.53 vs +0.34 could support.

It also found the same unmeasured roads claim stated as fact in the report's **project summary**, not just in beat 10, and softened it there too.

### Entry 6.40
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** I asked whether a fully-burned hex cell would be around 63,000 acres.
- **The exchange:** Yes — 62,494. Putting the deck's numbers against that scale was clarifying (even the worst-burning natural cells are only ~13% burned) but it also exposed an anomaly the agent chased down: **81 hex-seasons exceed a full hex of acres, the largest at 606,945 — 971% of a cell.** Tracing it through `hex_acres_res5.parquet` split the cause in two. The 69 perimeter-sourced cases are mostly legitimate: those fires *are* distributed across a median of 10 hexes with weights 0.10–1.0, and Alaska megafires are simply larger than a cell. The 23 point-sourced cases are a genuine defect — a point-only fire puts its whole acreage on the single hex containing its ignition. `hex_acres` justifies that at the 14-acre point-fire average, but **2,710 point fires over 1,000 acres carry 8.9% of all acres** on that basis.
- **What the student proposed:** impute a circular burn area from the ignition point for fires with no perimeter. The agent checked the geometry and found it favorable — a 606,945-acre circle has a **28 km radius against a hex's ~9.9 km circumradius**, so it would spread across ~10 cells instead of one — and reused `hex_burn`'s existing weight machinery rather than inventing anything.
- **What the student decided:** **write-up for W6, real work for W7.** The agent had recommended deferring on three grounds and I agreed with the split rather than the deferral: the fix is right, but rebuilding `hex_acres_res5.parquet` invalidates notebooks 13–15 and every acres figure in the deck, and the W6 findings do not move because beats 11 and 12 use rank statistics.
- **What the agent flagged to carry into the build:** fires elongate along wind and terrain, so a circle over-assigns upwind and under-assigns downwind. That is a *directional* error in a product whose whole purpose is siting work by location — worth testing against the perimeter-backed fires, where the true shape is known.
- **What this changed:** a "Known defect — point attribution of large unperimetered fires" section in `CLAUDE.md`, with the qualifier added to the MTBS line that previously said point attribution "is fine"; the W7 fix as the lead item in next week's To Do; a matching note in notebook 15. Two stale to-do items fixed in passing — beat 12 was still listed as a compression candidate (it is now beat 11's payoff) and the Human learned-rung item still described a rung that Entry 6.32 recorded as built and beaten.

### Entry 6.47
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** Beat 15 — "Megafire size is not forecastable before the season. Stop targeting it." The pivot of the deck: beats 10–14 descend, beat 16 starts the recovery.

It has **no new evidence** — every number it could show has already been shown (the tail is beat 11, the concentration beat 10, the nulls 12–14) — and it is the **hinge**, where the report already warns that 15→16 can read as "a failure followed by an unrelated consolation." Its conclusion: a chart here is actively risky, because re-showing evidence invites the audience to re-audit numbers at the one moment the talk needs them accepting a conclusion.
- **The three options offered:** a scope-of-the-null diagram; no figure at all (a statement slide); or reusing beat 11 with the tail circled.
- **What the student decided:** try the scope diagram, with the statement slide as the fallback if it does not land. It landed.
- **What was built:** `plot_null_scope` — **the deck's only figure that plots no data**. A closed gray box of what was tested and failed (drought, fuel load, prior burn, the combinations, all pre-season) above an open dashed orange box of what was not (wind, timing, crews on hand), with an arrow running down into it.
  - **The design argument worth keeping:** what is *not* yet on any slide is the null's **scope**, and scope is what separates a publishable null from a shrug. The untested box is drawn open — dashed, unfilled, below the closed one — so it reads as a door rather than a hole in the work, because beat 16 walks through it.
  - **One revision on review:** the top box originally said only that the covariates were pre-season, never what they failed *at*. Now reads "all known before the season opens — none predicts how big it gets," so a viewer who blinked through beats 12–14 still gets the claim.
- **What went into `talk_notes.md`:** a delivery section, because this slide is spoken more than read. The load-bearing instruction is **say "before the season" every time** — without it the sentence becomes "megafire size is unpredictable," a much bigger claim than anything measured and one a fire scientist could fairly challenge. Plus the answer to "did you try hard enough?": pre-season data was tried hard, same-day data was not tried at all, and both halves of that are good.
- **A small implementation note:** matplotlib's `Text` has no `letterspacing` property; the tracked-out box labels are spaced by joining the characters.
- **What this changed:** `plot_null_scope`; `img/w6_null_scope.png`; beat 15's evidence column; three notebook 15 cells; a new "Delivering the concession" section in `talk_notes.md`.

### Entry 6.48
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** Beat 16 — "Every megafire was an ignition first." The slide that has to make beat 15's concession productive.
- **What was built:** `plot_ignition_gate` — two bars in a common light track, no axis, both percentages printed: **6.7%** of igniting hex-seasons produced a ≥1,000-acre burn against **0.29%** of non-igniting ones, a **22.8x** gate. JJA natural, held-out years, from notebook 13 cell 22.
  - **Why the plainest possible encoding.** This is the deck's only comparison of two probabilities. Every other beat reuses an axis the audience has already learned (beat 11's decile axis appears three times), but there is nothing to reuse here, and a 22.8x ratio between two numbers under 7% would need a log axis to draw "fairly" — which would make the audience decode a scale instead of seeing a contrast. Two bars in a shared frame, so the eye compares filled fractions rather than floating lengths.
  - **The percentages are printed because the small bar is nearly invisible at true scale** — which is the finding, not a drawing problem.
- **A discrepancy the agent caught in its own figure.** The first render printed the ratio as **"23x"** at zero decimals, while the report, `CLAUDE.md` and the storyboard all quote **22.8x**. A slide disagreeing with the write-up is the kind of thing a reader notices and an author does not. Fixed to one decimal, with the reason written into the code so it does not drift back.
- **What the notebook prose adds:** why this rescues the recommendation rather than consoling it. Beats 10–14 would be a dead end *if burned area were the only thing a planner could site against*. The gate says consequential burns come almost entirely from igniting cells, and beat 7 says which cells those are (+0.53 human, +0.34 natural against a ~0.00 shuffled null). So the product changes shape: **rank ground by ignition likelihood, not by predicted acres** — a surface the data supports, where predicted acres is one it does not.
- **The caveat carried forward:** a gate is binary. It licenses "does this place ignite," not "how often." Reading beat 16 as "rank hexes by expected ignition count" would get the product wrong, which is precisely what beat 17 exists to prevent — escape probability per ignition *falls* with count, and 49% of large fires came from hexes with exactly one natural ignition.
- **What this changed:** `plot_ignition_gate`, `GATE`, `GATE_SOURCE`; `img/w6_ignition_gate.png`; beat 16's evidence column; three notebook 15 cells.

### Entry 6.49
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** Returning to beat 15 after building 16. Entry 6.47's scope diagram was on screen and I wanted to revisit it.
- **The observation, and it is mine:** to improve the *siting* forecasts we had to come down from the region to the hex. Here we are potentially saying that to improve the *fire size* forecasts we have to come down from the season to the day. Same move, other axis.
- **What the agent added:** that the symmetry is load-bearing rather than decorative, because it is the **same lesson the project has now met four times** and named once — W4's pooled climate null (*"the covariates are real but the grain of the model is wrong"*), beat 12's "dry places, not dry years," beat 7's region-to-hex drop, and now the season-to-day hypothesis. Three of those were diagnosed separately before anyone noticed they were one thing. It also flagged its own two doubts about the old figure, unprompted: the two-box diagram was a claim about *our methodology* rather than about fire, arriving right when the audience is deciding whether to trust us; and "NOT TESTED" was the larger, bolder half, so a viewer reading the figure before the headline got "here is a gap in our work" rather than an instruction.
- **What the student decided:** change the headline to the parallel — **"Siting needed a finer place. Size needs a finer moment."** Chosen over keeping the instruction with the parallel appended, and over the more abstract "we got the grain right in space and wrong in time."
- **What the student accepted as the cost:** the instruction — *"Stop targeting it"* — comes off the slide. It survives in the BLUF and beat 19, and beat 16 supplies the alternative one slide later. The agent flagged this trade before I chose rather than after.
- **What this changed structurally:** the figure was rebuilt from scratch. `plot_null_scope` (tested/untested boxes) is **superseded by `plot_grain_parallel`** — two rows, *where it burns: the region → the hex, solved* above *how big it gets: the season → the day, untested*, the first closed and the second dashed. The agent's note on why this reads better: the audience has already lived through the first grain drop and watched it work, so the second arrives as recognition rather than as a new claim. `img/w6_null_scope.png` deleted.
- **What this changed:** `plot_grain_parallel` replacing `plot_null_scope`; `img/w6_grain_parallel.png`; beat 15's headline and evidence; the gap-test paragraph in the report; the `talk_notes.md` concession section rewritten around the new framing, including a warm concession to anyone who spots the repeating pattern; three notebook 15 cells rewritten with the four-instance table.

### Entry 6.50
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** Two things — a check on what beat 16 actually licenses, then building beat 17.
- **The over-claim, and the correction.** I summarized beat 16 as "we get a lot of return on our investment if we can predict where fires start and mitigate their effects." The agent agreed with the first half and pushed back on the second, correctly: **nothing in this project measures what a treatment achieves.** No before/after, no control, no counterfactual. Beat 16 says igniting cells are 22.8x more likely to produce a big burn; it does not say working those cells makes the burn smaller. The distinction is **targeting** (supported) against **efficacy** (not). It also flagged that "a lot of return" needs the capture curve's caveat — 32% of starts under 6.1% of ground is 5.23x, but 90% of starts needs 77.8% of ground at 1.16x, so the return is real and decays fast.
  - **What I accepted:** the correction, and that the right citation for efficacy is Davis et al. (2024) — someone else's meta-analysis, roughly 62–72% severity reduction for thinning-plus-burning — to be **cited if asked, not folded into this project's findings.** This is the working agreement in `CLAUDE.md` about never asserting fire policy or practice.
  - **What the agent checked before writing anything:** whether the deck already over-claims. It does not — the BLUF and beat 19 both say *where to put effort*, never *what the effort achieves*, and the project summary's "whether they pay off depends on whether fire arrives there" is the correct form, a necessary condition rather than a sufficient one. Written into `talk_notes.md` as a Q&A defense rather than changing any slide.
- **Beat 17, and the design problem it posed.** The agent flagged that this beat **has to argue against its own most obvious chart**: escape probability genuinely *rises* with ignition count (5.4% at one start to 19.1% at 11–20), and plotting that curve alone would recommend ranking hexes by expected count — the exact product beat 17 exists to prevent.
  - **The two-panel answer.** Left: the two curves crossing, chance-of-a-big-fire rising while chance *per ignition* falls 0.054 → 0.014. Right: the decisive number, **49% of 2,724 large-fire cells had exactly one natural ignition**, 70% had two or fewer. Its stated reasoning — drawing only the second would be easier and would duck the honest objection; drawing only the first would be wrong.
  - **The provenance number is the argument, not the rate.** A conditional rate says what happens given a count; the provenance says where the consequential fires actually were. A siting rule ranked on count would deprioritise the ground that produced half of them.
- **What this fixes downstream:** beat 16 alone licenses "treat the hexes expected to ignite most." Beat 17 corrects it to "expected to ignite **at all**" — which is why `plot_siting_glance` draws two bands rather than graded tiers. The figure encoding and the finding were already consistent; now the beat that justifies it is on a slide.
- **What this changed:** `plot_one_is_enough`, `DIAL`, `DIAL_SOURCE`; `img/w6_one_is_enough.png`; beat 17's evidence column; three notebook 15 cells; a new "So if we treat those hexes, we cut the burn?" section heading the Q&A list in `talk_notes.md`.

### Entry 6.51
- **Date:** 2026-08-09
- **What was going on:** I said the beat 17 figure did not pass the take-it-in-at-a-glance test.
- **The agent's diagnosis, which was structural rather than cosmetic:** four faults. The left panel was **normalized to a ratio with no y-axis**, so both curves started together and diverged — encoding "one goes up, one goes down" while destroying the actual quantities, which are all probabilities between 1% and 19%. The two panels carried **different units** (a rate against a share of a population) with nothing marking the switch. The left panel argued a **rate-versus-total subtlety**, which is the supporting argument rather than the claim, and it was given the larger panel. And the headline is "One ignition is enough," so the 49% *is* the proof — yet it arrived second and smaller.
- **What the student chose:** the provenance bar alone. One stacked bar over all 2,724 large-fire cells: 49% one ignition, 21% two, 30% three or more, with only the first segment carrying color.
- **Where the counter-argument went.** The rate curves are not deleted — they moved to `talk_notes.md` as the answer to "but doesn't a cell with more ignitions carry more risk?" The agent's framing: that is a *question*, not the headline, and the same move we made on beat 12 when the NDVI variance figure became Q&A and the ablation ladder became the slide.
- **A data-integrity note worth keeping:** notebook 13 prints only two cumulative figures (49.3% at exactly one, 70.4% at two or fewer). The three bar segments are those differenced, so nothing on the figure is a number the notebook did not report. The agent checked this before drawing rather than inventing a third bucket.
- **What this changed:** `plot_one_is_enough` rewritten from two panels to one bar; `img/w6_one_is_enough.png`; beat 17's evidence column; three notebook 15 cells including a record of why the first version failed; a new Q&A section in `talk_notes.md` carrying the rate curves.

### Entry 6.52
- **Date:** 2026-08-09

Ordered by share, not by the headline's clause order, so the top row is where the acres are.

Moved to a new paragraph covering both no-data slides at once.
- **Second, and the substantive one: it is where we land, so it has to conclude and recommend.** The agent had closed the slide on *"three products, because the model has three classes"* — true, and a **specification rather than a conclusion**. Nothing on it said what the audience should do differently. Now it closes on **"Stop targeting how big it gets. Target where it starts,"** with the reliability caveat directly beneath. Two side effects worth recording: that line recovers the instruction that came off beat 15's headline when it was reframed as a grain diagnosis, and pairing it with its boundary is what five beats of nulls earned.

- **What this changed:** `plot_recommendation`; `img/w6_recommendation.png`; beat 19's evidence column, rewritten twice; a new "The two slides that show no data, and why" paragraph in the report; three notebook 15 cells.

### Entry 6.56

Verified after the change that slide geometry is untouched — nineteen slides, zero overflows, still exactly two visible shapes each.

## Week 7

### Entry 7.1
- **Date:** 2026-08-15
- **What was going on:** Reviewing the W6 deck ahead of the W7 dry run, I noticed the deck on disk had been edited in PowerPoint after `build_deck.py` generated it, and that I had deleted one slide along the way — beat 6, "A learned model made it worse."
- **The agent's first read was wrong, and worth recording.** It compared the .pptx to `BEATS` **by slide position** and reported nine reworded headlines plus a scrambled slide order. Matching figures by image content instead showed the order was correct: the deleted beat 6 shifted every later slide up one, and the "scrambled order" was that off-by-one. The headline rewordings are real; the order problem was an artifact of the diff method. **Check what you are keying on before reporting a discrepancy.**
- **What I asked for, and why:** insert the one missing slide only, leaving the other eighteen untouched — the port-forward into the script can wait. I had hand-resized sixteen of nineteen figures and set a warm off-white background, and a full rebuild would have discarded all of it.
- **What the agent measured before drawing anything:** background `FCFCFB` (not the script's pure white `FFFFFF`), and the neighbouring human-branch slide's figure geometry. `w6_human_ladder.png` is 2.854:1 against `w6_human_tiles.png` at 2.708:1, so it was sized to the same full frame width rather than to the script's fit-to-frame default — the two consecutive human-branch slides now match.
- **The verification is the part I care about:** the deck was diffed slide-by-slide against the committed version — geometry, text, image bytes, notes, background — with the inserted slide excluded. **Zero differences across all nineteen.** Nothing I had hand-tuned was touched.
- **What I got back that is better than what I deleted:** the restored figure is the re-sorted `w6_human_ladder.png` (bars descending 54 / 47 / 36, floor on top), and the speaker notes came back with it, including the WATCH line arguing the beat should be kept — it is the only slide conceding a model was tried and lost, which is what keeps the five nulls credible.
- **What this changed:** `coursework/W6/MSDS696_W6_Deck.pptx`, 19 slides → 20.
- **Still open:** the port-forward of the PowerPoint headline edits, the background color and the per-figure resizes into `src/build_deck.py`, which remains stale. Also noted for the W7 pass: slide 10 reads "Almost all the area burned **are** in almost none of the cells."

Renumbering the beats would have meant rewriting cross-references *inside* my own sourced notes ("matched pair with 11", "beat 11's axis again") — that is, editing the text the no-new-prose rule exists to protect. **Slide numbers give position; beat numbers are stable identities.** Sections sit in delivery order, so beat 9 now appears between beats 11 and 12.

### Entry 7.3
- **Date:** 2026-08-15
- **What was going on:** I raised what I think is the deck's real weakness for the executive challenge: it moves between predicting *shares*, *ranking*, *where fires start*, and *how many acres burn*, and a listener has no reliable cue for which one is on screen.
- **What the audit found, and it is three axes rather than one:** every slide is a combination of **grain** (Level III ecoregion vs. res-5 hex), **target** (cause composition vs. ignition counts vs. burned acres) and **quantity** (shares vs. rank vs. level). Beats 1–6 are region/cause; beat 7 changes all three at once. My own WATCH line on beat 7 already says exactly that — "three things change at once… the headline announces only the first; say the others" — so this was diagnosed in W6 and never fixed.
- **The sharpest instance:** "predictable" carries two meanings three slides apart. Beat 7's is a **rank** claim (Spearman); beat 11's is a **level** claim (log error on acres). The whole conclusion turns on that distinction — the order is trustworthy, the acre level much less so — and nothing before beat 19 prepares the audience for it.
- **The fix I chose: headlines, not speaker notes.** The headline is what the room reads and it costs no talking time. Starting with beat 6, "A learned model made it worse" left *it* unbound, and three slides later the deck is discussing acres.
- **I corrected the agent twice here, and both corrections mattered.** It first proposed "made the human-cause **mix** worse" — but that figure plots a **hit rate** (how often the leading human sub-cause out of 11 is named right), not a share, and putting a shares word on a hit-rate axis is the precise confusion my beat 3 WATCH line exists to prevent. It also misnumbered the slide as 7. Settled wording: **"A learned model made naming the leading cause worse."** "Naming" matches the verb already in beat 5's headline, so the two consecutive slides now read as one argument.
- **What this surfaced underneath:** beats 3, 5 and 6 use the same three-tile visual treatment but **not the same y-axis** — beat 3 is TVD-derived, beats 5 and 6 are top-1. Same look, different quantity. Open question I still need to answer from the notebook: which of the two beat 3's tiles actually plot.
- **What this changed:** beat 6's headline in the deck (I also enlarged that figure to 13.02in while I was in it, verified inside the slide); `final_script.md` synced, plus one WATCH line recording that the bars are a hit rate and that the TVDs listed in the evidence are *not* what is plotted.
- **Still open on this thread:** beats 4, 11 and 14 have the same unbound-target defect. Beat 14's "the gain misses **where** we need it most" is the most misleading of the three — "where" invites a map reading when the axis is cell size.
