### Entry 7.10
- **Date:** 2026-08-15
- **What was going on:** Reviewing beats 3 and 5, I asked whether we could state a level of confidence in those predictions. The agent's first answer measured the *spread of realized error* pooled across all held-out cells — which is an answer to a different question, and it said so once I pushed.
- **The reframing was mine and it is the whole finding.** Each region carries its own history, and each *season* within it is its own series, so a region-season has its own uncertainty. The right quantity is not "how variable is the error nationally" but "how settled has *this* cell been" — and that is available **before** the season opens, unlike realized error, which you only learn afterward.
- **What came out of it:** dispersion of the k=7 trailing window predicts the error of the k=7 trailing mean. **Spearman +0.484 on Tier 1, +0.577 on the 11-way human composition**, at 33 and 35 SD above a shuffled control. Split into quartiles by pre-season dispersion, Tier 1 runs **83.1% → 61.9%** accuracy and Human **72.5% → 39.4%** — the spread is half again as wide on the human branch, which is the weaker product, so its failures turn out to be anticipated rather than random.
- **The control I care most about is the within-region one,** which the agent proposed unprompted: across regions the correlation could merely restate "some regions are stable places," which is geography. It holds **inside** individual series — 74/93 and 80/92 positive — so it is genuinely about this cell in this year.
- **I made it verify the grouping before I believed any of it.** Asked how Klamath's four seasons were treated, the agent showed `(region, season)` grouping directly: winter never informs summer. Klamath DJF sits at dispersion 0.121 / 75.8% accuracy, JJA at 0.293 / 57.3%. One region, four levels of trust — and summer, where lightning competes with human ignition, is the volatile one.
- **The caveat is recorded as prominently as the result.** It **ranks** confidence, it does not calibrate it. And a quiet history is not a guarantee: 36 of 986 steadiest-quartile cells still scored below 25%, several at dispersion exactly 0.000 — settled right up until the regime breaks. Those are the confident-looking misses, and a planner would be misled by a flag that did not say so.
- **Where it went:** appended to `notebook/06_analysis.ipynb` as a fourth section in the notebook's own Hypothesis → code → **Finding** convention, reusing `TrailingMean`, `tvd` and `score_masked` rather than new machinery. The agent dry-ran all three cells outside the notebook first and caught a bad `cfg.group_keys` reference before I ever executed anything. **I have not run the notebook yet** — the cells carry no outputs until I do.
- **What this changed:** `notebook/06_analysis.ipynb` (+4 cells); `CLAUDE.md` — the "no uncertainty is propagated anywhere" line was flatly wrong after this and is now scoped to *calibrated* uncertainty, with the new result written up as its own settled section.

Cause: the headline is **acre-weighted** and the percentiles were **unweighted**. The national average mix fails hardest on the big-burn cells that dominate the acre weighting and does adequately on the many small ones, so both numbers were right and the pair read as an error. Fixed with a weighted-quantile helper so the span carries the same weighting as the mean — **one denominator throughout**, the same rule I imposed on the spoken shares in Entry 7.11.
- **The figure is better than the bare means, and the argument survived:** history spans **58–91%** against the national average's **21–61%**, barely overlapping. The national tile's first quartile reaching down to **21%** is the sharper version of beat 2's bimodality returning as forecast error.

- **The wording constraint I care about most:** the span is a **spread across region-seasons, not a confidence interval on the mean.** The figure's own drawn label says "typical range" so the distinction lives in the artifact rather than only in my notes, and a WATCH line forbids "plus or minus" and "we're X% confident."

### Entry 7.14
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Slide 5 is the deck's one conceded loss — a learned model tried and beaten by a trailing mean. Having just approved its SAY block, I stopped and asked: **how do we know the choice of model wasn't at fault?**
- **The honest answer was that we didn't.** The agent checked the code rather than answering from the write-up and found both learned rungs call `SimplexRegressor()` bare — `HistGradientBoostingRegressor` at stock settings, one family, one seed, no search. Every prior write-up conceded only the *tuning* half of that ("run once at standard settings and not tuned"); nothing anywhere conceded the *family* half, and the script's SAY said "**the model** could not beat taking its mean," which reads as all learned models.
- **What I asked for:** both the wording fix and the missing experiment. I did not want a Q&A defense standing in for a measurement.
- **The result, and it is not what I expected.** A ridge rung — same features, split, acre weights, held-out cells, simplex projection, only the learner swapped — **beats gradient boosting**: 52.2% top-1 against 47.5%, TVD 0.537 against 0.554. So the booster genuinely was paying a variance cost on a small wide panel (~5,300 training cells, 23 features, 11 correlated targets). **The rung I have been presenting was not the best learned model available.** It still loses to the floor's 54.1% / 0.489, which is what preserves the slide.
- **The finding that actually settles the question is the alpha sweep being flat** — top-1 identical to six decimal places from alpha 0.1 to 1000, TVD moving in the fifth decimal. The agent verified separately that alpha *does* propagate through the pipeline, so the flatness is real and not a plumbing bug. A four-decade regularization sweep that changes nothing is an information ceiling, not an under-tuned model. That is a far better answer to "did you tune it?" than anything I had before.
- **The control I asked the agent to add before I believed the tie:** is ridge just rediscovering the floor? If it were, "a linear model ties the floor" would be circular. It is not — 0.283 TVD units from the floor per cell, and it names a different top cause in a quarter of them. Two different learners converging just short of the trailing mean by different routes is stronger evidence than either alone.
- **Method note I want on the record.** The agent replicated cells 1/3/5/17/18/21 outside the notebook first and **reproduced the committed outputs exactly** (0.4887 / 0.5877 / 0.5536) before adding anything — so the new numbers come from a pipeline proven faithful, not a re-implementation that might differ. Then it executed the new cell as written in the notebook to confirm every figure in the Finding matches. **I have not run the notebook myself yet;** the three new cells carry no outputs.
- **No new dependency** — scikit-learn was already declared in `environment.yml`.
- **What this changed:** `notebook/08_human_cause.ipynb` (+3 cells: framing, `SimplexRidge` + alpha sweep + 5-rung ladder + floor-similarity control, Finding); `CLAUDE.md` — the human-branch "closed, not open" section now records the family test and scopes the remaining untried rung to a booster hyperparameter search; slide 5 SAY ("the model" → "gradient boosting") and five WATCH lines, including one noting the figure shows three bars while the ladder is now five rungs.

### Entry 7.15
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Having established that ridge beats the booster and still loses to the floor (Entry 7.14), the finding lived only in a notebook and a WATCH line. I decided to put it on the slide: **I want the audience to see that we did more diligence.**
- **The recommendation I took, and the one I declined.** The agent proposed adding one bar (ridge-with-history, 52%) and leaving ridge-on-coarse off, because at 35.67% it duplicates the gradient-boosting coarse bar at 35.66% — two identical-length bars both reading "36%" is reading time spent discovering they say the same thing. I agreed. The full five-rung table stays in notebook 08, which is the complete record; the figure is the argument, not the archive.
- **Why the fourth bar earns its place.** Three bars all labelled "gradient boosting" is what made "a learned model made it worse" read as a claim about learned models generally. Four bars across two families makes the headline literally true of what is plotted. And the *near-miss* is more persuasive than the losses: a bar stopping 1.9 points short of the floor rule argues for a ceiling in a way a bar 18 points short cannot.
- **A real bug surfaced that only appeared in the figure path.** `human_rung_scores` fits on the training rows but predicts the **whole** frame — fine for `HistGradientBoosting`, which accepts NaN natively, and fatal for ridge, which does not. Rows outside the usable mask carry NaN fingerprints. The fix is to predict on the scored rows only and scatter back, which is also just correct: nothing outside `te` is ever scored. Worth recording because the notebook path never had this problem — it masks first — so the two code paths diverged in a way only exercised by adding a NaN-intolerant learner.
- **The cross-check I now insist on is in the notebook cell itself.** `human_rung_scores` re-fits everything independently of notebook 08, so the two can silently drift. The cell asserts all four rungs against 08's published numbers and fails loudly on a mismatch. All four match to four decimals — 0.4887/0.5405, 0.5877/0.3566, 0.5536/0.4748, 0.5366/0.5215 — which is two independently written code paths agreeing, not one path repeated.
- **The label mismatch from Entry 7.13 is now fixed properly.** The floor bar reads "the region's own **seasonal** history," matching the tiles. I had been offered the cheap fix (say "seasonal" aloud on both slides) and preferred the figure carry it, since the regeneration was happening anyway.
- **What it costs.** Slide 5 goes 0:30 → 0:40 (97 words). The SAY deliberately does **not** say "ridge" — it says "a second kind of model," because the bar is labelled and the word buys nothing spoken to an executive audience. Deck total is **10:50** against ~10:00. I am carrying that until a measured run rather than trimming on estimates.
- **The timing table had drifted and is now derived, not maintained.** It still described a three-slide split from before slides 1, 3 and 4 moved, and summed to 10:10 when the per-slide TIME lines summed to 10:50. Rebuilt from the actual TIME lines with a note to re-derive rather than hand-edit.
- **What this changed:** `src/w6_visuals.py` (`human_rung_scores` gains `include_ridge`, the predict-on-test-rows fix, the "seasonal" relabel); `img/w6_human_ladder.png` regenerated at four bars; `notebook/16_w7_visuals.ipynb` (+3 cells and a rewritten intro — it said "one revision so far"); slide 5 SAY/EVIDENCE/WATCH and the timing table; `CLAUDE.md` ("two learned rungs" → "three learned rungs across two model families"). **The new notebook cells are unexecuted**, though the code was run outside the notebook to produce the committed figure.

### Entry 7.18
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Reading back through the script I noticed the notes kept referring to things we had removed or changed — "it moved here when the shuffled-control slide was cut," "which is the W7 addition," "it previously read." I told the agent to strip them: **no one but us knows about the changes we've been through.**

And slide 13's "the fourth time the project has met the same lesson: W4's pooled climate null, slide 10's places-not-years, slide 6's grain drop" stays, because that is a genuine intellectual thread I would cite out loud, not a record of editing.

### Entry 7.19
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Writing slide 7's SAY (the acres-concentration curve), and mid-draft I asked a question I should have asked much earlier: **by what method do we determine how much burn a hex holds?**
- **The answer, which I now have in the notes rather than in my head:** two rules chosen per fire. A fire with an MTBS perimeter has its acres split across the hexes the perimeter covers, weighted by intersected area. A point-only fire puts all its acres on the hex containing the ignition. Perimeter fires are 0.6% of records and 81.6% of acres; point-only fires average 14 acres against a 62,494-acre hex. Per-fire weights sum to 1, so the hex panel reconciles exactly to the ecoregion totals — a redistribution, never a restatement.
- **Why I wanted it written down.** "How do you know how much burned in that cell?" is an obvious question on a slide whose entire claim is about which cells hold the acres, and I did not want to be reconstructing a two-rule hybrid from memory in front of a pod.
- **The agent volunteered the limitation without being asked**, which is the part I want on the record: the point rule breaks in the tail — 2,710 point fires exceed 1,000 acres and carry 8.9% of all acres, each landing entirely on one cell, and 23 rows put more than a full hex on a single cell. So some of this curve's sharpness at the very tip is the attribution rule rather than fire behavior. That is now a second WATCH line with the honest bound attached: the claim is about shape across the whole distribution and slide 8's statistics are rank-based, so it does not move the argument.
- **A claim I asked for was measured and came back wrong.** I had the agent write a Q&A line saying natural fire concentrates more sharply than human. Checking `hex_acres_panel.parquet` directly: human's worst 1% of burning cells hold **71%** of human acres against natural's **55%**, but human's worst 10% hold **93.5%** against natural's **98.2%**. The branches **cross over** — human is more top-heavy at the very tip, natural across the top decile. The note now says so and explicitly forbids answering "more" or "less." A plausible-sounding comparison I would have asserted confidently is exactly the kind of thing a pod finds.
- **On the SAY itself:** first draft ran 111 words (~44s) against a 30-second slot, mostly narrating a figure that is already fully annotated — both headline numbers and the even-spread diagonal are printed on the plot. Cut to 88 (~35s) by dropping the axis tour and keeping the shape. I also had it state the fact from the flat end ("ninety percent of cells hold two percent") rather than the printed end, because the flat stretch is what the eye is on while I say it — and a WATCH line now warns not to say both, since they sound like two findings when they are one.
- **The closing line is the reason the slide exists:** *"a forecast is only as good as it is on the right-hand edge. Everywhere else, being right is cheap."* It converts a distribution into the standard slide 8 gets judged against. Flagged as the sentence to protect if the slide is trimmed.
- **Timing: 10:20 → 10:25.** I set slide 7's budget to 0:35 rather than leave a 0:30 label over a 35-second block, and the timing table now lists it explicitly.
- **What this changed:** slide 7 SAY, three EVIDENCE lines and six WATCH lines in `final_script.md`; the timing table.

### Entry 7.20
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Having got the full acre-attribution method into slide 7's notes (Entry 7.19), I asked for the **10-second version for the audience.** The notes were correct and unspeakable — a two-rule hybrid with an MTBS foreign key in it is not something I can deliver in a Q&A gap.
- **What I kept:** *"The record stores a fire as a dot but a size in acres. So where a fire has a mapped perimeter — and that's most of the burned area — we spread its acres across the cells it actually covered, instead of piling them all on the dot."* Eleven seconds. It names the **problem** before the method, which is what makes an executive see why it matters, and it closes off the sharper follow-up — *did you just assign every fire to one cell?* — rather than inviting it.
- **The agent offered a shorter one and I did not take it.** "Mapped fire perimeters, split across the cells they cover" is six seconds but skips the dot-versus-area mismatch, which is the whole reason the work exists. Saving five seconds by removing the interesting part is a bad trade in the one format where the audience has actually asked to hear more.
- **The advice I most valued was about what NOT to lead with.** My instinct was to open with "0.6% of fires but 81.6% of acres" because it is the striking number. The agent's argument: unexplained, it sounds like a **coverage gap** — the listener hears "you only used 0.6% of your data." It only lands after they know why a tiny fraction of fires carries most of the area. It is now marked as the answer to the *second* question, not the first.
- **The notes now escalate by depth of challenge:** ten-second version → the two rules with the key figures → the tail defect and its bound. That is the shape I want for every dense Q&A answer in this deck, and I will apply it to the others.

This is the one figure in the deck whose honest reading is **worse than the eye's**, everywhere else the risk runs the other way. That is not sloppiness — here their error is a records artifact (25.3% of rows sit at exactly 0.1 acres), while there the quantity is acres, which are real however coarsely recorded. The exclusion follows what is being measured. Now a WATCH line.

- **The next boundary at risk is 8 → 9**, which flips back to starts while still plotting a skill measure, so it can be heard as continuing slide 8's error story. Slide 9's SAY is unwritten; its WATCH now carries the warning **before** the prose gets drafted, which is the right order.
- **What this changed:** slide 7 SAY and a WATCH line; a "name the target whenever it changes" section in the header with the corrected mapping; a pre-emptive WATCH line on slide 9. Deck holds at 10:40.

### Entry 7.25
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Having fixed three separate target-ambiguity defects in a row, I stepped back and asked the broader question: is the **ping-ponging between fire starts and acres burned** going to confuse the audience, and can sequencing fix it — or am I overthinking it?
- **The agent said I was not overthinking it, then showed why most of it cannot be sequenced away.** Slides 6–15 run starts → acres → starts → acres → starts: four flips in nine slides. But the order is not arbitrary. The deck runs a **repair loop** — 6 wins on starts, 7–8 lose on acres, 9–12 attempt the repair, 13 diagnoses the problem as grain, 14–15 return to starts. Grouping all the starts slides together would destroy that: slides 9–12 only make sense *as a response* to 7–8's failure. Reordered, "here is what we did about the problem you just saw" becomes "here are some things we tried."
- **One movable piece was identified, and I find the argument convincing.** Slide 9 is not part of the repair loop — it characterizes *when* the ignition surface works, which belongs beside slide 6. Moving it would make 8 → 9 → 10 a continuous acres-failure-then-repair run and drop the deck from four flips to three, at no structural cost.
- **I decided not to do it yet.** Reordering slides is exactly the kind of change that feels obviously right on paper and turns out to break a transition I had not noticed — I have already been through one resequencing this week. The right time to judge whether the flips actually cost the audience anything is **after a measured run**, when I will know whether they tracked it. Deferred to a later sanity check, and written into the script header so it is not lost.
- **The reframe I am taking away.** The audience will not track "which quantity is this" across nine slides regardless of what I do. What they will track is the story — *we can predict where, we can't predict how much, so target where.* That is the thing to protect. Slides 13 and 17 are where they reassemble it, which raises the stakes on those two specifically.
- **What this changed:** an "open question, deferred" note in `final_script.md`'s header recording the analysis and the proposed swap. No slides moved.

### Entry 7.26
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** I ran notebooks 06, 08 and 16. Everything the agent had written into `CLAUDE.md`, the script and the deck over the past several days was a **dry-run prediction** — code executed outside the notebooks, never inside them. This was the first check of whether any of it was real.
- **It all held.** Every figure quoted in the script matches the executed output: the confidence signal at **+0.484 / +0.577** with **33 / 35 SD** shuffled controls, the quartile splits **83.1% → 61.9%** and **72.5% → 39.4%**, the within-region checks at **74/93** and **80/92**, Klamath's four seasons, the **36 of 986** counterexample, the four-rung ladder at **54.1 / 52.2 / 47.5 / 35.7**, and the flat alpha sweep identical to six decimals. Nothing needed correcting.
- **The check I am most glad we built is the one inside notebook 16.** Its ladder cell asserts all four rungs against notebook 08's published numbers and would have failed loudly on drift. It printed **"cross-check vs 08_human_cause.ipynb: all four rungs match"** — two independently written code paths agreeing on live data, not one path repeated.
- **One apparent discrepancy that turned out not to be.** The script says slide 3's tiles are on **3,949** held-out cells; notebook 06 prints **3,941** for the confidence analysis. Different populations, both right: the confidence work additionally requires ≥2 prior same-season observations to compute a dispersion, so it loses 8 cells. Worth noting because it is exactly the kind of thing that looks like an error under questioning and is not.
- **One real defect, cosmetic but worth fixing:** a `RuntimeWarning: Mean of empty slice` printed twice in cell 26. It is benign — `np.nanmean` on cells with fewer than two prior seasons, which are deliberately left unscored and masked out immediately after. But it reads as a bug in a notebook I am submitting. The existing `np.errstate(invalid="ignore")` never suppressed it because this is a **Python warning, not a floating-point flag** — a distinction I would not have known to look for. Now wrapped in `warnings.catch_warnings()` with a comment explaining why the NaNs are intended.
- **What this changed:** committed the executed outputs for all three notebooks; `notebook/06_analysis.ipynb` cell 26 gains the correct warning suppression. **The notebook will need one more run** to clear the stale warning from its stored output — a trivial one, but the cell is no longer byte-identical to what produced its output.

Within-hex: ..."* — my own analysis vocabulary, delivered cold. It never said what the figure showed, and it never said this was a **repair attempt**, which the WATCH block had been insisting on the whole time.
- **The rewrite leads with the repair framing:** *"So we tried to fix it — back to starts, adding what everyone expects to matter."* Without that, slide 10 reads as an unrelated experiment instead of as the answer to the failure slide 8 just showed. It also puts "back to starts" in the first sentence, since this is the third target flip.
- **What I kept from the original, because it is the actual mechanism:** the raw-versus-own-normal contrast. *"Do greener cells get more fires than browner ones? Yes. But when a cell is greener than its own normal, does it get more fires than its own normal? Barely."* That is what "identifies places, not years" means, and it is the difference between a null that sounds like a failure and one that sounds like a finding.
- **A slip I introduced and caught in the same pass.** My first rewrite asked whether a greener cell "burns more than its own normal" — acres language on a starts slide, which is precisely the confusion I have spent three sessions removing. Fixed to "gets more fires." Worth recording because I made the error immediately after building the rule against it.
- **Two numbers moved from prose into the notes as the mechanism's evidence:** drought's correlation with ignitions goes **-0.137 raw to -0.073** within-hex, NDVI **+0.228 to +0.098**. Both roughly halve. That is the numerical form of "strip out which cell you are looking at and most of the signal goes with it."
- **A vocabulary inconsistency I decided to live with.** This figure labels the branch **"lightning fire"**; slide 8's headline says **"natural."** Both are in the deck. Rather than regenerate figures, the note says they are the same class and to prefer "natural" when speaking, since that matches slides 3, 7, 8 and 17. Cheap to say, expensive to re-render.
- **Timing is now the live problem: 10:55 against ~10:00.** Ten of eighteen slides are over the 30-second budget, and every one of them earned it for a stated reason. I am not trimming on estimates — the note in the timing table now says the trim comes off a measured run. But I should expect to lose a slide or compress the 11–12 pair.
- **What this changed:** slide 10 SAY rewritten, five WATCH lines added, budget 0:30 → 0:40; the timing table.

### Entry 7.28
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Slides 11 and 12 — the matched pair where the covariate work wins and then turns out not to matter. Written together because neither makes sense alone.
- **Slide 11's constraint is that it must not be oversold**, since slide 12 takes it away thirty seconds later. The SAY states the gain, states that it holds up, and hands off: *"So the data does know something about how much burns. The question is whether it knows it anywhere useful."* Banking the result here would make slide 12 feel like a retraction rather than a completion.
- **A trap the figure's own docstring saved me from.** `plot_acres_ladder` annotates the **step** (+0.049) rather than the **level** (0.3075) deliberately, because the level invites comparison with slide 10's ~0.42-0.48 — a different population and a different target. My SAY says "a fifth better than history on its own," which is a within-slide relative and safe. A WATCH line now forbids quoting the level and gives the answer to "so it's worse than the ignition model?" — those numbers are not on the same scale.
- **The conjunction is the finding, not the size of the gain.** Drought alone -0.008, fuel alone +0.001, both +0.049. A viewer reading left to right will credit the last thing added rather than the combination, so the SAY says "you need both, and only both," and the physical reason is one clause: wet heavy fuel will not carry fire, dry bare ground has nothing to burn.
- **Slide 12 is the third slide landing the eye on the same right edge** — 7, 8 and 12 all drawn on the same axis in the same direction, which is the payoff of that decision. The SAY's core is *"we improved the forecast exactly where it doesn't matter."*
- **Something on the figure that is drawn but not labelled, and I want it said aloud:** the left end gets *worse*. Deciles 1-5 deteriorate, decile 1 nearly doubling from 18.8x to 35.9x — that is what the pink lens is. It rules out "well, it helps a bit everywhere," which is the most natural way for a listener to soften the finding.
- **The distinction I most want to keep:** the covariate model did not *fail*. It produced a verified gain at 26.6 SD holding across five split years. It failed to be **useful**. That is a different and more interesting claim, and saying "failed" would retract slide 11 and make the deck sound defeatist rather than diagnostic. WATCH line added.
- **Timing is now 11:15 against ~10:00, with three SAY blocks still unwritten.** I have decided the trim is a separate content-editing pass once everything is drafted — trimming prose that does not exist yet is guesswork. Slides 11-12 stay the standing compression candidate, worth about 40 seconds.
- **What this changed:** slide 11 and 12 SAY blocks, nine WATCH lines between them, both budgets 0:30 → 0:40; the timing table.

### Entry 7.29
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Reaching slide 14, I hit the headline warning that had been sitting there since W6 — *"big fires usually burn in the hex where they started"* — and rather than pick a replacement I asked whether slides 14 and 15 were earning their place at all, given the recommendations had already landed.
- **The agent's answer separated them, and I agree with the split.** Slide 14 (the ignition gate) was a **justification** slide — it argued that ignition is worth targeting. Slide 15 (one ignition is enough) is an **instruction** slide: rank cells by *whether* they ignite, not how often. The first proves something an audience will grant anyway; the second is counterintuitive and operational.
- **The argument that decided it for me:** my title slide and my closing slide both say "target where they start," and slide 15 is the **only place in the deck that says how**. It also holds a number that would otherwise be an ambush — ignition count ranks burned area *worse* than burn history does, +0.253 against +0.357. Without the binary framing, "target where they start" sounds naive to anyone who finds that.
- **So: dropped 14, kept 15, and folded 14's job into one opening sentence.** The gate now arrives as *"a cell that ignites at all is about twenty times more likely to produce a thousand-acre fire — that's the case for going after starts"*, then the slide spends its remaining time on the part that changes what a planner does.
- **This also disposes of the headline problem without my having to solve it.** The claim that worried me was spatial containment, which the figure never measured and which is partly true by construction (point-only fires are assigned to their ignition hex by the attribution rule). Cutting the slide removes the claim entirely.
- **On precision: the notes now say to say "about twenty times," not 22.8x.** Two decimal places on a screening statistic is false comfort, and the honest companion fact is that 93% of igniting hex-seasons still produce nothing large.
- **Mechanics.** Deck 18 → 17 slides, script sections renumbered 15-17 → 14-16, **14 cross-references** followed, and the header's target-sequence map updated (the deck now ends "...acres · acres — starts — cause" rather than "starts · starts"). Verified programmatically: 17 deck slides, 17 script sections, all headlines matching. Pre-cut deck backed up to the scratchpad.
- **Timing: 11:15 → 10:55**, with one SAY still unwritten. Slides 11-12 remain the standing compression candidate at ~40 seconds.
- **What this changed:** slide 14 removed from deck and script; slide 14 (was 15) gains a SAY, the gate figures as evidence, and three WATCH lines; header, timing table and 14 cross-references.

### Entry 7.30
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Slide 15, the unknown-cause triage — the last unwritten SAY. **All seventeen are now drafted.**

The SAY says "the quarter of fires I mentioned at the start" out loud to close that loop — without the callback, slide 0 sounds like a caveat and this sounds like a change of subject. It also carries both denominators in one sentence (a quarter by count, nearly a fifth by acres), which is the pairing I nearly got wrong back in Entry 7.11.

Five slides kept a stale budget after their SAY was written: **slide 6 was labelled 0:30 against 66 seconds of prose**, slide 3 was 0:55 against 64 seconds. Each individual slide's budget had been updated when I wrote it — but slides written *before* I started that habit never got revisited, and the running total inherited the error.

The audit also shows where the words are — slides **6** (164 words) and **3** (160) are the two heaviest, and both are load-bearing (the only grain change; the only slide naming the Tier-1 classes). Slide 4 is next at 124. Those three are a quarter of the deck's words.
- **I want the honest number in the file**, so the timing section now states plainly that it read 11:00 for the wrong reason. A trim planned against a fake baseline would have left me two minutes over on the day.
- **What this changed:** slide 15 SAY and five WATCH lines; every TIME line recomputed from measured prose; the Timing section rewritten as a derived table with the structural cut candidates ranked by least damage.

### Entry 7.31
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** The content pass for length. Target was about 400 words out of 1,746.
- **Line-level editing got 216 words** — 12% — without losing a claim. The pattern that produced most of it: **the SAY was narrating figures that are already annotated.** Slide 12 re-described an axis the audience had seen twice; slide 6 explained the grain change in two paragraphs where one does it; slide 3 closed with a comparison the three tiles make visually. Slide 6 went 164 → 108 words, slide 3 went 160 → 119, and neither lost anything I would defend.
- **The rule I would apply from the start next time:** if the figure prints it, do not say it. Say what the figure cannot — why it matters, what it rules out, what to do about it.
- **Where I stopped, and why.** The agent got to 1,530 and said the remaining ~180 had to come structurally, offering three options: compress slides 11–12, cut slide 13, or both. **I declined all of them** and chose to stop. My pace is an unmeasured variable — at 170 wpm the deck runs 9:00 and needs nothing; at 130 it runs 11:46 and needs a real cut. Removing load-bearing slides against an estimate is how you end up having deleted the wrong thing.
- **The measurement that settles it costs two minutes:** record slide 3 alone, 119 words, the densest in the deck. At 48 seconds I am at 150 wpm and fine. At 55 I am at 130 and need another ~170 words out. That is now written into the timing section in place of the guesswork.
- **The timing table is fully derived now** and reports the pace sensitivity as a three-row table rather than a single assumed number. It also records that per-slide rounding adds about a minute of slack across seventeen slides, so the rounded total (11:15) and the raw prose (10:12 at 150 wpm) differ for a legitimate reason — which is the kind of gap that looked like an error the last two times.
- **I have told the agent I will give it manual cut targets** rather than have it choose. It has done the mechanical work well, but which content earns its place in front of my pod is my call, and the remaining candidates are all things I would have to defend live.
- **What this changed:** eleven SAY blocks tightened, 1,746 → 1,530 words; every TIME line and the whole Timing section regenerated from measured prose.

### Entry 7.32

Slide 7 went 98 → 32 for the same reason. Slide 4 lost its entire score paragraph: the tiles show 9, 16 and 54 and the confidence split underneath.

### Entry 7.33

The agent flagged it and offered to restore it. I chose to end on the limitation instead: the bookend still exists **visually** — the line is on screen while I say something else — and ending a talk to executives on what the product cannot do is a stronger finish than repeating a slogan they can read.

- **Deck is 1,314 words**, up 67 from the trough because this slide went from 10 words to 77 — a case where the length pass had cut past the point of usefulness and the right move was to spend words back. Runs **9:20 at 150 wpm** with transitions, **10:41 at 130**.

### Entry 7.34

"Stop targeting how big fires get / target where they start" was an instruction; this names the thing I am actually handing over.
- **The bookend is now a rhyme rather than a repetition**, which I prefer. The old version said the identical sentence at both ends; this opens on "rank the ground, not the fire" and closes on "trust the order, not the number." Same shape, different content, and the second one is the caveat the first one earns.
- **"Rank" is also the more honest verb.** "Target" edges toward promising an outcome, and nothing in this project measures what a treatment achieves. The WATCH line about it being a targeting claim and not an efficacy one now says so explicitly.
- **The SAY gained a sentence rather than losing one:** *"Where fires start is predictable. How big they get, before the season, is not."* That split is the entire deck — both nulls and both wins hang off it — and slide 0 was previously stating a recommendation without the finding that justifies it.

### Entry 7.38
- **Date:** 2026-08-17

### Entry 7.39
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** Cleaning up a PowerPoint lock file (`~$MSDS696_W7_Deck.pptx`) that had been committed to the repo.
- **It turned out to explain the deck reversion.** PowerPoint writes `~$Name.pptx` beside an open deck and deletes it on close, so a *committed* lock file means the deck was open and being written to while the repo was being changed underneath it. That is the mechanism behind Entry 7.36's discovery that the deck had silently drifted back to a pre-edit state with all eighteen notes panes stale — not a mystery, just two writers.
- **Its deletion is also the confirmation I needed for Phase 0.** The lock file is gone from disk, which means the deck is closed, which means the committed version is the real one. Both Phase 0 items are now checked off with that evidence rather than an assumption.
- **Untracked it and added a gitignore rule** for `~$*.pptx`, `.docx` and `.xlsx`, with a why-comment matching the file's existing convention. Verified with a probe file that the rule actually matches — the pattern has a `$` in it and I wanted to see it work rather than assume shell quoting had behaved.
- **The plan now says to re-run the git-status check at the start of any session that edits the deck**, not just once. A single confirmation does not protect against the same collision happening again next week.
- **What this changed:** lock file untracked; `.gitignore` gains Office lock patterns; Phase 0 fully checked off with the reasoning recorded.

- **Why the closer needed its own slide.** "Rank the ground, not the fire" was printed beneath three product rows, competing with them for the same glance and landing as neither. A closing line has to be the only thing on screen or it is just a caption. The figure now does one job — show the three products — and slide 18 does the other.
- **Built as PowerPoint text, not as an image**, cloned from the title slide so the type treatment matches exactly. Two lines at 48pt, the finding beneath at 20pt as context rather than a third claim.
- **This is the one slide in the deck where reading it aloud is correct.** Everywhere else that is duplication and I have been cutting it all week; here the repetition *is* the delivery — the audience should hear and see the same words at the same moment, and the silence afterward is the point.
- **The bookend is now exact:** the title slide and the final slide print the same first line, with the boundary added at the end. Opens on the instruction, closes on the instruction plus its limit.
- **On the notes: I asked for SAY only, nothing else.** The panes had been carrying SAY + EVIDENCE + WATCH, which is 2,000–6,000 characters a slide. That is the right density for *preparing*, and exactly wrong for presenting — under a clock I need the line I am about to say, not a wall of caveats to scan past. The WATCH blocks stay in `final_script.md`, which is what I study from; the deck is what I deliver from. **Different artifacts, different jobs.**
- **Deck is 19 slides, 1,418 words, 10:05 at 150 wpm** with transitions. The closer costs 15 seconds and buys the ending.
- **What this changed:** `plot_recommendation` loses its two closing lines and re-centres its rows; `img/w6_recommendation.png` regenerated and re-embedded; new slide 18 in deck and script; all 19 notes panes reduced to SAY; header and timing table.

### Entry 7.43
- **Date:** 2026-08-17
- **What was going on:** I had been editing speaker notes directly in PowerPoint and asked for those changes to be pulled back into `final_script.md`. This is the reverse of every sync so far — the deck was the source and the script had to catch up.
- **The diff found three slides**, which matched my memory, and that is the reassuring part: with the notes reduced to SAY only, a mechanical comparison against the script is now exact rather than approximate. Had the panes still carried EVIDENCE and WATCH, the noise would have buried the actual edits.
- **Two were wording**, both about precision I wanted while speaking: "by acres *burned*" on the title slide, and on slide 2 "not state borders" plus "of natural causes — lightning, mostly" instead of "lightning-caused". The second matters because natural is the class name and lightning is the mechanism; conflating them is the kind of thing a fire scientist in the room would notice.
- **The third was a real cut.** Slide 3 went from 102 words to 53. I dropped the three percentages — they are printed on the tiles — and the acres-versus-fire-counts warning, which I had previously called the single most confusable pair of numbers in the deck (Entry 7.11).
- **The agent flagged the consequence rather than quietly accepting it**, which is what I want: that warning existed because I was *saying* the shares aloud. Now I do not say them, so nothing I say can spring the trap. It has become purely a Q&A matter, and the WATCH block already carries the full answer both ways. The agent rewrote one WATCH line to say exactly that instead of leaving a note that presumed a sentence I no longer speak.
- **Deck is 1,379 words, 9:49 at 150 wpm.** My own edits took another 39 words out.
- **What this changed:** four SAY edits across slides 0, 2 and 3 ported into `final_script.md`; one WATCH line rescoped; timing table regenerated. The deck was not touched — it was the source this time.

### Entry 7.44
- **Date:** 2026-08-18
- **What was going on:** Writing the W7 status report — Phase 5 of the remediation plan, and the last of the three graded submissions I can complete without the pod.
- **The template rule did its job.** `CLAUDE.md` says the report template is the most recent status report, so W6's structure was copied forward rather than reinvented: title, summary, research questions, milestones, last week's to-do with dispositions, this week's progress, next week's to-do, resources. The four elements the assignment names — near-final deck, repo link, decision log, draft LLM reflection — slot into that structure instead of replacing it.
- **The decision log is a summary, not a re-listing, and that was the whole editorial problem.** Entries 7.1–7.43 are 43 contemporaneous records; the report needs the load-bearing ones. I kept the ones that changed the deliverable — the retitle, the two cuts, the added reference slide and closer, the three ambiguity defects, the 1,746 → 1,379 word cut — and organized the deck changes as a table of *what changed and why* rather than a chronology, since chronology is a fact about the project and the reader does not care in what order things were tried. Same principle that reordered the storyboard in W6.
- **Two things got promoted to their own sections** because they are results rather than deck edits: per-cell confidence (7.10, 7.12) and the ridge/model-family closure (7.14, 7.15). Those are the only substantive analytical additions in W7 and burying them inside a decision log would have made the week read as pure production work.
- **The "last week's To Do" section is where I made myself be honest.** Three of six items are not done. Two of those are correctly deferred with a stated reason (the point-attribution rebuild would invalidate every acres figure in the week the deck had to ship; same-day escape is a different data requirement), one is superseded, and one — the recording — is simply outstanding and named as such. A status report that only lists what got done is not a status report.
- **The LLM reflection is drafted from the log with entry numbers attached**, in four parts: where the agent was wrong and I overrode it, where it caught things I would have shipped, the division of labour, and the verification habit. I wanted each claim traceable to a contemporaneous entry rather than reconstructed from memory at the end of term — which is the reason for logging as we go in the first place.
- **What this changed:** `coursework/W7/MSDS696_W7_Status_Report.md` created; Phase 5 items checked off in `REMEDIATION_PLAN.md`.

### Entry 7.45
- **Date:** 2026-08-19
- **What was going on:** I asked for a dead-code audit, then acted on the first finding: four modules in `src/` were reachable only from the command line, and I want all code traceable to a notebook.
- **The audit found the surface was small**, which was the useful part — 19 modules and only ~280 lines genuinely dead, nearly all of it in `w5_visuals` (`plot_national`, `animate_national`) plus a handful of unused constants and five unused imports. The four CLI-only modules — `burn_history`, `hex_ignitions`, `hex_climate`, `hex_ndvi` — were *not* dead; they have `__main__` blocks and their parquets feed notebooks 12–15. But nothing on the notebook side named them, so the reasoning behind those columns was invisible from where a reader actually reads.
- **My rule is legibility, not execution.** I don't need these notebooks run — I need the project readable. `notebook/17_covariate_builders.ipynb` is written to be read: per module, the self-check that guards its leakage rule, then a verification of the shipped artifact against the contract the module promises. The `build()` calls are present but guarded behind `REBUILD = False`, because two of them are multi-hour network fetches and the other two overwrite inputs to notebooks 12–15. Re-running them has to be a deliberate act, not a side effect of opening a notebook.
- **The dry-run caught a real error in the agent's own assertion**, and this is the part worth recording. It had asserted `burned_frac_lag{w} <= 1.0` on the reasoning that `burned_frac` is clamped to 1.0 — which is true, but the clamp is applied *per season*, and the lagged column is a *sum over a w-season window*. A hex that burns twice inside the window legitimately exceeds 1.0; 902 cells do at lag20. The fix was to assert the window bound `w` instead, and to write the distinction into the markdown rather than silently deleting the bad check. A verification notebook that ships a false invariant is worse than no notebook.
- **The agent verified rather than trusted**, which is what I want when nothing will be executed. It dry-ran all 18 cells in a scratch process against the real artifacts, confirmed the four self-checks pass and that the NDVI regional ordering reproduces the CLAUDE.md figures (Klamath 0.649, Idaho Batholith 0.268), then left the committed file unexecuted. That is the right split: prove it works, ship it clean.
- **What I have not done yet:** the actual deletions. The audit's Tier 1 list is real but I want to decide case by case — `render_cause_density` and `plot_volatility_map` both carry docstrings claiming deliberate retention, and that claim deserves a judgment rather than a blanket sweep.
- **What this changed:** `notebook/17_covariate_builders.ipynb` created (41 cells, 18 code). No `src/` changes; the audit was report-only.

### Entry 7.46
- **Date:** 2026-08-19
- **What was going on:** Acting on the dead-code audit — steps 1 through 3 of the four the agent proposed. I explicitly held back step 4 (`render_cause_density`, `plot_volatility_map`), because both carry docstrings claiming deliberate retention and that is a judgment I want to make after the defense, not during a cleanup sweep.
- **The agent asked before touching anything and I told it to pause**, which is the behavior I want on deletions. It had bundled the judgment calls into the same question as the mechanical work; splitting them was the right correction, and nothing was applied until I said so.
- **What came out:** five unused imports, two dead constants (`LADDER_CACHE`, `SEQ_BLUE`), and the two `w5_visuals` national plotters — 254 lines, the single biggest block. `plot_national` and `animate_national` were both real work at the time; `animate_national` is the 117-frame timelapse with the fixed color scale and the fade trail. Neither made the deck, and neither has been called since W5. Git has them if I want them back.
- **The verification caught something a blind sweep would have broken.** After cutting the two functions, the agent scanned for constants orphaned by the cut and found `TEXT_MUTED` with one remaining reference — its own definition. Deleting it would have been the obvious next move and would have broken `w6_visuals`, which imports it from `w5_visuals` and uses it in **43 places**. The within-file scan was the wrong scope for a cross-module import. It checked all six imported names before and after and left the constant alone.
- **Tests were the proof, not the assumption.** Baseline captured first (133 passed, 13 deselected), then the same after. I also had it run the full suite with the filter off — 146 passed, including the metric pins that re-derive headline numbers and the artifact fingerprints. Nothing moved.
- **What is left:** four dead symbols. `render_cause_density` plus three `hex_burn` resolution constants (`HEX_RES_COARSE`, `HEX_RES_FINE`, `MTBS_CRS`) that arguably document the resolution ladder rather than doing work. Zero unused imports remain across `src/` and `tests/`.
- **Flagged, then resolved — not a defect.** Mid-session `coursework/W7/MSDS696_W7_Deck.pptx` showed 2.0 MB → 278 MB in `git diff --stat`, and I said I did not want to commit a 278 MB pptx. The cause was the embedded video from my recording the presentation; once that was out, the deck went back to 2.1 MB and committed at that size (`2079206 → 2173214` bytes). Recorded videos are gitignored as of `d1f798e`. Nothing to act on.
- **What this changed:** `src/w5_visuals.py` 732 → 478 lines; one line each from `hex_burn.py`, `hex_climate.py` (two), `hex_panel.py`, `trailing.py`, `tests/test_cleaning.py`. No behavior change — every deleted symbol had zero call sites.

### Entry 7.47
- **Date:** 2026-08-20
- **What was going on:** Building the Week 7 executive-challenge Q&A. I wanted three questions an executive would actually ask, in my stakeholder's voice — a fire-agency planner holding a fixed pre-season budget — plus written answers for the discussion post. Output is `coursework/W7/MSDS696_W7_Executive_Challenge.md`.
- **The agent's first framing was wrong for this document and I said so.** It proposed each question with a "why it lands" analysis in its own commentary voice. That reads as an agent explaining my project back to me. I asked for a plain rationale stating what in the deck provokes the question, and then had to ask twice more — the rewrite still carried color ("this is the question a budget-holder came to ask," "anyone who has worked with agency records will ask"). Every rationale now opens by naming the slide that raises the question and stops there.
- **I discarded its third question.** It had proposed the slide 14 single-ignition objection — that ranking by ignition would miss the half of large fires whose cell ignited once. It is a sharp question but the gate-versus-dial answer is already written into the script's WATCH, so it tests nothing. I took its alternative on the missing-cause quarter instead, which is the disclosure I make voluntarily on slide 0 and therefore the one I am actually inviting.
- **The verification is the part worth recording.** The draft answer said missingness concentrates in human-dominated regions "not in the lightning-driven West," sourced from `CLAUDE.md`. I asked the agent to check it, then to re-run it from scratch rather than reading the notebook. Recomputing from `data/fires_clean.parquet` gave **Pearson −0.660, Spearman −0.611** across 105 ecoregions against the notebook's −0.636 / −0.558 — same direction, same significance, slightly stronger. Tier-1 shares reproduced exactly (58.8 / 22.6 / 18.5, 179.4M acres). The gap is likely the 1,510 null-region rows. "About −0.64" survives either run, so nothing in the deck changes.
- **The geography claim did not survive, and that is the real finding.** The well-attributed end is **Alaska specifically**, not the West — nine of the twelve cleanest regions above 100k acres are Alaskan, at 88–100% Natural. Meanwhile **Coast Range sits in the ten worst at 53% missing**, and Pacific Coastal Mountains at 51%. So parts of the West are among the *worst*-attributed and the sentence was wrong on both sides. Both instances are now rewritten to state the mechanism — unattributed acres are not hiding inside the Natural class — rather than locating it on a map. A challenger holding the per-region numbers could have used the old wording against me.
- **What I kept from the agent unchanged:** the necessary-condition framing on the efficacy question (rank the ground supplies the input an efficacy estimate needs; it is not one), and the nulls-as-procurement-finding framing on the first question. Both convert a limitation into something a budget-holder can act on, which is the register the whole Q&A needs.
- **What this changed:** `coursework/W7/MSDS696_W7_Executive_Challenge.md` created. No code or data changes; the correlation re-run was read-only.

Staging the commit, the agent read the actual file and found the claim still live in Challenge 3 — "while the most lightning-dominated regions have almost no missing cause at all" — and carried into the derived `.html` and `.pdf` alongside it. The correction I recorded in 7.47 had been reasoned through but never applied to the document. - **Date:** 2026-08-20
- **What was going on:** I deleted the derived PDF and HTML exports and removed the `archive/` directory, then asked the agent to find every reference to them and plan the cleanup before touching anything.
- **The inventory mattered more than the edits.** Six live dead references across four files, against roughly a dozen hits in the collaboration log and the W4 deliverables that must *not* be touched. - **I stopped it twice before it edited.** It asked how to handle the W7 status report's dead `REMEDIATION_PLAN.md` reference and whether to gitignore derived exports; I answered "do nothing, I'm reviewing your findings" to both, and it held with nothing staged. When I then said proceed, it read those two answers as standing and applied only the other items rather than treating the go-ahead as blanket approval. Left alone deliberately: the W7 status report, `.gitignore`, and every historical reference in the log and the W4 documents.

### Entry 7.50
- **Date:** 2026-08-20
- **What was going on:** Closing the one question I had held back from the 7.49 cleanup — whether `.gitignore` should keep derived exports out going forward.
- **The rule is `*.pdf` and `*.html` with a negation for `literature/pdf/**`.** The distinction it encodes is render versus input: a status report PDF is a print of a tracked `.md` and can go stale against its source, while a source paper is an input nothing in the repo generates. I had the why-comment cite Entry 7.48 directly, since that is the failure the rule exists to prevent — a correction landed in the `.md` and the `.pdf` kept the old wording.
- **I asked for the negation to be verified against a file that does not exist yet.** Checking it against the seven papers already tracked proves nothing: git does not ignore tracked files regardless. The test that matters is a hypothetical new paper dropped into `literature/pdf/`, which the agent confirmed stays visible. It also checked that no currently tracked file is shadowed by the new rule — none is.
- **Timing was deliberate.** I split this from 7.49 rather than letting it ride on "proceed with the plan," because a blanket export rule interacts with the literature corpus and I wanted it decided on its own rather than bundled into a reference cleanup.
- **What this changed:** `.gitignore` only. No files untracked, nothing on disk affected — the sweep in 7.49 had already cleared every derived export, so the rule is purely forward-looking.
